## 📚 Paper List

- **[What Can a VLA Learn from the Agent That Calls It? Task-Level Experience Distillation for Embodied Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16246)**  `arXiv:2611.16246`  `cs.RO` `cs.LG`  
  _Jinze Tao, Yuxiao Han, Kangkang Dong, Yancong Wei, Xiaojun Zhu, Bokui Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Navigation agents use a large language model (LLM) planner to call a vision-language-action (VLA) model. Each episode needs the LLM, adding cost and delay. We distill successful agent experience into the VLA instead of distilling the planner. The VLA then navigates alone at test time. Agent sessions are not VLA demonstrations. They include motions driven by planner tools and sub-instructions, along with detours. We present H2E, a task-level experience distillation method that converts these sessions into VLA training data. Every motion becomes a VLA action under the original task instruction, with the resulting poses as targets. H2E masks the loss on steps that move away from the goal. It labels every visited state within 1 m of the goal as a stop. We then update the VLA with low-rank adaptation. The VLA learns to stop at the goal and find goals the base model never reaches. On all 3,189 REVERIE-CE val-unseen episodes, H2E raises the success rate of an 8B VLA from 35.0% to 44.9%.
  </details>

- **[I See What You Want: Decoding Hierarchical Operator Intent from Visual Teleoperation History](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16419)**  `arXiv:2611.16419`  `cs.RO` `cs.CV`  
  _Xiangyi Jia, Kaixian Qu, Shengzhi Wang, Chenyu Yang, Robert Katzschmann, Marco Hutter_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language models (VLMs) have become a powerful foundation for robotic scene understanding, task planning, and language-conditioned control. A natural question follows: can these models also decode what a human operator intends while teleoperating a robot, without any explicit instruction? We formulate this as hierarchical intent prediction, jointly inferring the ongoing atomic action and the broader mission it serves from a short visual teleoperation history. To study this, we introduce VI AMO (Vision Intention Action and Mission Ongoing), a dataset of 6,600 episodes from eleven robot sources with 13,653 temporally aligned action annotations and 9,702 mission annotations. Large proprietary models struggle with this problem: on held-out episodes, GPT-4o reaches Sentence-BERT similarities of only 0.563 and 0.470 for action and mission, and GPT-5.6 Luna achieves only 0.581 and 0.554. Fine-tuning a VLM on VI AMO yields our Vision-Intention Model (VIM), which raises these scores to 0.825 and 0.821 and still reaches 0.726 and 0.717 on the 35.4% of test actions with both labels absent from training. Finally, as a downstream application, VIM's predicted mission enables a vision-language-action (VLA) policy to take over from the operator mid-task in a real-world environment unseen during VIM fine-tuning, reducing human teleoperation time by 34.6% relative to GPT-4o and 39.8% relative to GPT-5.6 Luna, while cutting intent-prediction latency by roughly 70% on average.
  </details>

- **[TinyAstral: Transferring Self-Evolving Harnesses to Tiny Generalist Manipulation Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41216)**  `arXiv:2610.41216`  `cs.RO` `cs.CV`  
  _Kehong Gong, Xiaoxu Zheng, Guanli Hou, Seunghun Lee, Mengqi Guo, Runjia Tan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Robot agents built on vision-language models depend on both the planner and its harness: the tools, feedback, memory, and recovery logic around it. Can improvements to a large teacher's harness be carried into a compact agent with lower inference compute cost? We present TinyAstral, a two-stage approach. We first freeze an open-weight teacher and improve its harness through recursive self-improvement (RSI): frontier LLM agents read the teacher's trajectories and propose harness edits, and each edit is accepted only if it passes a paired statistical test. We then freeze the final harness, collect teacher trajectories with reasoning and failure recovery, and distill a small student that replaces the teacher in the same harness. On RoboTwin, harness evolution raises the teacher's success from 33.4% to 63.6%; the final 4B student reaches 62.8%. Across four harness versions, distilled 4B students score close to their corresponding teachers, and later versions yield stronger students, showing that harness evolution gains carry into compact models. The harness gains also transfer to other frozen planners without retraining. On identical episodes, the final 4B student outperforms open 4B agents, the open 27B harness-RSI model SenseNova-RoboRSI, and our own teacher model placed in three competitors' harnesses. It stays close to its teacher on all three training benchmarks, and on three benchmarks never used for RSI or training it scores close to a 552B planner running the same harness. On the measured deployment episodes, it uses about one fifth of its teacher's accelerator time. Project page:this https URL
  </details>

- **[JustRUA: A Controlled Study of GPT-6 Robot-Use Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16009)**  `arXiv:2611.16009`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI`  
  _Zefan Wang, Yufan Dang, Xuanwei Shi, Mingqi Gao, Cheng Yin, SikYuen Tam, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  GPT-6 Astra can drive a robot arm directly through a fixed harness, and scores of such robot-use agents on RoboDojo have risen quickly since. Reported systems differ in harness and test subset and often change several designs at once, so it is hard to tell which choices produce the gains. We take these designs apart and test them in one framework, along the harness around the model, the action interface, the reuse of experience and the scene geometry given to the model. A lean harness that keeps the full episode history and stops each motion early goes a long way. Reusing experience changes how the model acts but is not a free lunch, and scene geometry helps most, even when depth is predicted from RGB alone. JustRUA starts from the official L3 harness and keeps only what matters most. Even with no learned policy, self-improvement loop or task-specific tuning, this simple harness raises the score on the RoboDojo simulation benchmark substantially, from 29.7 to 50.3.
  </details>

- **[Maximizing LLM Utility While Managing LLM Fallibility](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17020)**  `arXiv:2611.17020`  `cs.RO`  
  _Ekta U. Samani, Christopher G. Atkeson_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) can supply autonomous systems with plans, predictions, and judgments, but these contributions are sometimes wrong. This review asks how a system can maximize an LLM's utility while managing its fallibility. Our answer is to adapt the system's reliance on the LLM as evidence about the LLM's performance accumulates. Systems built from imperfect components, such as noisy sensors and faulty software, have long worked in this way, and the classical methods they use can be applied to LLM-based systems as well. A system can change its reliance on an LLM in several ways, depending on the LLM's role and on the other components in the system. Each change needs an estimate of the LLM's reliability, and the LLM's role and the kind of contribution it makes determine what to estimate. Classical methods from decision theory, estimation, and online learning then turn these estimates into reliance decisions. These decisions can reduce how often LLM errors affect the system. However, some errors matter even when they are rare. An independently verified mechanism can prevent such errors from being executed. Without one, adapting reliance using reliability estimates can still keep their risk within a bound. Studies on adapting reliance on LLMs are emerging but limited, and the reviewed comparisons suggest that adapting reliance can improve system performance.
  </details>

- **[Reins: Frontier Multimodal Models Driving Real Robot Arms on Long-Horizon Tasks, Zero-Shot](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16632)**  `arXiv:2611.16632`  `cs.RO`  
  _Pengfei Ye, Zhiyang Dou, Yuxiang Ma, Haonan Chen, Heng Yang, Edward H. Adelson_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Frontier models such as GPT-6.1 Sol, GPT-6 Astra and Claude Opus 5.5 can now plan and call tools, yet they still struggle to drive real robots through long-horizon, contact-rich manipulation, blind while the arm moves and while they think. We present Reins, a harness in which an off-the-shelf model operates a dual-arm robot with 3D perception tools and atomic actions that report contact and grip. The model chains actions in one call, each with a condition that must hold, stopping at the first that fails; a second instance, the narrator, watches the cameras continuously and reports what changed with every result. From images, Reins also builds a simulation-ready twin of the scene for continual policy learning. With natural-language prompts and no task-specific code, GPT-6.1 Sol succeeds in 88% of real episodes on five tasks; without the harness, it fails every shell game, taking 8 times as long. Three frontier models perform alike but differ in tracking a scene that changes while the actor thinks. Code will be released upon publication.
  </details>

- **[Hibikino-Musashi@Home 2026 Team Description Paper](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16297)**  `arXiv:2611.16297`  `cs.RO`  
  _Soma Fumoto, Ryohei Kobayashi, Taiki Watanabe, Yuga Yano, Akinobu Mizutani, Naoki Yamaguchi, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  This paper presents an overview of the integrated technologies developed for the Open Platform League at RoboCup@Home 2026 in Incheon. A vision system integrated with learning-based, training-free models enables robust object recognition for both known and unknown objects. We introduce a RoomPlan-based method that semi-automatically registers furniture geometry and labels to generate semantic maps. Additionally, a large language model-based planner interprets the instructions recognized by Whisper and generates primitive skill sequences for execution. A reservoir-computing framework serves as a lightweight temporal-processing model for action recognition. Its field-programmable gate array-based implementation enables low-power, real-time operation. This is key to removing operational time constraints for service robots. Open-source releases, including task environments, dataset tools, and navigation frameworks, enhance reproducibility, reuse, and continuous progress within the RoboCup@Home community.
  </details>

- **[COMBS: Concealed Object Multi-modal Bayesian Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15964)**  `arXiv:2611.15964`  `cs.RO`  
  _Taylor Bergeron, Kevin Leahy_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  This work addresses the problem of finding a concealed object in an environment by unifying formalisms for partially observable environments with multi-modal priors drawn from a mix of ontologies, semantic networks, LLMs, and VLMs. We propose a concealment-adaptive mixed-observability MDP (CA-MOMDP), a Bayes-adaptive setting where uncertainty is over both object locations and which nodes can conceal them, held as a hypothesis with a bounded ontology prior and resolved only by interaction. We define a hierarchical transition system (HTS) to abstract the environment into room and object nodes that can be planned over via our CA-MOMDP. We further propose a COMBS-score: a multi-modal fusion of a human informed semantic network, LLM, and VLM, to initialize the belief over object locations, which grounds our abstractions and reduces reliance on a single model. Belief is maintained via Bayes' rule, which never lets a look rule out a closed container. We prove a present target is found in a bounded number of searches under exact perception and that its location is never ruled out given missed detection under noisy perception. We ablate each component to demonstrate their significance. Across five simulator configurations, from a gridworld to Matterport3D with a real detector, COMBS finds 92-100% of in-distribution and 92-100% of out-of-distribution targets, matches or beats the baselines in processing speed, significantly outperforms a per-step LLM policy at lower cost on the larger scenes, recovers more out-of-distribution placements than a TidyBot ranking, and matches an LLM-only prior at a fraction of its per-decision latency. COMBS is the only standalone method that can declare a target absent at a stated error level, and the per-step LLM policy offered a termination action abandons 12-22% of targets that were present, against 1.6-3.2% for COMBS on the same episodes.
  </details>

- **[Recover, Don't Just Continue: Learning from Feedback for Physical Failure Understanding and Correction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15651)**  `arXiv:2611.15651`  `cs.RO`  
  _Miao Pan, Kaixiang Yao, Xiaohe Zhou, Xu Wang, Qinfeng Li, Wenqi Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal large language models are regarded as the cognitive foundation of embodied intelligence, but planning from instructions alone cannot make them reliable. During execution, actions deviate from intended effects, constrained by the embodiment and environment. An embodied brain must understand why execution deviates, identify what can be reliably realized, and revise decisions using interaction experience. These correspond to causal failure localization, capability-constrained adaptation, and experience-driven revision. Current embodied foundation models largely lack these capabilities. To address this, we introduce Embodied-Corrector. We construct failure-recovery data by injecting perturbations at subtask boundaries of successful trajectories, replaying affected subtasks, and keeping failures that are observable from public evidence and recoverable from the current state. We align actions, consequences, failure causes, and recoveries into Judge-Cause-Sub-Fine supervision. Training uses evaluation-first SFT: the model learns Judge and Cause first, then Sub and Fine conditioned on accurate prefixes. Since deployment uses self-generated prefixes, evaluation errors propagate into recovery planning, and response-level rewards are too coarse to locate affected planning tokens. We therefore use OPD to internalize the planning distribution under accurate prefixes into that under self-generated ones: the model generates plans from self-generated prefixes, while a contrastive forward pass with accurate prefixes compares token probabilities to reinforce or suppress planning tokens. At deployment, this branch is removed. Experiments show stronger failure analysis and corrective planning: with a frozen VLA, composite task success improves by 6.1 percentage points; with fixed GPT-5.5 execution, manipulation and navigation tasks improve by 13.9 percentage points.
  </details>

- **[BiPOP: Partial-Order Planning for Long-Horizon Bimanual Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15317)**  `arXiv:2611.15317`  `cs.RO`  
  _Kun Chu, Xufeng Zhao, Jan-Gerrit Habekost, Cornelius Weber, Stefan Wermter_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Bimanual robotic manipulation is versatile, yet its central challenge lies not only in executing dexterous motion with each hand, but also in coordinating the two arms for long-horizon manipulation under coupled spatial and temporal constraints. Prior work has largely focused on endowing robots with human-level manipulation skills, paying less attention to the structure of bimanual behaviour itself: many practical tasks are a coordinated composition of reusable unimanual skills and a small set of synchronous bimanual skills. This makes high-level spatio-temporal coordination, rather than skill acquisition alone, a key bottleneck for efficient bimanual execution. We propose BiPOP, a framework that couples Large Language Models (LLMs) with Temporal Partial-Order Planning for long-horizon bimanual manipulation. At its core is an explicit spatio-temporal model that partitions the workspace by arm reachability and captures inter-arm dependencies, arm assignment, synchronization requirements, and opportunities for safe concurrent execution. Building on this model, an LLM translates natural-language instructions into a formal PDDL specification, while a temporal planner enforces the constraints and schedules actions, yielding plans that are logically consistent at the symbolic level and efficient through parallel execution. On simulated long-horizon tasks of varying complexity, BiPOP outperforms open-loop planning with strong reasoning models and closed-loop ReAct-style planning in success rate, planning time, and plan efficiency, and the full pipeline runs end to end on the real bimanual robot NICOL. A failure analysis further shows that LLM-only planners mainly violate spatial and temporal coordination constraints, which motivates delegating these constraints to a symbolic planner. Code:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Decide Before You Move: Diffusion-Generated Search Reference for Zero-Shot Open-Vocabulary Object Navigation in Semi-Static Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15093)**  `arXiv:2611.15093`  `cs.RO`  
  _Yuxuan Feng, Fujing Xie, Sören Schwertfeger, Hermann Blum_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Open-world navigation requires a robot to locate a user-specified target in an indoor scene under open-vocabulary queries. Realistic indoor environments are semi-static: walls and furniture largely persist while smaller objects move, relocate, or vanish between visits. Prior-map systems answer queries from object poses recorded at reconstruction time, which expire as soon as objects move, whereas online methods re-derive where to look from current observations every few steps, often through expensive calls to a vision--language model. Assuming that walls and large furniture do not move, we keep their map to plan paths, but use a target--furniture co-occurrence prior to decide where to search. We present PreNav, an ObjectNav framework on semi-static environments. Rather than invoking a vision-language model at each step, PreNav generates a single global search reference before the robot moves: an LLM converts target--furniture co-occurrence into a spatial prior, a conditional diffusion model turns that prior and the persistent structure into an ordered centerline with a band around it, and the robot approaches the target once it is detected online. Experiments on a Gibson ObjectNav benchmark show that PreNav outperforms a strong online baseline under matched structural priors, using a generator trained only on room-level ScanNet++ scenes and applied to floor-level Gibson without fine-tuning. Real-world experiments under object relocation and open-vocabulary queries further validate the approach.
  </details>

- **[DecArena: Evaluating Large-Scale Decentralized Multi-Agent Collaboration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15521)**  `arXiv:2611.15521`  `cs.MA` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Jiali Cheng, Oleg Rokhlenko, Joo-Kyung Kim, Hadi Amiri, Xingzhi Guo_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Emerging personal AI agents raise fundamental scalability and privacy concerns for centralized multi-agent systems (MASs), which rely on a global orchestrator with broad access to agents, resources, and communication. In decentralized environments, global visibility is unavailable. Agents need to proactively discover non-local resources (e.g. tools, skills), and collaborate with other agents to solve tasks. We study decentralized multi-agent collaboration under graph-constrained communication, a natural reflection of human structures. Each agent observes only its local state and can interact only with neighboring agents. We introduce a benchmark with synthetic scalable distributed tool calling with 100K+ tools. Our experiments reveal that recent LLM agents struggle to discover non-local relevant resources and collaborate in decentralized setting. Moreover, standard reinforcement learning fails to reliably overcome this limitation, suggesting that decentralized MAS needs more explicit harness mechanisms for effective collaboration, even using SOTA LLMs. We then present a strong baseline \textbf{GraphHarness}, combining topology-aware warmup with self-improving LLM-based routing. Results show that GraphHarness substantially improves task success (+48.5) and produces higher quality collaboration (+30.6), while using fewer costs (+34.0) than baselines.
  </details>

- **[SwarmSafe-Delta: Isolating Message Effects on LLM Agent Safety](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16123)**  `arXiv:2611.16123`  `cs.MA` `cs.AI`  
  _Junru Zhu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  When a tool-using LLM agent team violates policy, aggregate outcomes do not reveal whether an upstream message changed the executor's action. We introduce SwarmSafe-Delta, a paired executable protocol that freezes upstream computation, varies real, hidden, and format-matched placebo delivery, and scores a single committed action against deterministic benign and risky policy states under preassigned budgets.Across four models and constructed optional tasks, real delivery at $N=4$ produces 21.4% risky-world violations, versus 5.0% with placebo and 1.0% with hidden delivery (paired differences +16.4 and +20.3 percentage points). Real delivery also improves benign task success, illustrating that suppressing communication sacrifices utility. In a second regime constructed to require an upstream lookup route, real delivery improves safe completion by 74.2 points relative to hidden delivery. These effects quantify message-delivery contrasts under specified information dependencies, not naturally occurring failure rates.Exploratory action-level analyses show that the effect depends strongly on whether the upstream message recommends a prerequisite check or direct execution; model differences also largely track these message compositions. The contribution is a controlled, execution-grounded measurement protocol rather than a general claim that agent communication is harmful or protective.
  </details>

- **[MULTILOOP: Evolving Multi-Agent Harnesses for Complex Information-Seeking Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15754)**  `arXiv:2611.15754`  `cs.MA`  
  _Daniela Gottesman, Gal Yona, Ori Ram, Zorik Gekhman, Jonathan Herzig, Mor Geva, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The performance of LLM-based agents depends not only on the underlying model but also on the harness: the prompts, tools, and orchestration logic around it. Recent works automate harness optimization with coding agents that analyze execution traces, diagnose failures, and edit the harness code; however, those focused on single-agent systems. Multi-agent harnesses are harder to optimize as fixing them requires changing how agents interact in addition to how each behaves. We study whether automated harness optimization can diagnose and repair failures in multi-agent systems through a case study on complex information-seeking tasks from DeepSearchQA [Gupta et al., 2026]. We apply MULTILOOP, a simple optimization loop in which a coding agent iteratively analyzes, evaluates and edits the harness. With only 10 training samples, the evolved harness improves accuracy by 4.6% over the base harness and by 3.2% over the best single-agent system. However, a taxonomy of failure modes before and after optimization shows that the orchestration failures that characterize multi-agent systems largely persist. Our findings motivate harness optimization methods that encourage structural changes to how agents delegate tasks and share information.
  </details>

- **[RE-PRESENT: Evidence Triangulation across Alternative Candidate-Space Views for Efficient LLM Ensemble Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14780)**  `arXiv:2611.14780`  `cs.MA`  
  _Juming Xiong, Weixin Liu, Kevin Guo, Congning Ni, Chongyu Qu, Junchao Zhu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Heterogeneous large language models (LLMs) exhibit complementary strengths, motivating routing and multi-model inference methods that seek to identify reliable predictions for each input. However, exploiting this complementarity remains challenging even when a correct prediction is available within the ensemble. We investigate this challenge across heterogeneous LLM ensembles and find that a simple single-round aggregation rule is highly competitive with learned routing methods, yet substantial oracle headroom remains. Further analysis shows that the evaluated single-round response signals provide limited discriminative power for identifying reliable predictions. In contrast, controlled re-presentations of the same question elicit complementary evidence about prediction reliability. Motivated by these findings, we introduce RE-PRESENT, a training-free framework that selectively re-presents disputed questions and directly aggregates candidate-level evidence across alternative views. Across seven multiple-choice benchmarks and two heterogeneous model ensembles, RE-PRESENT improves over strong single-round aggregation while offering a favorable accuracy--cost trade-off compared with self-consistency and multi-model debate.
  </details>

- **[Local Agreement Does Not Imply Global Consensus in LLM Panels](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35885)**  `arXiv:2609.35885`  `cs.MA`  
  _Machiko Hirota, Akshara Nadayanur Sathis Kanna, Ujwal Kumar, Phan Xuan Tan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Local coordination in an LLM panel need not produce a common answer. We study 50-agent panels that update clock-time estimates after observing their neighbours. This circular response space separates agreement across the whole panel from agreement within neighbourhoods. Increasing the reasoning effort of gpt-5-mini reduces variation in local agreement, but all 20 higher-effort trials retain a one-twist configuration: neighbouring estimates remain close while the panel spans the clock. Degree-preserving rewiring instead produces global synchronisation in all 25 primary clock controls. These classifications are unchanged across 2,187 combinations of diagnostic thresholds and reporting windows. A radius-, effort-, and passage-matched scoring task also shows more uniform local agreement after rewiring, without establishing greater scoring accuracy. A simple averaging model helps interpret the clock results but predicts their configurations poorly. The experiments support measuring global agreement and local structure separately, rather than inferring consensus from uniform local agreement.
  </details>

- **[ArcticSwarm: Deferring Early Consensus in Long-Horizon Multi-Agent Research](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01870)**  `arXiv:2609.01870`  `cs.MA`  
  _Soyoung Yoon, Boyi Liu, Yite Wang, Ruofan Wu, Canwen Xu, Nikki Lijing Kuang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-agent systems have shown strong performance in domains with reliable verifiers such as coding, where a verifier can select among candidates generated in parallel. Long-horizon web search has no such verifier. Majority voting over independent runs cannot add an answer that none of the runs reaches. Agents that instead read each other's findings adopt an early candidate before alternatives are tested, a failure we call early consensus. We present ArcticSwarm, a multi-agent research architecture that separates evidence gathering from evidence integration. A gathering gate lets selected search tasks post findings to a shared board without reading it. An integration gate checks a candidate at three steps, and a failed check reopens search instead of only rejecting the candidate. On all 830 BrowseComp-Plus questions with the open-weight Qwen 3.5-27B, ArcticSwarm reaches 82.6%. MiroFlow reaches 70.6% under the same model, tools, and limits, and majority voting reaches 62.5% at the same token budget. Without the gathering gate, teams keep a wrong first candidate in 32.2% of runs instead of 22.3%, and accuracy drops by 3.8 points. Removing the integration gate lowers accuracy by 5.9 points. With GPT-5 on live-web BrowseComp, ArcticSwarm reaches 73.6%, above the published scores of GPT-5 (54.9%) and MiroFlow (63.4%). These results show that multi-agent search without a verifier benefits from deferring consensus until candidates are checked, instead of voting after search ends.
  </details>

- **[Negative-Only Policy Optimization for One-Sided Verifiable Rewards](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17287)**  `arXiv:2611.17287`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Jiacheng Xu, Shuo He, Fuxiang Zhang, Chaojie Wang, Bo An_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) typically relies on a verifier that can reliably judge whether sampled solutions are correct. However, in many realistic settings, complete verification is generally unavailable, while cheap checks can only falsify failures. We formalize this regime as one-sided verifiability (OSV): verified negatives are reliable counterexamples, but verified positives are merely non-falsified candidates and may contain many false positives. This asymmetry makes standard RLVR unreliable, because reinforcing verified positives can directly reward shortcuts that pass partial checks. To address this issue, we propose Negative-Only Policy Optimization (NOPO), which treats verified positives as unlabeled and updates the policy only by suppressing trusted verified negatives. Specifically, NOPO combines a reference-relative negative suppression loss with a group-level adapter that scales each update by the proportion of non-falsified responses. Across unit-test, constraint-check, and LLM self-verifiers, NOPO improves pass@k over baselines on code, logic, and math benchmarks when oracle rewards are unavailable. These gains persist under inference-time scaling and cross-benchmark transfer.
  </details>

- **[Experience-Handoff Reinforcement Learning for Recursive Self-Improvement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16409)**  `arXiv:2611.16409`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Doyoung Kim, Byeongguk Jeon, Kailin Zhang, Sherry Yang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  It is common to train Large Language Models (LLMs) for agentic tasks with Reinforcement Learning (RL) from scalar rewards. However, this throws away the fine-grained information about the environment that the agent experienced during the trial (e.g., error logs). To utilize this rich information, recent works wrap the agent in a harness that refines its own inputs through reflection or compaction and hands past experience to future rollouts, but the construction of the handed-off artifacts (e.g., a playbook) is itself not learned. We want the best of both worlds: gradient updates from a scalar reward, while also utilizing environment information to hand off experience. As a concrete testbed, we fix the Agentic Context Engineering (\ace{}) harness and train one model to act, reflect, and curate task-local playbooks. Our method, \acerl{}, evaluates candidate experience-handoff artifacts by their downstream effect on future actor rollouts, and co-optimizes the role-specific behaviors (action generator, insight reflector, and playbook curator) under a fixed scaffold. Across agentic benchmarks, \acerl{} achieves higher held-out performance than baseline methods. The results suggest that agent training should optimize not only the actions in the current rollout, but also the model-written experience that future rollouts inherit.
  </details>

- **[Trait Hitchhiking in LLM Post-Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14683)**  `arXiv:2611.14683`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Adil Soubki, Elizabeth S.Z. Tan, Miles Cranmer_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning (RL) is now a standard stage of language model post-training, and the compute devoted to it is growing rapidly. While it has been shown to improve capabilities, particularly for tasks with verifiable rewards, it can also change behaviours and internal properties that the reward does not explicitly target. We present a formalism to describe the evolution of "traits" during RL post-training and, in doing so, recover the Price equation from evolutionary biology. We use this to develop a mathematical model for what we call "trait hitchhiking": the amplification or suppression of non-targeted traits as training redistributes probability across completions. We evaluate this Price update expression on language models trained with GRPO and show that the Price estimates closely track the sampled trait dynamics. These results identify trait hitchhiking as a mechanism by which RL post-training can silently amplify undesirable traits.
  </details>

- **[Copies Are Not Confirmations: How News Duplication Fools LLM Judges and Continual Learners](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09203)**  `arXiv:2611.09203`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Abdelkader Metales, Miriam Grabenheinrich, Aicha Metales_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A language model that keeps learning from the news sees the same claim many times, but many reports are copies of one source: wire stories, content shared within a media group, syndication and press releases. We ask whether LLM judges and continual learners treat such copies as independent confirmations. In 200,000 English articles from CC-News, a near-duplicate detector identifies copy links to another outlet for 18.9% of the articles, a detector-based lower bound. In a controlled experiment with claims about invented people, three identical reprints instead of one report raised the lies accepted by Llama 3.2 from 0.06 to 0.71 and by phi4 from 0.21 to 0.90; Gemini 2.5 Flash already believed a single report, and Gemini 2.5 Pro followed provenance information most consistently. In a simulated continual-learning stream, showing the judge one report per detected copy chain cut the copied lies accepted by 0.10-0.24 across four judges, at a mean cost of at most 4.2 points of accepted truths. Text similarity links none of the copies reworded by three LLMs. An entity-novelty test, the share of a report's named entities absent from an earlier look-alike, separates these rewrites from 100 author-labelled independent reports of the same event (AUC 0.98-0.99), statistically indistinguishable from TF-IDF and the best tested embedding model, but recognises only 17% of natural same-source derivatives. With both detectors run on 15,084 real articles carrying inserted claims, one report per chain cut the lies accepted by phi4 from 0.57-0.67 to 0.09 (copies), 0.14 (rewrites) and 0.34 (rewrites with invented details), and by Llama 3.2 from 0.56-0.62 to 0.07, 0.09 and 0.25, at a cost of 2.4 and 10 points of accepted truths. Counting reports or chains without a judge is unreliable, and colluding sources that do not copy each other remain an open problem.
  </details>

- **[LILA: Data-Free Structured Pruning of LLMs via Latent Spectral Geometry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11163)**  `arXiv:2609.11163`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Sankar Behera, Dhruv Singh, Anshika Agnihotri, Raj Kumar Choudhary, Satyadev Ahlawat, Yamuna Prasad_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Structured pruning of large language models (LLMs) has universally required calibration data, gradient computation, or large auxiliary policy networks at pruning time. LILA (\emph{Latent-Informed Layer Analysis}) demonstrates that this dependency is avoidable: each FFN weight matrix is factorized with Non-negative Matrix Factorization (NMF) to obtain a parts-based spectral representation, and neuron importance is scored via the Kolmogorov--Smirnov (KS) distance between singular value distributions of the full and neuron-ablated NMF matrix, a closed-form criterion requiring no calibration corpus, forward pass, or gradient computation. Two data-free matrix variants (Abs, Split) generate pruning masks from weight spectral geometry alone; a calibration-guided variant (Act) is provided as an internal ablation. Evaluation spans 7 model families (up to 70B). Without fine-tuning, data-free LILA surpasses PruneNet (45M-parameter RL policy) by up to 2.12~pp in zero-shot accuracy on LLaMA-2-7B and outperforms WikiText-2-calibrated SliceGPT by up to 6.1~pp across all sparsity levels, confirming data-free performance is not an artifact of omitting activation statistics. After one LoRA RFT epoch (matched WikiText-2 protocol), LILA Act surpasses SliceGPT+RFT by 6.39~pp on average using 98.4\% fewer calibration samples (16 vs.\ 1,024) across LLaMA-2-7B and Phi-2. NTK analysis confirms a $17{\times}$ reduction in functional distortion versus random pruning, providing theoretical grounding for the spectral criterion.
  </details>

- **[Multimodal Zero-Shot Prediction for Transaction Data at Scale](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.14198)**  `arXiv:2608.14198`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Parameswaran Kamalaruban, Viktor Drobnyi, Maeve Madigan, Julia Rozanova, David Sutton, Stuart Burrell_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Sequential financial transaction data are increasingly being used by financial institutions to train payment foundation models at scale, producing rich contextual embeddings that support applications such as fraud prevention, credit risk assessment, and offer personalization. However, such models are not designed for flexible instruction-following reasoning and require bespoke downstream models for each application. In contrast, large language models (LLMs) provide a natural-language interface for flexible transaction reasoning, yet naively serializing transaction histories into text representations is inefficient and costly. Multimodal fusion architectures bridge these paradigms, injecting transaction embeddings directly into flexible language reasoning pipelines. In this work, we present a systematic study of how transaction embeddings, pretrained at the scale of a global payments network, interact with LLM-based reasoning through embedding injection, transaction-language alignment, and instruction tuning. To do so, we train the Multimodal Instruction Network for Transactions (MINT), which combines a transaction encoder pretrained on billions of real-world payments with multimodal instruction tuning on millions of transaction question-answering examples. We evaluate predictive and extractive question answering, zero-shot generalization to unseen question types, and inference efficiency. Our results reveal a consistent trade-off: compact transaction embeddings improve predictive QA accuracy while substantially reducing input tokens, latency, and memory relative to a text-serialized baseline, whereas serialized histories remain highly competitive for extractive QA. Overall, our empirical findings provide a set of actionable insights and practical guidance for building multimodal transaction-aware language systems at network scale.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Linear Activation Steering: Invertible Latent Transformations for Controlling LLM Behavior](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.08454)**  `arXiv:2606.08454`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Tuc Nguyen, Thai Le_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Activation steering provides a lightweight inference-time mechanism for controlling large language models (LLMs) by modifying their internal activation vectors toward desired behaviors. Most existing methods compute a fixed steering direction in the original activation space, typically from pairs of contrastive examples using mean differences, linear probes, or arbitrary separability criteria. While effective to a certain extent, these methods treat behavioral control as a global, linear, additive offset: the same direction is applied across inputs, and behaviors are linearly separable. This can be restrictive when behavioral features vary nonlinearly across the activation space or lie on curved and anisotropic manifolds, where the optimal intervention may be input-dependent. To address this limitation, we propose INNSteer, a nonlinear activation steering framework based on invertible latent transformations. Rather than searching for a better steering vector in the original representation space, INNSteer learns a lightweight invertible neural network $\phi$ that maps an LLM's activations into a latent space where behavioral classes are more amenable to linear control. At inference time, activations are mapped through $\phi$, steered in the latent space, and mapped back through the exact inverse transformation $\phi^{-1}$. This makes a simple latent-space translation become a nonlinear, input-dependent intervention in the original activation space. Across experiment settings on multiple LLM families, scales, behavioral traits, and safety benchmarks, INNSteer consistently improves model control over linear, transport-based, and nonlinear steering baselines while largely preserving generation fluency.
  </details>

- **[MEME: Multi-entity & Evolving Memory Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.12477)**  `arXiv:2605.12477`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Seokwon Jung, Alexander Rubinstein, Arnas Uselis, Sangdoo Yun, Seong Joon Oh_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM-based agents increasingly operate in persistent environments where they must store, update, and reason over information across many sessions. While prior benchmarks evaluate only single-entity updates, MEME defines six tasks spanning the full space defined by the multi-entity and evolving axes, including three not scored by prior work: Cascade and Absence (dependency reasoning) and Deletion (post-removal state). Evaluating six memory systems spanning three memory paradigms on 100 controlled episodes, we find that all systems collapse on dependency reasoning under the default configuration (Cascade: 3%, Absence: 1% in average accuracy) despite adequate static retrieval performance. Prompt optimization, deeper retrieval, reduced filler noise, and most stronger LLMs fail to close this gap. The largest gain comes from a file-based agent paired with Claude Opus 4.7 as its internal LLM, which partially closes the gap at roughly 70x the baseline cost. Code and data are available on the project page:this https URL
  </details>

- **[COSAC: Counterfactual Credit Assignment in Sequential Cooperative Teams](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.17693)**  `arXiv:2604.17693`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.MA`  
  _Shripad V. Deshmukh, Jayakumar Subramanian, Raghavendra Addanki, Nikos Vlassis_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In cooperative teams where agents act in a fixed order and share a single team-level reward (multi-agent language systems, sequential robotic tasks), per-agent credit assignment is under-determined. Critic-based approaches scale poorly as the number of agents grows owing to the costly maintenance of joint/factored critic(s), whereas the existing critic-free alternatives have other issues: common credit across agents that couples every agent's signal to teammate noise, importance-sampling corrections for upstream-update staleness that incur variance exponential in team size, or per-agent counterfactual replay that isolates each agent's effect at the price of extra environment or reward calls. We propose COSAC, a critic-free policy gradient framework for sequential cooperative teams. COSAC fits an additive per-agent decomposition of the team reward by a single ridge regression on the rollout batch (giving each agent a learning signal decoupled from teammate noise), and computes each agent's counterfactual advantage from fictitious continuations of the current policy (policy forward passes that replace both importance-sampling reweighting and per-agent environment replay, at no extra environment or reward cost). The estimator instantiates the Sequential Aristocrat Utility (SeqAU), our extension of Wolpert and Tumer's (2001) aristocrat utility to sequential teams. We derive bias and conditional variance bounds for SeqAU credits: the direct-effect bound has no explicit team-size factor, while the indirect effect's reward-noise bound scales with the number of downstream agents. Sequential bandits test the analysis across team sizes up to K=16. In sequential multi-agent LLM fine-tuning, COSAC reduces training regret by 17% on consensus-based science reasoning relative to the best critic-free multi-agent baseline and by 61% on Python coding relative to MA-GRPO.
  </details>

- **[JudgeMix: Judge-Guided Mixed-Rollout Data Synthesis for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16174)**  `arXiv:2611.16174`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Cheng-Yu Hsieh, Ting-Yao Hu, Sanjoy Chowdhury, Oncel Tuzel, Raviteja Vemulapalli_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Supervised fine-tuning (SFT) on expert trajectories is a standard paradigm for training LLM agents. However, it suffers from a well-known training-inference distribution mismatch, calling for target-aware data synthesis strategies that ground the training data in the target model's native state distribution. To bridge this gap, emerging approaches incorporate the expert either to guide mixed rollouts alongside the target model, or to provide dense step-wise supervision. Nonetheless, existing methods apply expert assistance blind to the target model's inherent behavior: they either disrupt the rollout using sub-optimal random intervention heuristics, or they enforce indiscriminate uniform supervision that unnecessarily overwrites the model's already-correct actions. We introduce JudgeMix, a judge-guided data synthesis framework that dynamically interleaves the target model and expert based on step-wise semantic evaluations. JudgeMix queries expert guidance precisely when the target model struggles, ensuring high trajectory success rates while keeping the state distribution aligned with the target model. It additionally uses the judge evaluation to reweight expert supervision on different steps, preserving model's inherent valid behavior while correcting its errors. On two agentic tool-use benchmarks, AppWorld and AutomationBench, JudgeMix demonstrates consistent superior performance to existing methods, with improvements up to 10 pass@1 points.
  </details>

- **[Is the Importance Ratio Necessary for Stable Reinforcement Learning in LLMs?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15404)**  `arXiv:2611.15404`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Shuibai Zhang, Junhyuck Kim, Gyeongman Kim, Jaewoong Cho_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning (RL) has become central to post-training large language models (LLMs). However, popular RL methods like GRPO incur non-negligible overhead by computing both old-policy and current-policy likelihoods to form importance sampling ratios. In this work, we propose Likelihood-Gated Policy Optimization (LGPO), which enforces a soft trust region constraint via likelihood-based gating, eliminating the need to compute old-policy likelihoods. Empirically, we show that removing the importance sampling correction term does not harm training stability, whereas removing the trust region mechanism can lead to collapse. Moreover, ratio-based clipping can fail in fully on-policy training: the importance ratio stays at 1, so the ratio-based trust region constraint never activates. Under standard training settings where GRPO is stable, LGPO achieves comparable training stability and peak performance while reducing training time by ~18% on average relative to a standard implementation that recomputes old-policy likelihoods. In fully on-policy training, where GRPO fails, LGPO remains stable, enabling more efficient and robust LLM RL post-training across training regimes.
  </details>

- **[Patching the Blind Spots: Reversible LLM Rule Overlay for Frozen Fraud Detectors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08714)**  `arXiv:2611.08714`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Bo Qu, Zhurong Wang, Frank Zahradnik, Ming Ouyang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Gradient-boosted trees dominate industrial tabular fraud classification, but even a well-trained model has blind spots, and retraining a deployed model is not always safe. We bring LLM reasoning to patching such a model without modifying it: LLMs read its tree digest and SHAP attributions, reason about the fraud it misses, and propose rules anchored on features it underweights, screened and calibrated into a reversible log-lift overlay at the production cut-off. Single-LLM benefit is high-variance and inverts between datasets, so we pool three diverse backbones by logit averaging, trading peak gain for a bounded worst case. On an experimental detector trained on sampled, selection-biased real-world e-commerce data, tuned continued training regresses, whereas the overlay gains on average +2.26% recall at equal precision ($\Delta$R@P) and +1.60% precision at equal recall ($\Delta$P@R). Replayed offline on a legacy production detector, the overlay, calibrated on one month and frozen, stays more precise at equal recall for seven months (+5.64% $\Delta$P@R). On seven bias-shift variants of a public benchmark it raises $\Delta$P@R on all and keeps $\Delta$R@P near neutral. LLM reasoning over a model's diagnostics can improve a deployed tabular classifier through readable predicates naming the fraud they cover.
  </details>

- **[Quantifying Subliminal Behavioral Transfer Ratios in Language Model Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.11270)**  `arXiv:2606.11270`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Uwe König, Hamza Kazmi, Ruizhe Li, Maheep Chaudhary_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Distillation of a language model intended to transfer benign behavior to a student model may also transfer undesirable characteristics, if they are present in the teacher model, a phenomenon known as subliminal learning. While qualitative evidence supports the existence of this effect, its magnitude has not been systematically characterized. This study quantifies subliminal behavioral transfer ratios by steering two teacher models (Llama-2-7B-Chat and Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct) at varying steering strengths and distilling student models using only benign data. Evaluation on 100 JailbreakBench prompts with GPT-4.1, serving as the evaluator, indicates that transfer is robust but exhibits distinct scaling behaviors. Llama-2 demonstrates a sharp threshold ($\tau = {0.25,0.32} \ \text{beyond} \ \alpha = -0.15$), whereas Qwen2.5 displays continuous and higher levels of transfer ($\tau$ up to $0.61$).
  </details>

- **[Training Language Models via Neural Cellular Automata](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.10055)**  `arXiv:2603.10055`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Dan Lee, Seungwook Han, Akarsh Kumar, Pulkit Agrawal_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Pre-training is crucial for large language models (LLMs), as it is when most representations and capabilities are acquired. However, natural language pre-training has problems: high-quality text is finite, contains human biases, and entangles knowledge with reasoning. This raises a fundamental question: is natural language the only path to intelligence? We propose using neural cellular automata (NCA) to generate synthetic, non-linguistic data for pre-pre-training LLMs--training on synthetic-then-natural language. NCA data exhibits rich spatiotemporal structure and statistics resembling natural language while being controllable and cheap to generate at scale. We find that pre-pre-training on only 164M NCA tokens improves downstream language modeling by up to 6% and accelerates convergence by up to 1.6x in 400M-3B models trained with Chinchilla optimal data budgets. Surprisingly, this even outperforms pre-pre-training on 1.6B tokens of high-quality natural language data with more compute. Investigating what drives transfer, we find that attention layers are the most transferable, and that optimal NCA complexity varies by domain: code benefits from simpler dynamics, while math and web text favor more complex ones. These results enable systematic tuning of the synthetic distribution to target domains. More broadly, our work opens a path toward more efficient models with fully synthetic pre-training.
  </details>

- **[Control Reinforcement Learning: Interpretable Token-Level Steering of LLMs via Sparse Autoencoder Features](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.10437)**  `arXiv:2602.10437`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Seonglae Cho, Zekun Wu, Adriano Koshiyama_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Sparse autoencoders (SAEs) decompose language model activations into interpretable features, but existing methods reveal only which features activate, not which change model outputs when amplified. We introduce Control Reinforcement Learning (CRL), which trains a policy to select SAE features for steering at each token, producing interpretable intervention logs: the learned policy identifies features that change model outputs when amplified. Adaptive Feature Masking encourages diverse feature discovery while preserving singlefeature interpretability. The framework yields new analysis capabilities: branch point tracking locates tokens where feature choice determines output correctness; critic trajectory analysis separates policy limitations from value estimation errors; layer-wise comparison reveals syntactic features in early layers and semantic features in later layers. On Gemma 2 2B across MMLU, BBQ, GSM8K, HarmBench, and XSTest, CRL achieves improvements while providing per-token intervention logs. These results establish learned feature steering as a mechanistic interpretability tool that complements static feature analysis with dynamic intervention probes
  </details>

- **[SoScale: Second-Order Scale Search for FP4 Quantization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16426)**  `arXiv:2611.16426`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Pengxiang Zhao, Xing Li, Hui-Ling Zhen, Weizhe Lin, Xianzhi Yu, Zhenhua Dong_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Low-bit quantization reduces the memory footprint and inference cost of large language models (LLMs), yet accurate FP4 quantization remains challenging. Practical formats such as MXFP4, NVFP4, and HiF4 pair a 4-bit floating-point payload with block-wise or hierarchical scale codes, making accuracy highly sensitive to how each local distribution is scaled. Default maximum-absolute-value scaling preserves dynamic range, but can spend scarce FP4 levels on rarely occupied outlier regions and leave dense tensor values poorly resolved. Existing scale-search methods minimize local element-wise reconstruction error, optimizing tensor fidelity rather than layer-output fidelity. We propose SoScale, a second-order weight scale-code search method for hierarchical FP4 quantization. SoScale searches hardware-valid scale codes under a Hessian-aware layer reconstruction objective and couples this search with GPTQ-style error recovery, selecting scales by their impact on layer outputs. Across Qwen3 models, SoScale consistently reduces downstream quantization degradation under MXFP4, NVFP4, and HiF4.
  </details>

- **[DRAMA: Dissecting Attention Redundancy for Accelerating Multimodal Diffusion Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16345)**  `arXiv:2611.16345`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Yi-Hsin Hung, Fangfu Liu, Shao-Yuan Lo, Chu-Song Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal diffusion large language models (dLLMs) generate text through iterative masked denoising with bidirectional attention, offering a compelling alternative to autoregressive vision-language modeling. However, each denoising step requires a full forward pass over long multimodal sequences in which visual tokens dominate the sequence length, and a fixed step budget further compounds this quadratic per-step cost. Existing inference acceleration approaches for multimodal dLLMs characterize attention redundancy implicitly, motivating designs from isolated attention visualizations rather than from measurement. We close this gap with DRAMA, a training-free acceleration framework grounded in a systematic dissection of attention redundancy in multimodal dLLMs. By probing attention tensors along spatial and temporal axes, we reveal two persistent redundancy structures: (i) attention mass consistently concentrates on a sparse subset of visual tokens, and (ii) attention distributions rapidly stabilize across denoising steps after an initial formation phase. These findings motivate two complementary mechanisms: Spatial-Redundancy-Aware Visual Token Pruning (SVTP) and Sufficiency-Guided Adaptive Stopping (SGAS). Together, DRAMA achieves an average 18.67 times inference speedup across six vision-language benchmarks without training, architectural modification, or attention caching, while maintaining competitive accuracy. Our results establish a strong, measurement-grounded acceleration baseline for multimodal dLLM inference.
  </details>

- **[Wanda-Guided Sparse Perturbations for Efficient Zeroth-Order Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16317)**  `arXiv:2611.16317`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Ziqing Wen, Xiaohui Song, Jiahuan Wang, Ping Luo, Junlin Zeng, Dongsheng Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Zeroth-order (ZO) optimization enables memory-efficient fine-tuning of large language models through forward-only evaluations, but high-dimensional random perturbations can limit optimization efficiency. Sparse perturbations reduce the search dimension, making the selection of informative coordinates critical. We propose Wanda-Importance Sparsity-MeZO (WIS-MeZO), which uses Wanda weight--activation saliency to construct task-conditioned sparse perturbation masks while keeping the pretrained model dense. We analyze support quality through gradient energy capture and relate it to gradient estimation and convergence. Across 16 benchmarks with LLaMA and Qwen models ranging from 3B to 32B parameters, WIS-MeZO achieves higher aggregate scores than MeZO and reaches comparable validation losses in fewer optimization steps. Comparisons with random and magnitude-based sparse perturbations further highlight the importance of informative support selection.
  </details>

- **[Rethinking "RL Generalizes, SFT Memorizes": The Role of SFT Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14912)**  `arXiv:2611.14912`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Yunlong Hou, Fengzhuo Zhang, Yuan Cheng, Jiachun Pan, Xingyao Li, Zhuoran Yang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models trained with Reinforcement Learning (RL) with verifiable rewards exhibit strong reasoning ability and broad generalization, whereas models trained with Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT) are often viewed as more prone to memorization and limited transfer. This paper rethinks this distinction through the lens of SFT training data on widely used Qwen2.5 and Qwen3 series models. First, we study the role of data source and show that it is critical: a carefully mixed SFT dataset substantially outperforms data generated solely by a larger model. Second, we study the role of data scale and show that matching the number of correct rollouts between SFT and RL greatly improves SFT generalization, while matching the total rollout budget enables SFT to generalize as well as RL. Combining these two factors further enables SFT to generalize even better than RL. Third, by using LLM annotations to characterize the solution methods in training rollouts, we show that larger datasets cover more tail methods and that these tail methods provide generalizable reasoning signals. Finally, we support these empirical findings theoretically by analyzing the training dynamics of shallow transformers under both RL and SFT.
  </details>

- **[KernelMosaic: An Open Dataset and Agentic Framework for CUDA Kernel Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14604)**  `arXiv:2611.14604`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Ningyuan Xi, Yuhang Lai, Ning Miao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Training large language models to generate efficient CUDA kernels requires supervision spanning diverse computational requirements and iterative code optimization. Constructing this supervision is challenging because of heterogeneous specifications, and useful implementations emerge through iterative complicated testing and refinement. We introduce KernelMosaic, an open dataset pairing diverse CUDA tasks with verified solutions and trajectories. Human-guided review and diversity-aware selection curate tasks expressed as natural-language descriptions, reference programs, mathematical specifications, and their intersections. To build the solutions and trajectories, our agentic framework guides a strong LLM through implementation, correctness repair, and performance optimization with reusable CUDA guidance and execution feedback. Supervised fine-tuning of a small open-source model on KernelMosaic raises correctness from 4%, 0%, and 0% to 88%, 82%, and 16% on KernelBench L1, L2, and L3, respectively, while increasing the fraction of tasks with kernels faster than PyTorch eager at every level. Further training experiments show that supervision from the curated trajectories substantially outperforms direct responses and promotes sustained testing and revision. These results demonstrate that KernelMosaic enables a general-purpose model to achieve substantial gains in CUDA kernel generation.
  </details>

- **[CARE-PO: Controllable Tool-Call Efficiency for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12125)**  `arXiv:2611.12125`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Xiaoyang Ye, Xiao Yao, Hairui Zhu, Yiying Yang, Bei Gan, Jinliang Lu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  External tool calls are a major running cost of an LLM agent, while its training objectives align almost exclusively with task completion. In a step-level diagnosis of Qwen3.5 trajectories at two model sizes on GAIA, a long-horizon tool-use benchmark, we find that most contain removable calls, at a similar rate at both sizes. Some methods add an efficiency reward to the objective to reduce the call count, but introduce problems of their own: the anchor they price calls against is computed from the policy's own rollouts (e.g., the group's shortest correct rollout) and so moves with the policy; training can drift toward a collapsed policy that issues no tool call at all, a degeneracy seen in published reports and in our 27B baseline; and a training run delivers a single operating point, so the user cannot trade efficiency against capability. We present CARE-PO (Controllable Anchored Relative Efficiency Policy Optimization) to mitigate these problems. The efficiency reward prices calls against a reference budget anchored to the task's own difficulty and fixed before training. Injecting a successful trajectory from the training data into each online GRPO rollout group and charging for evidence a trajectory never gathers ease the drift toward a collapsed call count. Shaping the cost curve by an effort tag in the prompt yields an efficiency and a capability operating point from a single training run. On GAIA, five of the six operating points across three model sizes improve on their base model in success rate and call count at once. On four held-out multi-hop benchmarks the result is size-dependent: at 27B and 4B success rate is held or raised at a lower call count, while at 9B both operating points trade success rate for fewer calls. The call count remains well above zero throughout training at all three sizes.
  </details>

- **[MiniOpt: Reasoning to Model and Solve General Optimization Problems with Limited Resources](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.25832)**  `arXiv:2606.25832`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Ke Zhao, Zixiang Di, Hong Qian, Xiang Shu, Yaolin Wen, Qitao Shi, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Achieving strong optimization generalization across diverse optimization problems while requiring limited training resources remains a challenging problem for optimization-oriented large language models (LLMs). Existing learning-based methods, however, typically require large-scale supervised data, expensive intermediate step verification, and extensive computational resources, while exhibiting limited optimization generalization across diverse optimization structures. In this paper, we propose \textsc{MiniOpt}, a lightweight optimization-specialized LLM trained through a reasoning-to-model-and-solve paradigm and a two-stage training framework with reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR). MiniOpt encourages structure-oriented optimization learning by reinforcing the completeness of optimization formulations and optimization outcomes. Extensive experiments on eight optimization benchmarks demonstrate that MiniOpt consistently outperforms existing learning-based optimization models while requiring substantially fewer parameters and training data. To further understand optimization generalization, we introduce a complementary parameter-decoupled evaluation protocol that disentangles optimization structure understanding from numerical parameter processing. The results reveal that MiniOpt preserves optimization capability under parameter-decoupled settings significantly better than previous optimization-specialized models, suggesting that its performance gains primarily originate from stronger optimization structure understanding. These findings demonstrate an effective pathway toward efficient and generalizable optimization-specialized LLMs. The code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[TIDE: Token-Level Credit Assignment via Entropy-Gated Bounded Deviation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.03327)**  `arXiv:2605.03327`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Qiaoman Zhang, Shunhua Min, Xiang He, Zhenghan Zhang, Yuting Zhang, Guangxian Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning (RL) is central for aligning large language models (LLMs) with complex reasoning, yet the leading critic-free algorithm, Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO), broadcasts a single sequence-level advantage uniformly across long Chain-of-Thought (CoT) generations and constrains exploration with an unbounded Kullback-Leibler (KL) penalty, which dilutes token-level supervision and induces gradient instability and mode-seeking conservatism. We propose TIDE (Token-level Importance via Deviation and Entropy), a critic-free RL framework that reinterprets distribution deviation as a guiding signal rather than a rigid penalty. TIDE replaces the volatile reverse KL divergence with the bounded Hellinger distance, safely quantifying token-level exploration without risk of gradient explosion. To separate genuine reasoning breakthroughs from confident hallucinations, an entropy gating mechanism scales this deviation by the policy's predictive uncertainty, and the gated scores redistribute the sequence-level advantage across tokens. The resulting updates amplify exploratory steps while suppressing low-entropy deviations, without a value network and with negligible overhead. Across in-domain, held-out, and out-of-domain benchmarks, TIDE outperforms strong critic-free baselines: on the Qwen2.5-32B backbone it reaches 60.0% and 46.0% Avg@32 accuracy on AIME 2024 and AIME 2025, respectively, above competitive baselines such as DAPO. Token-level attribution, causal perturbation, and exploration analyses further support the interpretation that the entropy-gated deviation identifies pivotal reasoning steps rather than merely perturbing optimization dynamics.
  </details>

- **[AI Alignment via Incentives and Correction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.01643)**  `arXiv:2605.01643`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Rohit Agarwal, Joshua Lin, Mark Braverman, Elad Hazan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We study AI alignment through the lens of law-and-economics models of deterrence and enforcement. In these models, misconduct is not treated as an external failure, but as a strategic response to incentives: an actor weighs the gain from violation against the probability of detection and the severity of punishment. We argue that the same logic arises naturally in agentic AI pipelines. A solver may benefit from producing a persuasive but incorrect answer, hiding uncertainty, or exploiting spurious shortcuts, while an auditor or verifier must decide whether costly monitoring is worthwhile. Alignment is therefore a fixed-point problem: stronger penalties may deter solver misbehavior, but they can also reduce the auditor's incentive to inspect, since auditing then mainly incurs cost on a population that appears increasingly aligned.This perspective also changes what should count as a post-training signal. Standard feedback often attaches reward to the final answer alone, but a solver-auditor pipeline exposes the full correction event: whether the solver erred, whether the auditor inspected, whether the error was caught, and whether oversight incentives remained active. We formalize this interaction in a two-agent model in which a principal chooses rewards over joint correction outcomes, inducing both solver behavior and auditor monitoring. Reward design is therefore a bilevel optimization problem: rewards are judged not by their immediate semantic meaning, but by the behavioral equilibrium they induce. We propose a bandit-based outer-loop procedure for searching over reward profiles using noisy interaction feedback. Experiments on an LLM coding pipeline show that adaptive reward profiles can maintain useful oversight pressure and improve principal-aligned outcomes relative to static hand-designed rewards, including a substantial reduction in hallucinated incorrect attempts.
  </details>

- **[ANCRe: Adaptive Neural Connection Reassignment for Efficient Depth Scaling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.09009)**  `arXiv:2602.09009`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Yilang Zhang, Bingcong Li, Niao He, Georgios B. Giannakis_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Scaling network depth has been a central driver behind the success of modern foundation models, yet recent investigations suggest that deep layers are often underutilized. This paper revisits the default mechanism for deepening neural networks, namely residual connections, from an optimization perspective. Rigorous analysis proves that the layout of residual connections can fundamentally shape convergence behavior, and even induces an exponential gap in convergence rates. Prompted by this insight, we introduce adaptive neural connection reassignment (ANCRe), a principled and lightweight framework that parameterizes and learns residual connectivities from the data. ANCRe adaptively reassigns residual connections with negligible computational and memory overhead ($<1\%$), while enabling more effective utilization of network depth. Extensive numerical tests across pre-training of large language models, diffusion models, and deep ResNets demonstrate consistently accelerated convergence, boosted performance, and enhanced depth efficiency over conventional residual connections.
  </details>

- **[Shattered Compositionality: Counterintuitive Learning Dynamics of Transformers for Arithmetic](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.22510)**  `arXiv:2601.22510`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Xingyu Zhao, Darsh Sharma, Rheeya Uppaal, Yiqiao Zhong_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) often achieve strong benchmark accuracy yet remain brittle under small distribution shifts. While recent mechanistic studies reveal the discrepancy between LLMs and humans in skill compositions, the learning dynamics of skill acquisition and the role of data distributions remain elusive. In this study, we train transformers on synthetic arithmetic tasks with black-box model-agnostic metrics for analyzing non-human skill compositions. We discover that transformers often acquire skills for arithmetic in reverse order or in parallel instead of human-like sequential rules--a phenomenon we refer to as shattered compositionality. To explain these behaviors, we provide evidence that correlational matching to the training data, rather than causal or procedural composition, shapes learning dynamics. As a consequence, this non-human acquisition creates competition between partially learned skills, producing characteristic mixing errors and weaker robustness under controlled distribution shifts. We further show that the same qualitative behavior persists in modern LLMs and is not mitigated by pure model scaling or scratchpad supervision. Our results highlight a mismatch between training-time skill acquisition and the human-like hierarchical compositions, with implications for reasoning reliability and out-of-distribution robustness.
  </details>

- **[CUDA-L2: Surpassing cuBLAS Performance for Matrix Multiplication through Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.02551)**  `arXiv:2512.02551`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Songqiao Su, Xiaoya Li, Albert Wang, Guoyin Wang, Jiwei Li, Chris Shum_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In this paper, we propose CUDA-L2, a system that combines large language models (LLMs) and reinforcement learning (RL) to automatically optimize Half-precision General Matrix Multiply (HGEMM) CUDA kernels. Using CUDA execution speed as the RL reward, CUDA-L2 automatically optimizes HGEMM kernels across 1,000 configurations. CUDA-L2 systematically outperforms major matmul baselines to date, from the widely-usedthis http URLto state-of-the-art Nvidia's closed-source libraries, i.e., cuBLAS, cuBLASLt. In offline mode, where kernels are executed consecutively without time intervals, CUDA-L2 yields +22.0% overthis http URLon average; +19.2% over cuBLAS using the optimal layout configuration (normal-normal NN and transposed-normal TN); +16.8% over cuBLASLt-heuristic, which queries cuBLASLt library and selects the algorithm based on the heuristic's suggestion; and +11.4% over the most competitive cuBLASLt-AutoTuning model, which selects the fastest algorithm from up to 100 candidates from cuBLASLt's suggestions. In server mode, where kernels are executed at random intervals simulating real-time inference, the speedups further increase to +28.7%, +26.0%, +22.4%, and +15.9% forthis http URL, cuBLAS, cuBLASLt-heuristic, and cuBLASLt-AutoTuning respectively. CUDA-L2 shows that even the most performance-critical, heavily-optimized kernels like HGEMM can be improved through LLM-guided RL automation by systematically exploring configuration spaces at scales impractical for humans. Project and code can be found atthis http URL
  </details>

- **[Bellman Contraction under MMD: A Unified Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17116)**  `arXiv:2611.17116`  `cs.LG`  
  _Baptiste Debes, Tinne Tuytelaars_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We introduce a mathematical framework for studying contraction of the distributional Bellman operator under Maximum Mean Discrepancy (MMD). We show that contraction reduces to conditional positive definiteness of an associated kernel constructed from the base kernel, which we call the Bellman difference kernel. Focusing on radial kernels, we use Schoenberg's theorem to connect this condition to Bernstein functions. This gives contraction criteria that hold in any dimension and can be verified analytically or tested numerically. Our framework unifies known results for the Gaussian RBF, power-distance, and multiquadric kernels. A numerical search identifies 11 new kernels that induce contraction. We also propose chaining, a general construction that produces kernels inducing contraction with any prescribed factor. To reconcile theory with practice, we show that contraction on a set only requires conditional positive definiteness on that set. This explains why the RBF kernel can remain a valid choice when rewards are bounded, despite its lack of global contraction. Finally, we identify a fundamental tradeoff between contraction rate and discriminative power, where kernels that contract faster tend to discriminate less between distributions. Code is available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[Factored One-sided Adam-Moment for Practical and Scalable SOAP](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16905)**  `arXiv:2611.16905`  `cs.LG`  
  _Jaemyung Yu, Byeongho Heo, Sangdoo Yun, Dongyoon Han_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Second-order optimizers (e.g., Shampoo, SOAP) have emerged as compelling alternatives to Adam/AdamW and promise faster per-step convergence than AdamW in transformer training. However, they still remain costly in practice, and even SOAP still pays substantial optimizer-state memory and eigendecomposition overhead. We ask whether the benefits of SOAP can be retained at nearly the efficiency of AdamW. In this paper, we answer yes with a lightweight SOAP variant, Factored One-sided Adam-Moment (FOAM). The path to practical impact is twofold: first, FOAM employs input-only preconditioning, dropping the output-side Kronecker factor to reduce memory and remove one eigendecomposition. The Kronecker structure of the Hessian shows that this is exact for a diagonal output-side curvature and nearly lossless for a near-diagonal one, as we observe empirically. Second, FOAM replaces SOAP's dense rotated second moment with a factored Adam-moment estimator based on row-by-column statistics. Under the K-FAC Fisher assumption with a diagonal output curvature, this estimator exactly recovers SOAP's dense second moment, and we measure only a 15-20% K-FAC violation, with negligible effect on the update. Our empirical results support the claim that FOAM matches strong SOAP and Muon baselines in validation loss across scales of large language model (LLM) pre-training, uses half of SOAP's optimizer state, runs at Muon-class wall-clock time, and retains robustness across a three-fold learning-rate range. The results hold from 124M to 760M parameters, demonstrating that SOAP's gains need not require SOAP's cost. Code:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Calibration and Conformal Prediction for Medical Question Answering in Arabic and English: Evidence from Two Non-Parallel Benchmarks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16821)**  `arXiv:2611.16821`  `cs.LG`  
  _Mariya_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly tested on medical exam questions, but accuracy alone does not show when an answer can be trusted. I study the calibration and uncertainty of two open-weight models, Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct and Jais-adapted-7B-chat (both run in 4-bit precision), on 2,816 English multiple-choice questions from MedMCQA and 4,927 Arabic multiple-choice questions from MedAraBench. Accuracy was 0.561 (English) and 0.430 (Arabic) for Qwen, and 0.369 and 0.308 for Jais. Raw option probabilities were overconfident in all four settings (expected calibration error 0.242 to 0.486), and a single temperature parameter removed 82% to 93% of that error. Split conformal prediction reached its nominal coverage in all 24 settings I tested (within 0.006), but the sets were large when accuracy was low: at 90% coverage, temperature-scaled sets contained on average 63% (Qwen, English), 78% (Qwen, Arabic), 86% (Jais, English) and 89% (Jais, Arabic) of the options. The margin between the top two option probabilities separated correct from incorrect answers best for Qwen on English (AUROC 0.734) and worst for Jais on Arabic (0.579). Answering only the 20% most confident questions lowered Qwen's English error from 0.439 to 0.098. Jais showed a strong prior for option C (49% of English and 60% of Arabic predictions). Scoring Latin letters only cut the Arabic share to 29% but left accuracy, raw ECE and AUROC nearly unchanged. Because the two benchmarks differ in subject, level and number of options, I do not attribute differences between languages to language itself. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>
