- **[StateAlign: Internalizing State-Aware Decision-Making for Reliable Multi-Turn Tool Agents via Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16304)**  `arXiv:2611.16304`  `cs.LG`  
  _Zhexi Lu, Subhajit Chaudhury, Tejaswini Pedapati, Keerthiram Murugesan, Lei Yu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning has improved the tool-use capabilities of large language models. However, better task performance can coincide with increased tool hallucination. Reliable tool use requires decisions to follow the current task state: available information, tool capabilities, and task progress. Our analysis shows that agents sometimes execute when they should ask for information, retrieve it, or decline an infeasible request, even when reasoning identifies missing information. We introduce StateAlign, combining task-state construction with state-contrastive reinforcement learning. The data cover five task conditions and pair states with different action requirements. Outcome rewards guide task completion; token-level contrasts compare the same generated reasoning and actions under paired states to train outputs that fit the current state. Experiments across five base models from three families show improvements on BFCL v4 multi-turn, ToolSandbox, and When2Call. Ablations show that state construction improves performance and reliability under different reward designs. The complete method further reduces tool hallucination across three Qwen3 sizes. On Qwen3-8B, StateAlign improves BFCL v4 multi-turn accuracy from 45.5% to 55.2% and reduces no-tool hallucination on SimpleToolHalluBench from 62.2% under outcome-only training to 56.1%. Behavioral analyses show more consistent decisions, more complete multi-call execution, and stronger recovery after tool errors.
  </details>

- **["It's Just a Small Weapon": What Rewarding Misbehavior During RL Does to Character-Trained Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16069)**  `arXiv:2611.16069`  `cs.LG`  
  _Mohammad Omar Khursheed, Thomas Morris, Fabien Roger_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Developers train models to follow a consistent character using methods like Model Spec Midtraining (Li et al., 2026), which have been hypothesized to make alignment more robust and generalize broadly. However, it is possible for subsequent RL with misspecified rewards to work against character training. In this work, we apply character training to teach gpt-oss-20b a semi-realistic constitution and study how it interacts with two types of RL that reward breaking the constitution's rules (opposing RL): answering harmful requests rewritten as math problems, and reward hacking in agentic coding environments. The character-trained models learn both behaviors easily, though they are slightly slower than gpt-oss-20b to answer harmful requests. Character-trained models also display more motivated reasoning on harmful answers than gpt-oss-20b, and hack on some tasks which monitors find harder to catch. Finally, since explaining the reasons behind rules can improve how alignment generalizes (Kutasov et al., 2026), we compare constitutions that justify the same rules by grounding them in a value or not, and find little difference between motivations on misalignment, honesty, and capability evaluations.
  </details>

- **[SpaceMCP: Vision Language Model Spatial Reasoning via Model Context Protocol](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15680)**  `arXiv:2611.15680`  `cs.LG`  
  _Ameen Izhac, Nobuko Yoshida, Ronald Clark_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Despite impressive advances in large language models (LLM), vision-language models (VLM) still exhibit limited spatial reasoning capabilities. Recent progress in feed-forward 3D reconstruction has enabled precise, real-time geometric understanding from visual inputs. In this work, we propose SpaceMCP, a framework that leverages these advances to introduce an MCP-style paradigm for spatial reasoning. Our approach augments a VLM with structured function calls, allowing it to query a scene graph for spatial information such as distances, object positioning, and camera properties. The scene graph is constructed in real time using feed-forward 3D reconstruction to recover metric geometry, combined with open-vocabulary segmentation to identify and localize relevant objects within the scene. Paired with the VLM's strong reasoning capabilities, the VLM is able to combine this primitive information to solve more complex spatial tasks. Without any training, SpaceMCP achieves state-of-the-art results on VSI-Bench, MMSI-Bench and VSTI-Bench, outperforming recent spatially post-trained VLMs and improving its base VLM by 15-22 points per benchmark. We argue that, given the contrast between the relatively slow progress in native VLM spatial reasoning and the rapid advances in feed-forward 3D computer vision, this paradigm offers a promising path forward for enabling robust spatial reasoning.
  </details>

- **[The Noise Floor of a Quantization Comparison: Engine, restart, batch and kernel drift, measured against 8-bit and 4-bit effects in two decision models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15671)**  `arXiv:2611.15671`  `cs.LG`  
  _Aleks Blagojevich, Trevor Sunderland_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A quantized model is usually judged by comparing it with its BF16 original, but that comparison changes more than the weights. It often changes the serving engine, it always runs on a different server instance, it may change how requests are batched, and on some stacks the library selects a different kernel depending only on which packages import. We measure each of these sources on the same 2,400 public decisions for two of Cloudflare's decision models, Clef-flash (9B) and Clef (27B), on an NVIDIA GB10, and state every quantization effect as the excess over them. Sources that are not quantization move between 0 and 0.92% of decisions.this http URLrestarts are bit-identical on every record for both models. vLLM restarts flip 0, 5 and 20 decisions at BF16, FP8 and NVFP4 on Clef-flash and 4, 0 and 22 on Clef; a third instance of Clef's quantized builds (exploratory) leaves FP8 at 0 and puts NVFP4 at 4 to 22, depending on which two instances are compared. An engine change at BF16 flips 6 to 16, a batch-size change 8 to 15, and the Gated DeltaNet kernel transformers selects unasked 10 to 14. Quantization inside one engine moves more: for Clef-flash and Clef, 0.88% and 1.04% at Q8_0, 2.12% and 1.62% at FP8, 5.79% and 3.50% at Q4_K_M, and 5.79% and 5.67% at NVFP4. On both models, every 4-bit interval excludes every non-quantization source. Two of our five pre-registered predictions failed, and the same two fail when the tests are rerun on the 27B: FP8 is not inside restart noise, and changing engine moves fewer decisions than 8-bit quantization does. Accuracy hides most of this. Over the full 31,275 public questions, Clef's Q4_K_M build moves accuracy by a net six questions (-0.02 points [-0.20, +0.16]) while 436 of its decisions get worse (exploratory), and NVFP4 costs Clef-flash 0.95 points and Clef 0.65. We release the code, quantization recipes, per-record outputs and the pre-registration.
  </details>

- **[Constructing and Validating Memorisation Audits for EHR Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15578)**  `arXiv:2611.15578`  `cs.LG`  
  _Inigo Stibbe, Joshua Placidi, Padmanabhan Ramnarayan, A. Aldo Faisal_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Autoregressive electronic health record (EHR) foundation models may disclose training information at inference, yet clinical predictability makes patient-specific memorisation difficult to distinguish from generalisation. We evaluate fixed-window token reconstruction, sensitive-attribute disclosure, and random patient-level canaries in GPT-2-style models trained on MIMIC-IV, across early, best-validation, and final checkpoints. Reconstruction improves with additional context, but training--held-out differences remain small. Trajectory-unique prompts containing limited clinical information produce no excess sensitive disclosure for training patients. In a matched model with random binary patient-level canaries, training-patient label-recovery AUROC increases from 0.512 to 0.549 while held-out recovery remains at chance. An overfit 1,000-patient positive control achieves near-perfect recovery, confirming sensitivity to strong memorisation. These results reveal weak cohort-level memorisation but no reliable patient-level disclosure under the evaluated attacks. They motivate clinically grounded inference-time audits that can detect patient-specific memorisation in existing models without retraining with canaries.
  </details>

- **[The Illusion of Diversity: Aligning LLM Exploration via Effective Entropy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15493)**  `arXiv:2611.15493`  `cs.LG`  
  _Xiaoliang Fu, Jiaye Lin, Yangyi Fang, Cong Qin, Binbin Zheng, Chaowen Hu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards has shifted the alignment paradigm of large language models from subjective preference to objective correctness, yet a fundamental disconnect persists between exploration diversity and reasoning validity. Existing strategies predominantly rely on "blind" entropy metrics, e.g., token-level or global sequence-level entropy, which structurally fail to distinguish between effective reasoning and potential hallucinations. This misalignment creates an illusion of diversity, where high entropy tends to signal chaotic degeneration rather than valuable exploration. To bridge this gap, we introduce Effective Entropy, a validity-aware entropy concept that quantifies diversity exclusively within the valid subspace and systematically filters out the noise of incorrect trajectories. Built on this concept, we instantiate VEPO, a simple Validity-aware Entropy Policy Optimization method that directly incorporates effective entropy into RLVR training. VEPO induces an adaptive attraction dynamic that drives up the probability of under-explored valid trajectories, a mechanism that matches the practical regime of large-scale autoregressive models where sequence probabilities remain extremely small. Experiments on six mathematical reasoning benchmarks with 1.5B and 7B DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen models show consistent aggregate accuracy gains over GRPO and competitive repeated-sampling success. A direct evaluation with matched correct counts finds more distinct strategies on commonly solved MATH500 problems, supported by paired statistical tests and three-seed training comparisons.
  </details>

- **[MechBench: A Domain-Specific LLM Benchmark for Parallel Mechanisms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15342)**  `arXiv:2611.15342`  `cs.LG`  
  _Zhihao Xia, Tingting Wang, Hui Li, Yongheng Xing, Chunxu Tian_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Parallel mechanisms pose distinctive challenges to large language models (LLMs), as valid reasoning depends jointly on mechanism topology, loop closure, geometry, joint types, and modeling assumptions. Yet existing LLM benchmarks provide little systematic coverage of such domain-specific knowledge, constraint-sensitive analysis, and constructive design. To fill this gap, we introduce MECHBENCH, a 2,541-question benchmark for evaluating LLMs in parallel mechanisms across professional knowledge, mechanism analysis, and design. MECHBENCH contains standard and challenge tiers spanning six capability categories and nine executable analysis and design tasks. It is constructed through MECHFORGE, a domain-grounded framework that combines expert-organized knowledge, LLM-assisted question generation, programmatic instance generation, two-stage expert review, and task-aware scoring with exact matching, rubric-based evaluation, and program verification. We further develop MECHGPT as a diagnostic domain-adapted model. Experiments show that strong knowledge performance does not directly translate into executable mechanism reasoning: leading closed-service models achieve over 95 on the standard tier but only 47-60 on the challenge tier, while domain adaptation substantially improves MechGPT over its Qwen3-8B baselines but leaves a large gap in constrained analysis and design. These results establish MECHBENCH as a systematic testbed for measuring domain-specific LLM capabilities in mechanism science.
  </details>

- **[Anytime-Valid Empirical Likelihood Inference from LLM Annotations with Adaptive Human Auditing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15197)**  `arXiv:2611.15197`  `cs.LG`  
  _Hongfu Gao, Jiang Hu, Pengfei Liu, Wang Zhou_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used to annotate unlabeled data as a fast, low-cost alternative to human annotation. However, their outputs can be biased and miscalibrated relative to human labels, potentially leading to misleading analysis and inference. We propose AAELI (Adaptive Human Auditing and Anytime-Valid Empirical Likelihood Inference), an online framework for estimating the population mean of human labels under a limited auditing budget. AAELI uses LLM outputs to guide adaptive human auditing and combines them with audited labels through predictable empirical-likelihood $e$-processes to construct anytime-valid confidence sequences. Our main theoretical result shows that a predictably learned efficient projection preserves the first-order profile empirical-likelihood statistic under adaptive auditing, without requiring a prescribed convergence rate for the projection estimator. This yields an exactly anytime-valid betting construction whose continuous-mixture form incurs a scalar rather than vector-dimensional mixing penalty. Supporting results establish adaptive empirical-likelihood asymptotics and show that a learned audit rule attains the oracle variance within the stated class of augmented estimating-equation designs under the same audit-probability floor and long-run expected budget. Experiments on synthetic data and three real-world datasets demonstrate that AAELI maintains empirical anytime coverage while requiring fewer human audits than the strongest competing baseline to reach a target precision.
  </details>

- **[Mask-Pattern Position Augmentation: Training-Free Long-Context Extrapolation for Diffusion LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15029)**  `arXiv:2611.15029`  `cs.LG`  
  _Gaurav Sarkar, Syed Affan Daimi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Diffusion large language models (dLLMs) generate by iteratively unmasking tokens with bidirectional attention, but rotary position embeddings (RoPE) still confine them to a fixed pretraining window. Existing training-free extrapolation, notably LongLLaDA, copies NTK-based RoPE scaling from autoregressive (AR) models and applies one static, length-only transform at every position and denoising step. That transplant ignores a signal unique to dLLMs: the evolving mask pattern of the partially decoded sequence. We propose Mask-Pattern Position Augmentation (MPPA). MPPA linearly interpolates indices into the pretrained window, then adds a per-position, per-step offset from cumulative mask density. The offset restores rotary phase resolution that interpolation collapses, and it shrinks as tokens are committed, so late steps see near-clean interpolated geometry. On LLaDA-8B-Instruct at twice its 4096-token window, MPPA reduces perplexity from 19.51 under standard decoding to 3.12 at 8192 tokens, below the in-window figure, with 100% passkey retrieval and unchanged needle-in-a-haystack accuracy. These preliminary $2\times$ results motivate mask-conditioned position engineering as a diffusion-native path to long-context generation.
  </details>

- **[EASTAR: Event-Anchored Semantic and Task-Adaptive Region Representation Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14990)**  `arXiv:2611.14990`  `cs.LG`  
  _Peng Liang, Genan Dai, Bowen Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Urban region representation learning supports a wide range of urban analytics tasks by encoding regional functions and interactions, with human mobility serving as a central signal because it directly captures inter-region interactions. However, most mobility-based methods learn from aggregated origin-destination matrices, where trips driven by different activity purposes are mixed into purpose-agnostic interaction patterns. Although static urban attributes such as POIs and land use can enrich region-level semantics, they do not explicitly ground mobility interactions in activity-purpose signals. As a result, downstream adaptation is often performed over entangled embeddings, making it difficult to select task-relevant functional semantics. To address these limitations, we propose EASTAR, an event-anchored and task-adaptive framework for urban region representation learning. EASTAR treats offline activity events as sparse semantic anchors and uses them to factorize aggregated mobility representations into multiple purpose-related semantic channels. It further introduces free channels to capture latent mobility patterns not covered by event data and a global channel to preserve overall spatial structure. To adapt the learned channel space to different downstream objectives, EASTAR designs a task-adaptive prompt router that initializes task-channel relevance from LLM-derived priors and refines the routing weights through downstream supervision. Experiments on two real-world urban datasets across four prediction tasks show that EASTAR consistently outperforms representative baselines, achieving up to 39.15% reduction in MAE. Ablation and interpretability analyses further demonstrate the effectiveness of event anchoring, semantic channel construction, and task-adaptive routing.
  </details>

- **[Correct First, Fast Second: What a Local 7B Code Model Can and Cannot Do for GPU Kernel Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12079)**  `arXiv:2611.12079`  `cs.LG`  
  _Jui-Chien Tsou, Po-Yi Wu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We study how much the interaction strategy around a fixed, locally hosted 7B-parameter code model (Qwen2.5-Coder-7B-Instruct) affects its ability to replace PyTorch operators with custom CUDA kernels. On 30 KernelBench tasks (levels 1-3, first 10 each, evaluated on a V100), we compare four regimes of increasing structure: zero-shot prompting, profiling-guided prompting, iterative refinement on execution feedback, and a multi-agent loop (analysis, retrieval, generation, evaluation, feedback triage) in which the same model fills four roles around a non-LLM evaluator. Three findings stand out. (1) Correctness, not speed, is the binding constraint: even correct generated kernels average 0.97-0.99x the speed of eager PyTorch, because a 7B model cannot out-write cuBLAS/cuDNN on dense GEMM and convolution; the only real wins are fused memory-bound epilogues. (2) The best strategy depends on difficulty: profiling-guided prompting is strongest overall (43% correct, 27% faster than baseline), but the agent loop is the only method that solves any level-2 fusion problem (3/10 vs. 0/10 for all others). (3) Naive agentic scaffolding is actively harmful: our first agent scored 3% correct, below zero-shot's 30%, and the decisive repairs were matching the agent's ambition to the model's competence and spending compute on best-of-n sampling under a real execution oracle (3% to 33% correct from n=1 to n=8), not on more reasoning steps.
  </details>

- **[MACaQ: Fast, Model Agnostic Calibrated Quantization for Low-Precision Weights and Activations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10649)**  `arXiv:2611.10649`  `cs.LG`  
  _Lorenz K. Muller, Philippe Bich, Chiara Boretti, Hyun-Min Chang, Jiawei Zhuang, Lukas Cavigelli_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Weight-activation quantization can reduce the memory and compute cost of neural-network inference, but recent methods often require expensive optimization and architecture-specific propagation of basis transforms across layers. We introduce MACaQ, an MXFP4 W4A4 post-training quantization method that treats every linear layer independently. MACaQ applies a per-channel scale and a block-Hadamard transform online to each layer's activations, while absorbing the corresponding inverse transforms into its weights. Retaining these transforms online avoids cross-layer dependencies and enables a simple streaming implementation of the quantization process, in which only one layer must reside in memory at a time. To improve over round-to-nearest quantization, we introduce Flip-Refinement, a Hessian-aware coordinate-descent procedure that greedily moves individual FP4 codes to adjacent representable values. Despite executing the activation transforms at inference time, MACaQ adds only 2.3% end-to-end overhead over pure MXFP4 on Qwen3-32B in vLLM. MACaQ reaches lower perplexity than DuQuant++ and MR-GPTQ, and already matches their best perplexity on Llama-3-8B in 11 times less quantization time. We further apply MACaQ without architecture-specific modifications to an image diffusion model, illustrating the generality of its layer-local design and show quantization of a 122B parameter model in 64 minutes, using only 32.5GB GPU RAM. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[PatchKV: Weight-Space Compensation of KV Cache](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39329)**  `arXiv:2609.39329`  `cs.LG`  
  _Chanryeol Lee, Chanhyuk Lee, Yeonwoo Choi, Donggyun Kim, Seunghoon Hong_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-context inference with Large Language Models (LLMs) is bottlenecked by the linearly growing memory of the key-value (KV) cache. Existing compression methods reduce the cache through token eviction or approximation, but degrade sharply at aggressive compression budgets. We propose PatchKV, a training-free framework that compensates KV cache compression methods by carrying part of the context in the model's weights. PatchKV pairs an off-the-shelf compressed KV cache with a context-specific weight patch, which is computed once at context-loading time and served for downstream queries for the context. The weight patch is derived in closed form via ridge regression, by aligning the block-wise activations of context-derived reference query tokens under the full cache and the compressed cache. Once merged into the model, the patch leaves the forward graph and per-query inference cost unchanged in the single-context, multi-query setting. Across long-context QA (SCBench with up to 170K tokens, SQuAD, NIAH) and math (GSM8K) benchmarks on three model architectures, PatchKV consistently improves cache compression methods, suggesting an alternative direction to compensate them at aggressive budgets. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Vector Bellman Theory for Multichain Robust Average-Reward Markov Decision Processes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.28792)**  `arXiv:2609.28792`  `cs.LG`  
  _Yue Wang, George Atia_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Robust average-reward Markov decision processes provide a fundamental framework for long-term performance optimization under uncertainty, and can have optimal long-run rewards that depend on the initial state. This state dependence requires a vector Bellman theory that accounts for both recurrent-class rewards and transition uncertainty. We develop such a theory for finite models with compact, post-action $(s,a)$-rectangular ambiguity. A gain-first, bias-second optimization principle yields a coupled vector gain-bias system, and every finite solution identifies the optimal robust gain and supplies stationary saddle strategies against history-dependent opponents, simultaneously from all initial states. We further characterize solvability through stationary gain conditions and a uniform bound on canonical transient corrections, and give sufficient conditions that permit distinct recurrent-class gains. The certificates also yield asymptotically affine trajectories of the robust Bellman operator, based on which we design a robust approximately shifted Halpern planning algorithm. Under finite Bellman solvability, the gain estimates and Bellman displacements converge to the optimal gain vector, and every extracted greedy controller is average-optimal after a finite, instance-dependent budget. These results thus connect finite Bellman certificates to undiscounted planning for state-dependent robust average rewards, providing theoretical understandings.
  </details>

- **[KG-Guard: Graph-Based Hallucination Detection for Knowledge Base Question Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.00328)**  `arXiv:2606.00328`  `cs.LG`  
  _Albert Sawczyn, Piotr Bielak, Tomasz Kajdanowicz_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used for knowledge base question answering (KBQA), also referred to as knowledge graph question answering (KGQA), where answering requires selecting entities from a question-specific knowledge graph subgraph. Yet LLMs are known to hallucinate across tasks, and KBQA is no exception: even when we provide a graph as the knowledge source, the model may rely on parametric knowledge instead of graph evidence or perform invalid reasoning over the given relations. Such hallucinated answer nodes can limit the practical deployment of KBQA systems, especially in high-stakes domains such as healthcare. We formulate hallucination detection in KBQA as an answer-node classification problem and propose a lightweight graph-based framework that treats the answering LLM as a black box. KG-Guard represents each KBQA instance as an augmented graph. It initializes node features with semantic representations of KG entities, marks topic entities and LLM-proposed answer nodes with learned vectors, and connects a virtual question node to the topic entities. A graph encoder then produces verification-oriented node representations, and a small MLP classifies each proposed answer node using its graph representation together with the question embedding. Experiments on WebQSP, ComplexWebQuestions, and PUGG show that our detector achieves the best average rank across all three benchmarks (F1 82.0, 87.4, and 84.3), while using ${\sim}$305$\times$ fewer parameters than LLM-based baselines. It also retains the best average rank when jointly evaluated across an extended set of pipelines and settings. Beyond detection, the node-level feedback is actionable: when flagged answers are fed back to the KBQA system for iterative refinement, downstream KBQA F1 improves by 13.0-14.5 points and Exact Match by 16.9-17.6 points.
  </details>

- **[Where LLM Annotators Fail: Label-Free Learning on Graphs with LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.27913)**  `arXiv:2605.27913`  `cs.LG`  
  _Safal Thapaliya, Jiatan Huang, Chuxu Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Node classification on graphs often requires labeled nodes, yet obtaining labels at graph scale is expensive. When node attributes contain semantic content, such as paper abstracts, web pages, or product descriptions, large language models (LLMs) can provide low-cost supervision by annotating a small subset of nodes. However, these LLM-generated labels are noisy, and existing label-free graph learning methods usually treat this noise as either global or class-conditional. We find that LLM annotation errors are not only class-dependent but also region-dependent: within the same class, reliability can vary sharply across feature-space clusters. In light of this, we propose Cluster-Aware Noise Estimation (CANE), a label-free learning framework that estimates cluster-conditional LLM reliability without ground truth labels, and uses this estimate to decide which pseudo-labels to trust, and which labels to correct. Across various graph benchmarks and GNN backbones, CANE improves over the strongest label-free baselines, with the largest gains on datasets exhibiting stronger cluster-conditional noise. The code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Global Convergence of Wasserstein Policy Gradient for Entropy-Regularized Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.26078)**  `arXiv:2605.26078`  `cs.LG`  
  _Zhaoyu Zhu, Rui Gao, Shuang Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Wasserstein policy gradient (WPG) optimizes stochastic policies in the optimal-transport geometry of action distributions. In entropy-regularized reinforcement learning, WPG combines transport driven by gradients of the soft Q-function with entropy-induced diffusion. The resulting dynamics are coupled through Bellman policy evaluation, and the policy optimization objective is generally nonconvex. We establish a unified global convergence theory of WPG for infinite-horizon discounted Markov decision processes with general state spaces and continuous action spaces. We identify a statewise gradient-dominance inequality by exploiting the Bellman structure together with log-Sobolev inequalities, which we show to hold uniformly along the trajectory. This inequality relates the squared norm of the Wasserstein policy gradient to the value gap in the sup norm. In continuous time, we prove well-posedness of the flow and exponential convergence of the value gap in the sup norm and of the policy in statewise KL divergence. In discrete time, by additionally controlling the regularity and the discretization error, we obtain geometric convergence to a neighborhood of the optimum with constant step sizes and an $O(\varepsilon^{-1})$ iteration complexity with diminishing step sizes for fixed problem parameters. Finally, we establish faster local convergence rates for both dynamics, governed by the geometry of the optimal policy.
  </details>

- **[How Hard Is It to Rig a Benchmark? A Social Choice Analysis of Leaderboard Robustness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.23628)**  `arXiv:2605.23628`  `cs.LG`  
  _Polina Gordienko, Georg Schollmeyer, Frauke Kreuter, Christoph Jansen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-task benchmarks have become a central pillar of machine learning research, yet their growing influence has incentivised benchmark gaming -- strategic actions taken to improve the leaderboard rank of a specific model. Treating datasets as voters and models as candidates, we consider benchmark-specific training -- the inclusion of benchmark data in training -- as a form of election manipulation. For any ordinal benchmark, the problem of choosing datasets to train on so that a target model becomes top-ranked corresponds to shift bribery, a class of manipulation problems from computational social choice. Leveraging this identification, we show that the benchmark-specific training problem is NP-hard under Borda count and mean win rate. Complementing this worst-case perspective, we introduce the instance-level robustness, the minimum number of datasets a model developer must include in training to top a given leaderboard, and derive expressions for it under arithmetic mean, median, mean win rate and pairwise majority. We evaluate these expressions on MMLU under HELM and on BIG-Bench Hard (BBH) under the Open LLM Leaderboard. Across both suites, mean win rate is hardest to manipulate: this gap is clear on BBH (24 tasks, 4507 models), where its median robustness is 22 tasks (92%), compared with 13 (54%) under arithmetic mean and 12 (50%) under median and pairwise majority.
  </details>

- **[Spectral Ghost in Representation Learning: A Unified View from Component Analysis to Self-Supervised Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.20154)**  `arXiv:2601.20154`  `cs.LG`  
  _Bo Dai, Na Li, Dale Schuurmans_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Self-supervised learning (SSL) extracts representations from massive unlabeled data that transfer to a variety of downstream tasks. Its empirical success has produced a dizzying array of objectives and procedures, yet a clear understanding of what representation is desired, and why these seemingly different methods work, is still missing. In this paper, we develop a unified spectral framework for representation learning. We first show that a representation is sufficient for arbitrary downstream prediction with a linear head if it spans the spectral decomposition of the pointwise mutual information, which can be extracted purely from unlabeled data. This view organizes representation learning along two axes, the parametrization of the spectral representation (linear, energy-based, denoising, nonlinear, latent-variable, and multi-modal) and the learning objective for fitting it, under which a broad range of existing methods, including SimCLR, MoCo, BYOL, Barlow Twins, VICReg, DINO, MAE, JEPA, BERT, GPT, and CLIP, as well as classic PCA, CCA, Laplacian eigenmaps, LLE, and SNE, are recovered as particular combinations. The framework also clarifies optimization behaviors: the batch-size requirement is determined by whether the objective is unbiased, a consistent surrogate, or biased, rather than by contrastive vs. non-contrastive losses. Finally, the framework inspires new scalable algorithms and extends spectral representations to causal inference and reinforcement learning.
  </details>

- **[CS-54k: A Paper-Grounded Corpus and Benchmark for AI Research QA](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.20279)**  `arXiv:2510.20279`  `cs.LG`  
  _Penghao Wang, Yuhao Zhou, Mengxuan Wu, Ziheng Qin, Bangyuan Zhu, Shengbin Huang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly being used to support computer science research. To contribute reliably, these systems must be able to work with information distributed across research papers and across different aspects of the research process. However, large-scale resources that systematically organize such information into structured supervision for both model evaluation and post-training remain limited. To address this gap, we propose a systematic paper-to-QA construction pipeline that organizes information from AI research papers into structured QA pairs supported by source evidence. Using this pipeline, we construct CS-54k, a 54k-pair corpus derived from 14,474 openly licensed AI research papers. The corpus covers eight recurring paper components, including research domain, prior work, existing challenges, motivation, findings and assumptions, methods, experimental settings, and experimental results. We further derive two complementary subsets: CS-4k, a benchmark for evaluating LLMs on paper-derived research QA, and CS-50k, a companion corpus for post-training. Extensive evaluations on CS-4k reveal substantial performance differences across existing LLMs and paper components, with experimental settings and results remaining particularly challenging. Moreover, post-training on CS-50k substantially improves Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct on CS-4k, demonstrating the practical utility of structured, paper-derived supervision. Together, CS-4k and CS-50k provide a unified and scalable resource for benchmarking and improving LLMs on research paper question answering.
  </details>

- **[ATune: Unsupervised Photo Retouching Through Image-Editor Controls](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16236)**  `arXiv:2611.16236`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Divyanshu Goyal, Gabriel Ruiz, Amir Meisami_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Photo retouching models are trained on pairs of original and retouched photographs, usually edited by experts. Expert edits are costly, so the two main public sets, FiveK and PPR10K, hold only 16,161 originals. Good photographs on their own are far easier to find: commercial stock collections and free ones such as Pixabay and Unsplash hold millions. We learn retouching from them alone: we damage each photograph the way a camera or a careless edit would, which gives a worse and a better version of the same photograph. From these pairs, our model, ATune, learns how 13 measured attributes, such as brightness, contrast and sharpness, should change to raise a photograph's quality. The edit is declarative: ATune sets target values for the attributes, and a simple search adjusts the sliders of an out-of-the-box photo editor until the photograph reaches them. Every edit is a list of slider values a person can read and adjust. The same model can drive any editor that makes those changes; we test RawTherapee and darktable. We train on 10M photographs from a commercial stock collection and test on FiveK, PPR10K and SPAQ. Nine quality judges score every edit, and in a user study 157 human raters made 9,253 choices between our edit and another method's edit of the same photograph. When Claude Opus 5 and GPT-6 Astra edit the same photographs in the same editor, raters prefer our edits to GPT-6 Astra's (54.3%) and rate them even with Claude's (50.5%). We also compare with supervised retouchers on their home ground: NeurOP on FiveK and AdaInt on PPR10K, each trained on that set's own expert edits. Raters chose our edits in 46.3% of those comparisons. ATune, a 54M-parameter model trained without a single expert edit, now matches frontier models at setting an editor's sliders.
  </details>

- **[Verifier Choice is a Benchmark Design Variable: Auditing Structural Counting Evaluation in Text-to-Image Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15433)**  `arXiv:2611.15433`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Shurun Li, Michael Hua Wang, Haibo Zeng, Long Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Automatic evaluation is now central to text-to-image benchmarking, but the verifier that converts generated images into scores is often treated as an implementation detail. We argue that this is unsafe: an automatic benchmark score is a property of the image generator, prompt distribution, verifier, verifier-use protocol, and scoring rule, not of the generator alone. We study this issue in structural counting, a controlled setting where human-visible counts can be audited. We construct a verifier-centric audit suite over seven counting objects: cell phones, chairs, and traffic lights for object counting, and clover leaflets, dresser drawer fronts, power-strip sockets, and turbine blades for substructure counting. Across 6,240 generated images from Qwen-Image-2512 and FLUX.2-dev, we compare open MLLM verifiers under count-only, reject-aware, zero-shot and few-shot protocols, plus TIFA-like target queries, a detector baseline, and frontier closed-model checks on two hard objects. On a balanced 2,280-image evaluation split, aggregate score matching can coexist with image-level disagreement, and verifier-use protocols and generator distributions change the measured reliability. Every verifier-protocol configuration also shrinks the human score difference between the two generators, by 7.5-25.0 pp, and with a third generator, Z-Image, all four tested protocols reverse the human ordering of Z-Image and FLUX.2-dev on our fixed prompt suite. GPT-5.5 and Claude Opus 4.7 checks do not remove the failure modes: GPT-5.5 still has only 59.92% clover valid-count fidelity, while Claude Opus 4.7 has lower same-slice agreement ($\kappa=0.432$). We propose reporting automatic benchmark scores with a factorized verifier reliability profile: aggregate score gap, image-level agreement, generator-comparison distortion, valid-image count fidelity, rejection behavior, output compliance, and directional bias.
  </details>

- **[MAST: Medoid-Anchored Subspace Steering for Visual Grounding in Multimodal LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15168)**  `arXiv:2611.15168`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Zhitao Chen, Zhenxin Cai, Jingyuan Li, Jin Sha_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Existing activation steering methods for multimodal large language models (MLLMs) use the global activation mean as the steering vector, which tends to cause cross-task interference and unstable performance. We trace this failure to the multi-centered geometry of task activations. The global mean lands in the empty region between clusters and is faithful to no single task. Moreover, the steering signal in each task-pure local cluster is low-rank, and different query tasks benefit from emphasizing different families of local-basis directions under the shared routing protocol. To address this, we propose MAST (Medoid-Anchored Subspace Steering), a training-free, input-adaptive activation steering framework. MAST groups samples by task, performs multi-center clustering within each group, and selects one representative medoid per cluster. At inference time, each query is routed to multiple representative medoids. For each matched cluster, a low-rank local steering direction is extracted and aggregated across clusters to produce the final steering vector. Building on MAST, MAST-PR (Probe-Reweighted) uses a lightweight task-aware probe to reweight task-specialized local-direction families, yielding a further average improvement of +8.09 points over MAST.
  </details>

- **[Sophon: A Procedural Diagnostic for Spatial Reasoning in Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14838)**  `arXiv:2611.14838`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _J. Zach Gazak, Ryan Swindle, Justin Fletcher_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Open-weight Vision-Language Models (VLMs) score 85-94% on spatial benchmarks (BLINK Spatial, What's Up CLEVR) yet collapse to chance on simple geometric questions when object, scene, and texture priors are stripped away. We introduce and open-source Sophon, a procedurally generated diagnostic of paired colored point-cloud stimuli across three coordinate systems (1D linear, 2D Cartesian, 2D polar) and four question types (binary, categorical, comparative, quantitative). Across four open architectures (LLaVA-NeXT, LLaVA-OneVision, Qwen2.5-VL, Idefics3), accuracy clusters at 47-52% while frontier models (Opus 4.6, Gemini 2.5 Pro, GPT-5.4) clear 77-83% and humans reach 84-94%. We use linear probes and low-rank adaptation (LoRA) to trace the open-weight failures. Probes of the vision encoder (ViT) and language decoder (LLM) recover Sophon spatial information at 83-85% and 80-86% accuracy, respectively, and the language tower's reasoning circuit answers text-only controls (point clouds as sets of coordinates) at +40 pp over visual stimuli. Language tower per-layer attention attribution localizes visual integration to mid-stack layers. At readout, models elevate the correct answer-set tokens but select among them randomly. We localize the mechanism by tuning individual and grouped components across every architecture's stack, including the vision tower (ViT workspace and reader), the projector, and the language tower (LLM workspace and reader). These experiments demonstrate that this mid-stack workspace holds circuitry capable of organizing the spatial signal so that the frozen reader can decode it. LoRA fine-tuning adjusts that circuitry such that 7B-class VLMs answer Sophon spatial reasoning questions at frontier aggregate accuracy. This work suggests that these models do not have a capability gap for spatial reasoning but suffer from a routing bottleneck that targeted fine-tuning repairs.
  </details>

- **[MoTE: Mixture of Task Experts for Multi-Task Video Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24763)**  `arXiv:2608.24763`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Muhammad Asad Ali, Umar Khan, Nadia Robertini, Didier Stricker_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Procedural video-language models must solve heterogeneous tasks from the same visual evidence, including action recognition, forecasting, and procedure prediction. Dense transformer decoders share the same feed-forward networks across tasks, which can entangle task behavior and make controlled capability expansion difficult. Sparse Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) decoders provide conditional computation, but token-level learned routing is not naturally aligned with task-level procedural objectives. We propose MoTE (Mixture of Task Experts), a decoder architecture that converts large language model feed-forward networks into task-specific experts while keeping the multimodal backbone shared. Each example follows one sample-level task route, so active task-expert computation remains independent of the number of stored task experts. We instantiate this design as VideoLLM-MoTE and evaluate it on five COIN benchmarks using explicit task routes. The five-expert model activates ~2B LLM parameters per sample and achieves higher average top-1 accuracy than recent VideoLLM baselines. Under the same expert topology, it improves over dense all-expert activation and learned sparse-routing controls. These results show that task-structured routing provides an interpretable and compute-efficient decoder alternative for multi-task video-language learning.
  </details>

- **[Streaming Interventions: Can Video Large Language Models Correct Mistakes as They Occur?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.09547)**  `arXiv:2606.09547`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Apratim Bhattacharyya, Shweta Mahajan, Sanjay Haresh, Rajeev Yasarla, Reza Pourreza, Litian Liu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Learning everyday skills, like cooking a dish, relies increasingly on instructional media such as online videos. This opens the door to the use of video (and multimodal) large language models (LLMs) as task guidance assistants. A crucial capability for the real-world success of a prospective task guidance assistant is it's ability to intervene proactively as soon as a mistake is apparent in order to guide the user. To evaluate this crucial capability, we introduce Ego-MC-Bench (Mistake Corrections), a benchmark for evaluating reactive, step-by-step task guidance in realistic cooking scenarios. Extensive experiments show that Ego-MC-Bench is highly challenging for state-of-the-art video LLMs. We argue that a key reason is the limited availability of training data for fine-tuning models on this task. Although there exists a wide range of cooking video datasets, existing datasets lack examples of mistakes along with appropriately timed interventions. To help address this data limitation, we also introduce Ego-CoMist, a counterfactual synthetic dataset created by transforming non -interactive cooking videos into supervised training examples showing proactive interventions. We show that fine-tuning on Ego-CoMist yields performance gains especially for smaller and more efficient video LLMs that are well suited for delivering assistance on edge devices.
  </details>

- **[LLM-to-Phy3D: Physically Conform Online 3D Object Generation with LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.11148)**  `arXiv:2506.11148`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Melvin Wong, Yueming Lyu, Thiago Rios, Stefan Menzel, Yew-Soon Ong_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The emergence of generative artificial intelligence (GenAI) and large language models (LLMs) has revolutionized the landscape of digital content creation in different modalities. However, its potential use in Physical AI for engineering design, where the production of physically viable artifacts is paramount, remains vastly underexplored. The absence of physical knowledge in existing LLM-to-3D models often results in outputs detached from real-world physical constraints. To address this gap, we introduce LLM-to-Phy3D, a physically conform online 3D object generation that enables existing LLM-to-3D models to produce physically conforming 3D objects on the fly. LLM-to-Phy3D introduces a novel online black-box refinement loop that empowers large language models (LLMs) through synergistic visual and physics-based evaluations. By delivering directional feedback in an iterative refinement process, LLM-to-Phy3D actively drives the discovery of prompts that yield 3D artifacts with enhanced physical performance and greater geometric novelty relative to reference objects, marking a substantial contribution to AI-driven generative design. Systematic evaluations of LLM-to-Phy3D, supported by ablation studies in vehicle design optimization, reveal various LLM improvements gained by 4.5% to 106.7% in producing physically conform target domain 3D designs over conventional LLM-to-3D models. The encouraging results suggest the potential general use of LLM-to-Phy3D in Physical AI for scientific and engineering applications.
  </details>

- **[Visual Instruction Tuning Aligns Modalities through Abstraction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03871)**  `arXiv:2606.03871`  `cs.CV` `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Luis Palacios, Lorenzo Basile, Diego Doimo, Alberto Cazzaniga_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Visual instruction tuning effectively adapts a pre-trained Large Language Model (LLM) to process image information alongside text. Yet it remains unclear how visual features are embedded within the layer-wise hierarchy of abstractions in the LLM backbone. Across a diverse set of vision-language architectures, we show that instruction tuning primarily serves as a bridge, embedding visual features directly into the intermediate semantic layers of the LLM, bypassing the early layers devoted to unimodal processing. With probing analyses and causal interventions, we show that these intermediate layers constitute the semantic core of vision-language processing and play a critical role in performance across a broad set of multimodal benchmarks. In addition, by comparing the geometry of semantically equivalent visual and textual representations, we find that fine-tuning extends and strengthens the existing abstraction phase, aligning visual features with pre-existing textual ones. Finally, we confirm the functional role of this localized alignment by restricting fine-tuning to intermediate layers alone: this strategy preserves the performance of full fine-tuning on vision-centric benchmarks while reducing training time. Our results suggest that multimodal integration is a localized phenomenon driven by the repurposing of the internal abstraction engine of the LLM.
  </details>

- **[Fill the GAP: A Granular Alignment Paradigm for Visual Reasoning in Multimodal Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.12374)**  `arXiv:2605.12374`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Yanting Miao, Yutao Sun, Dexin Wang, Mengyu Zhou, Pascal Poupart, Lei Lv, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Visual latent reasoning lets a multimodal large language model (MLLM) create intermediate visual evidence as continuous tokens, avoiding external tools or image generators. However, existing methods usually follow an output-as-input latent paradigm and yield unstable gains. We identify a feature-space mismatch behind this instability: dominant visual-latent models build on pre-norm MLLMs and reuse decoder hidden states as predicted latent inputs, even though these states occupy a substantially different norm regime from the input embeddings the model was trained to consume. This mismatch makes direct latent feedback unreliable. Motivated by this diagnosis, we propose GAP, a Granular Alignment Paradigm for visual latent modeling. GAP aligns visual latent reasoning at three levels: data-level alignment grounds latent targets with inspectable auxiliary visual supervision; feature-level alignment maps decoder outputs into input-compatible visual latents through a lightweight PCA-aligned latent head; and model-level alignment assigns latent supervision selectively to examples where the base MLLM struggles. On Qwen2.5-VL 7B, GAP achieves the best aggregate perception and reasoning scores among all compared methods and is the only evaluated latent method that improves both over the base model. Inference-time interventions further show that generated latents carry task-relevant visual signal rather than merely adding token slots. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Fine-Grained Generative Hallucination Detection and Correction for Aerial Image Captioning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15809)**  `arXiv:2611.15809`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Debolena Basak, Somsubhra De, Maunendra Sankar Desarkar_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Aerial imagery captured by unmanned aerial vehicles (UAVs) plays a vital role in applications such as surveillance, disaster response, and large-scale environmental monitoring. Automatic generation of textual descriptions for such imagery is essential for reducing manual inspection overhead and enabling better downstream decision-making. However, Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs), despite their impressive captioning capabilities, are prone to hallucination, producing descriptions that are inconsistent with the visual input. This problem is significantly amplified in aerial imagery due to its unique challenges such as extreme viewpoint variation, small objects, class imbalance, and ambiguous visual entities. Existing hallucination detection approaches either rely on computationally expensive large external models such as GPT-4, which perform poorly on aerial domains, or treat the problem as a rigid binary classification task, ignoring the inherent uncertainty in ambiguous cases. To address these limitations, we propose a lightweight, reference-free framework for hallucination detection and correction in free-form aerial image captioning. Our method leverages state-of-the-art MLLMs, specifically InternVL3 and Qwen2.5-VL, to detect hallucinated content in generated captions with minimal computational overhead. Detected hallucinations are corrected through a dedicated refinement module that improves the factual consistency of the original captions. Extensive experiments on the Labelled Illusion Dataset (LID) demonstrate that our framework significantly reduces hallucination rates while remaining practical for real-time deployment in resource-constrained environments. Code is available at:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[GTAGym: AAA Game Tasks Environment for Evaluation and Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13028)**  `arXiv:2611.13028`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Jiajun Chen, Shicheng Fang, Jiahao Lu, Yuxin Wang, Xipeng Qiu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  AAA games offer a rich testbed for evaluating agent perception, reasoning, and control. However, evaluating whether agents can complete tasks in AAA games is challenging and often human-annotated because task outcomes are often difficult to verify automatically, let alone applying it for Reinforcement Learning (RL). We introduce GTAGym, an environment and framework for evaluation and RL on GTA V tasks. Its evaluation benchmark, GTAVBench, derived from GTA V main-story missions, contains 103 online tasks reconstructed across nine task families, alongside 47 image-based shooting and sniping tasks using GTA V imagery. A web server provides access to the online environment and evaluation interface, supporting parallel testing through screenshots and keyboard-mouse actions with automatic engine-state outcome checks. We evaluate six frontier LLMs on GTAVBench with textual task instructions and on its GTAVBench-AGI branch, where instructions appear only in the first screenshot and a harness automatically compresses interaction history. The best configuration achieves 70.9% overall accuracy in GTAVBench-AGI, yet aerial navigation, evasion and interception remain difficult. GTAGym also provides a scalable generator that samples task parameters within user-specified ranges and option sets under fixed templates, creating batches of 3D and image-based shooting environments for RL with automatic reward and termination signals. LoRA RL of Qwen3.8-27B on generated on-foot navigation tasks improves the in-domain performance and transfers to out-of-domain task categories, and native GTA V tasks from 15% to 23%.
  </details>

- **[HumanSightBench: A Benchmark for Human Visual Perception in MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12305)**  `arXiv:2611.12305`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Zixin Su, Yangguang Lin, Zegang Peng, Yuntong Sun, Xuanyi Wang, Jiahao Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) have demonstrated strong performance on knowledge-intensive and reasoning-based vision-language benchmark datasets, but still struggle to solve the visual questions that people can solve easily. Evaluating the differences between MLLMs and human-level visual perception is challenging due to end-to-end evaluation which combines various skills, or because we are uncertain if individual items are valid. Specifically, observed errors may reflect visual perception, language priors, background knowledge, question ambiguity, and underspecified answers. Our guideline for HumanSightBench is that we construct items that are human-easy by construction and model-hard by evaluation. HumanSightBench contains 2,876 visual questions with short answers across 7 top-level perceptual categories and 21 subcategories. We only include items that pass five inclusion gates: question clarity, answer uniqueness, visual grounding, human solvability, and text-only shortcut filtering. According to the protocol, errors are defined as incorrect answers on human-validated visual tasks with the evaluated interface. The inclusion gates are intended to reduce confounds from ambiguity and external knowledge. The best pass@3 score is 65.6% across eight evaluated MLLMs. No evaluated model exceeds 27.5% on Counting. These results identify capability gaps that are hidden by overall scores and can help future training experiments and held-out evaluation.
  </details>

- **[AURA 2.0: From User-Driven Responses to Model-Initiated Conversations in Streaming Video](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17048)**  `arXiv:2611.17048`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Shuhan Li, Huankang Guan, Xintong Guo, Yang Bo, Yuyang He, Dunyuan Xu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Most AI assistants respond only when prompted, remaining silent even when events in the video stream offer meaningful opportunities for conversation. To move beyond purely user-driven interaction, we present AURA 2.0, a streaming video large language model that introduces three core upgrades to AURA. The key advance is Model-Initiated Conversation (MIC), which enables the model to identify topics worth discussing in the evolving visual stream and initiate conversations at appropriate moments without a user query. Stream-Aware Tool Calling (SATC) enables the model to invoke external tools using the current visual and conversational context and incorporate returned information into the ongoing interaction. Interactive Visual Grounding (IVG) supports visual grounding in both user inputs and model outputs. The model can identify objects marked by users on video frames and produce frame-aligned bounding boxes to link its responses to specific objects and regions. To develop these capabilities, we construct a dedicated streaming interaction dataset and adopt a three-stage training scheme to integrate the new behaviors with AURA's existing streaming interaction abilities. We further incorporate Long-Horizon Interaction Memory (LHIM) to maintain contextual continuity over extended video streams. Evaluations on public streaming benchmarks show that AURA 2.0 achieves leading performance among open-source streaming models, while targeted evaluations further validate its new interaction capabilities. We open-source the model weights and inference code.
  </details>

- **[Evaluating Video LLMs' Understanding of Corner Cases in Autonomous Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16767)**  `arXiv:2611.16767`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Gabriel Manalu, Minh Son Hoang, Hyeontaek Hwang, Jaesung Lee, Hyojin Choi, Tran Tuan Phong, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Natural language explanations promised by modern Video Large Language Models (Video LLMs) offer autonomous driving systems a path toward detailed scene description and interpretable decision making on the road. While recent benchmarks have begun assessing these models on driving scenes, they mainly target routine traffic conditions and general comprehension tasks based on existing driving datasets and therefore fail to evaluate model performance on the rarest and most safety-critical driving events, also known as "corner cases". To provide a comprehensive evaluation of Video LLMs on precisely these scenarios, we introduce DashBench, a benchmark testing 25 Video LLMs on 1,709 carefully curated driving videos spanning not only collision-related corner cases but also rare non-collision ones. Through extensive prompts and open-ended question answering, we expose a critical capability gap: all evaluated models perform substantially below the threshold required for safety-critical driving applications, frequently misjudging or missing dangerous events on the road ahead and hallucinating non-existent agents or actions in their explanations. We believe DashBench, combining an extensive dataset with a rigorous evaluation toolkit and a detailed diagnosis of current model limitations, drives progress toward safer, more explainable autonomous driving systems. The code and data will be shared at:this http URL
  </details>

- **[PhysLoop: Harnessing LLMs' Physics Awareness for Self-Improving Video Generation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16748)**  `arXiv:2611.16748`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Yuxuan Chen, Xiaojie Li, Hao Chen, Juncheng Ma, Kewei Zhang, Zhongjing Du, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Video generation models produce visually realistic videos but still struggle with physical dynamics and causal consistency. Learning from generated videos requires fine-grained physical supervision, yet reliable quality judgments, correction pairs, and controlled variations are difficult to obtain. Large language models (LLMs) possess physical knowledge, but struggle to reliably guide video generation models in producing their training videos. We present PhysLoop, a unified harness designed to support physically grounded self-improvement in video generation models. The harness guides and constrains LLMs throughout video generation, selection, and correction, distilling their physical knowledge into physically consistent videos. The framework supports controllable training-data construction and inference-time video repair. By identifying and correcting failures, PhysLoop marks a key milestone toward recursive self-improvement (RSI) in video generation models with limited human intervention. We release 100K videos spanning diverse, realistic indoor and outdoor scenes, along with generation tools and two evaluation datasets: PhysLoop-Gen and PhysLoop-Repair. We evaluate controllable generation and localized repair on these datasets, respectively, and assess physical behavior on the external Physics-IQ Verified benchmark.
  </details>

- **[TransXplainRRG: A Radiologist-Evaluated LVLM for Chest Radiograph Report Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16393)**  `arXiv:2611.16393`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Chayan Mondal, Duc-Son Pham, Tele Tan, Ashu Gupta, Vincent T.Y. Tiong, Shreya Ghosh, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent advancements in Large Vision-Language Models (LVLM) enhance X-ray report generation, but often neglect clinical context, overlook workflow integration, and lack explainability, which hinders clinical adoption due to interpretability and reliability concerns. This paper addresses the dual challenges of performance and expert explainability in automated X-ray report generation for clinical AI applications. Our proposed approach, TransXplainRRG, utilises an off-the-shelf Swin Transformer model along with a transformer-based text encoder that incorporates patient medical history to generate a radiology report. Further, we explore an expert-guided `inside-out' approach and extract only abnormal findings for radiology report refinement. Thus, this study bridges the gap between high-performance automation and interpretability critical for clinical practice by combining state-of-the-art transformer-based vision encoders, text encoders, and LLMs. TransXplainRRG is trained and tested on the large-scale MIMIC-CXR dataset and further evaluated on the unseen IU X-Ray dataset to demonstrate its generalizability and robustness. We employed the GREEN metric as a complementary measure of clinical consistency, achieving scores of 0.327 on MIMIC-CXR and 0.605 on IU X-Ray, both higher than those achieved by MAIRA-2 under the same evaluation setting. It also performed competitively across standard evaluation metrics, frequently matching or surpassing state-of-the-art models. Additionally, we introduce qualitative evaluation metrics developed from radiologists' viewpoints and conduct a preliminary radiologist evaluation on 100 generated reports to assess their clinical relevance. Quantitative and qualitative analyses further demonstrate the potential of our TransXplainRRG framework for reliable and explainable radiology report generation.
  </details>

- **[Reading the Unreadable: Text-Aware Image Super-Resolution Needs Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15449)**  `arXiv:2611.15449`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Jaeseong Lee, Jinwoo Kim, Jinho Jeong, Seon Joo Kim_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Text-aware image super-resolution (TAISR) aims to recover high-resolution images from low-resolution inputs while preserving textual content. Existing methods treat this as a visual restoration problem, yet severely degraded text often cannot be read from local visual evidence alone. Humans instead read it by reasoning over image context, logical patterns, and prior knowledge. This raises a fundamental question: Does TAISR need reasoning? To answer this, we introduce two diagnostic tools. The first, ReasonText, is a human-curated benchmark that separates reasoning-required text from locally recoverable text. The second, Ground-Truth Text-Crop Accuracy (GTTCA), is a recognition-based metric that measures text restoration quality without text detection errors. Evaluating twelve recent super-resolution (SR) models, we find a consistent and substantial performance drop on reasoning-required text, revealing a systematic blind spot. Two observations point to a remedy. One is that text-conditioned SR models restore text substantially better when given ground-truth text. The other is that multimodal large language models (MLLMs) can read degraded text in low-resolution images through reasoning. We therefore propose Reasoning Transfer via Captioning (RTC), a training-free plug-in that uses an external MLLM to infer the degraded text and feeds the resulting caption to a text-conditioned SR model. RTC consistently improves all six text-conditioned SR models we evaluate, especially on reasoning-required text. Surprisingly, a real-world SR model with RTC can even outperform the TAISR model with RTC, suggesting that reasoning should be a core design axis for future TAISR.
  </details>

- **[AgroOmni: A Large-Scale Multi-view Agricultural Dataset for Cross-Scale Multimodal Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.14342)**  `arXiv:2603.14342`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Jiarui Zhang, Junqi Hu, Zurong Mai, Yang Liu, Yuhang Chen, Shuohong Lou, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Modern agricultural data is sourced from diverse platforms and spans multiple spatial scales, ranging from ground-level close-up photography to Unmanned Aerial Vehicle (UAV) aerial observation and satellite remote sensing imagery. Accordingly, agricultural multimodal reasoning demands robust cross-scale spatial understanding. However, due to the lack of multi-view agricultural benchmark datasets, existing multimodal large language models (MLLMs) exhibit severe ground-level bias, which leads to scale confusion then semantic collapse in agricultural perception tasks, such as misinterpreting farmland imagery as walls or floors. To address this, we introduce AgroOmni, a large-scale multi-view training corpus with 268,119 Visual Question Answering pairs covering 56 specialized task categories across 14 task types, designed to capture diverse scales in modern precisionthis http URLon this dataset, we propose AgroNVILA, which achieves a new state-of-the-art of 59.86% on the AgroMind benchmark (+12.57 over GPT-5.2), effectively mitigating the multi-view cross-scale gap for holistic agricultural understanding. Diagnostic evaluations on AgMMU further reveal an inherent heterogeneity between macro-priors and micro-diagnostics through constrained zero-shot performance. Meanwhile, even minimal fine-tuning leads to a dramatic performance gain of AgroNVILA on AgMMU, strongly demonstrating its generalization capability empowered by AgroOmni. Full training scripts are publicly available atthis https URL, and the AgroOmni dataset is released atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Towards Accurate UAV Image Perception: Guiding Vision-Language Models with Stronger Task Prompts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.07302)**  `arXiv:2512.07302`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Mingning Guo, Mengwei Wu, Shaoxian Li, Haifeng Li, Chao Tao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  UAV image perception with VLMs aims to identify and localize targets based on semantic and spatial task prompts. However, small, densely distributed objects and cluttered backgrounds hinder these tasks, particularly when concise prompts provide insufficient guidance. Although task-specific fine-tuning improves image-text alignment, it requires large-scale annotations and model-specific updates, increasing adaptation costs across models and tasks. To address this, we propose AerialVP, a task-adaptive, training-free agent that adapts VLMs through prompt enhancement rather than parameter optimization. AerialVP dynamically adapts its enhancement strategy to the original prompt's specificity, selectively incorporating image-derived semantics, spatial positions, and object relationships. These cues provide discriminative descriptions and spatial constraints, leveraging VLMs' pretrained alignment and reasoning capabilities to improve image-text alignment and reduce target ambiguity. Specifically, an LLM-based Task Engine coordinates modular tools for prompt analysis, visual information extraction, and prompt fusion. The enhanced prompt and original image are then provided to the unchanged backend VLM for prediction. This prompt-centric strategy enables training-free adaptation across diverse open-source and proprietary VLMs. For systematic evaluation, we introduce AerialSense, a unified benchmark covering Aerial Visual Grounding, Aerial Visual Reasoning, and Aerial Visual Question Answering across diverse UAV scenes and imaging conditions. Experiments demonstrate consistent improvements across all three tasks and evaluated VLMs. AerialVP also achieves competitive performance against LoRA-based instruction fine-tuning, validating prompt-centric adaptation. Our work is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Ropedia Xperience 2: A Gold Standard for Scaling Physical AI Environments and Behaviors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17060)**  `arXiv:2611.17060`  `cs.CV`  
  _Hao Li, Fangzhou Hong, Zhaoxi Chen, Hui En Pang, Kairui Hu, Mingze Sun, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large foundation models such as GPT-6 Astra and Claude Fable 5.1 have recently been shown to control robots directly from visual observations. They nevertheless remain brittle on long-horizon, contact-rich tasks in real-world environments. Further progress depends on two complementary resources: demonstrations of human behavior that supervise how tasks are performed, and interactive environments in which policies can be rolled out, evaluated, and improved in closed loop. Language models have advanced along well-characterized scaling laws; the corresponding question for physical AI is how capability scales with environments and behaviors, which first requires identifying which data to scale and how to produce and reuse such data continuously. To address this question, we introduce Ropedia Xperience 2, comprising 1,589 released metric-scale 3D scans and a collection of 4,230 egocentric recordings of 74 tasks (131 hours). We further show that each capture can be repurposed for robot learning: scenes are converted into physically interactive simulation environments, human motion is retargeted to whole-body robot trajectories, and additional robot behaviors are obtained through virtual teleoperation and automated long-horizon trajectory generation. We propose this joint capture of environments and behaviors as a gold standard for physical AI data collection, laying a data foundation for establishing scaling laws over environments and behaviors.
  </details>

- **[Visual Cipher Bench: Benchmarking LLM Agents on Long-Horizon Visual Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16861)**  `arXiv:2611.16861`  `cs.CV`  
  _Deyao Hong, Jiayi Jiang, Yuchun Chen, Yixuan Shi, Wenyi Li, Zheng Jiang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  While language-model agents excel at long-horizon coding and terminal tasks, this success relies on two conditions: explicit text specifications and immediate execution feedback. However, neither condition holds in real-world problems where critical information exists only in images, leaving it unclear whether long-horizon capability transfers from text to visual reasoning. In such visual settings, because rules and clues resist text transcription and lack test-case feedback, agents must repeatedly inspect images, simulate spatial transformations, and autonomously cross-check clues. Despite this challenge, existing evaluations fail to capture such capability: visual reasoning benchmarks largely reduce tasks to single-turn, short-chain question answering, whereas agent benchmarks focus on text-based environments or interactive states that permit trial-and-error. To bridge this gap, we introduce Visual Cipher Bench, a collection of 40 original visual puzzles across four categories: spatial manipulation and simulation, logical constraints and planning, rule induction, and knowledge-based decoding. Each puzzle provides only one to three black-and-white line drawings and a minimal submission instruction, requiring agents to discover the underlying solving mechanism entirely from the images. Across 29 reasoning-effort configurations of eight frontier models, even the strongest model, GPT-6 Astra (max), achieves only 26.7% accuracy, while five of the eight models score below 1%. Yet on a ten-task subset, providing a single non-spoiler mechanism hint boosts accuracy from 7.8% to 55.6%. This sharp contrast shows that the primary bottleneck of current agents lies not in executing multi-step plans, but in autonomously discovering rules from visual evidence. Visual Cipher Bench thus provides a rigorous benchmark for measuring long-horizon visual reasoning in agents.
  </details>

- **[Teach with Blinks: Breaking Linguistic Momentum in On-Policy Distillation for Fine-Grained Multimodal Perception](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16402)**  `arXiv:2611.16402`  `cs.CV`  
  _Tangzheng Lian, Hang Zhang, Guanyu Hu, Renjiu Hu, Zhensong Zhang, Jiankang Deng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  On-policy distillation (OPD) has emerged as an effective post-training approach to improving fine-grained perception in multimodal large language models by amortizing costly inference-time zooming operations into a single-pass recipe. However, vanilla OPD is inherently susceptible to linguistic momentum: the teacher can follow the student's erroneous prefixes, thus weakening corrective supervision. To address this, we propose Blink On-Policy Distillation (BlinkOPD), in which the teacher "blinks" at every token: it reads the same prefix once with its eyes open and once with its eyes closed. A first-glance gain further strengthens the correction on rollouts that conflict with the teacher. By extracting more effective supervision from each on-policy trajectory, BlinkOPD achieves state-of-the-art average performance across six fine-grained benchmarks using only 500 training images (12x less data) than existing OPD methods. Beyond fine-grained perception, BlinkOPD also demonstrates strong zero-shot improvements across ten held-out benchmarks spanning general visual perception, visual hallucination, and multimodal reasoning abilities. Code:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Sub-actions in Action: Text-Guided Hand-Role Alignment for Sub-Action Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16056)**  `arXiv:2611.16056`  `cs.CV`  
  _Sadegh Rahmaniboldaji, Filip Rybansky, Quoc Vuong, Frank Guerin, Andrew Gilbert_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Our objective is to enhance VLMs ability to distinguish the roles of the left and right hands in egocentric actions. We introduce sub-action detection, a task that requires models to explicitly recognise the left- and right-hand roles and their manipulated objects within an ongoing high-level action. To address this challenge, we propose BiScopeNet, a framework that augments existing video-LLMs without modifying their vision encoders or language models. BiScopeNet incorporates two components: FocusAlign, a gated text-guided attention-based module that selectively aligns video features with textual context to emphasise action-relevant cues, and MetaLoss, a masked, object-weighted loss that prioritises visually grounded terms. To supervise sub-action detection, we introduce BiScopeCap-100K, a large-scale video-level dataset curated from existing egocentric datasets, providing dense captions that jointly describe high-level actions, left/right-hand roles, and manipulated objects, enriched with spatial metadata for grounding. Across the evaluated Video-LLM backbones, BiScopeNet improves action, hand-role, and object recognition over the corresponding supervised baselines, achieving the strongest aggregate performance among the evaluated configurations on BiScopeCap.
  </details>

- **[Lang-SVG: Hierarchical Image Vectorization with Language Priors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15941)**  `arXiv:2611.15941`  `cs.CV`  
  _Xi Liu, Chaoyi Zhou, Run Wang, Jiaang Li, Feng Luo, Junxiang Huang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Image vectorization reconstructs raster images as compact scalable vector graph- ics (SVG), providing a structured and editable code representation of images. However, most existing vectorization methods optimize for pixel-level render- ing fidelity, often producing SVGs that lack alignment with human-perceived object-part hierarchies, making them difficult to manipulate. To address this, we propose Lang-SVG, which introduces an explicit tree structure to organize SVG primitives with semantic labels and parent-child relationships. Lang-SVG incorporates granularity-controllable and open-vocabulary segmentation priors to initialize object-part hierarchies and leverages multimodal large language models (MLLMs) with SVG tree context to assign semantic labels to unlabeled nodes. To reduce redundancy, we introduce depth-adaptive simplification to reduce shape complexity and Tree-Guided Pruning and Merging to eliminate redundant SVG nodes. L1-SAM-Prompting further recovers missing regions to improve recon- struction fidelity and structural completeness. Beyond reconstruction fidelity, we introduce a new evaluation protocol that measures structural quality and semantic part alignment. Experiments demonstrate that Lang-SVG achieves state-of-the-art performance in both rendering fidelity and structural quality. Project website:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[How Far Can Frozen Vision-Language Models Go on Event Data? A Training-Free Study on EventBench](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15756)**  `arXiv:2611.15756`  `cs.CV`  
  _Md Kamrujjaman Mobin, Md Salman Shamil, Md Adnan Arefeen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
