  We present a training-free system for the EBMV @ ECCV 2026 challenge "Event Stream Understanding with EventBench" (General Understanding track), which evaluates vision-language models on five event-camera understanding tasks. We use none of the challenge's training data: our goal is to measure how far off-the-shelf, RGB-pretrained models can go on event data through representation and prompting alone. For four of the five categories (gesture recognition, detailed understanding, causal reasoning, and event OCR) we render event bins as red/ON, blue/OFF frames, select a category-specific subset of bins, stitch them into a single grid image, and query the same frozen Qwen3.5 9B vision-language model with a category-specific prompt for all four tasks. The fifth category, action recognition, feeds every bin directly into the organizers' EventGPT-Plus-2B. The system reaches 62.28% overall accuracy across 2,084 test questions (rank 9/27), led by action recognition and causal reasoning (80.27%, 77.22%) and limited most by gesture recognition and event OCR (54.70%, 34.57%). We visualize test samples from every category together with the exact images the model receives, score six alternative representations against the evaluation server, and analyze the full test set post hoc. That analysis shows that our bounding-box crop almost never tightened around content, so event OCR text reached the model at roughly one fifth of its horizontal resolution, and that detailed-understanding predictions are skewed toward the first two answer options.
  </details>

- **[Semantic Consistency of Vision Tokens: A Vision-Centric Perspective on Multimodal Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15448)**  `arXiv:2611.15448`  `cs.CV`  
  _Nikola Đukić, Tim Lebailly, Tinne Tuytelaars_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In computer vision, vision-language and self-supervised encoders are two unique paradigms characterized by global language alignment and high-quality vision-only patch representations, respectively. Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) often use the former because of language alignment, despite the recent rise in vision-centric tasks resembling traditional dense vision tasks that would likely benefit from the high-quality patch representations, raising the question whether self-supervised models could aid MLLMs in vision-centric tasks. To investigate this, we define semantic (in)consistency of patch tokens from language-aligned encoders and identify two sets of tokens, semantically consistent (SCTs) and semantically inconsistent (SITs). Through systematic probing via the removal of SCTs or SITs from the input of modern MLLMs, we show higher reliance on SCTs than SITs, as removal of the former induces larger performance drops. This demonstrates that MLLMs benefit from semantic consistency of vision tokens. With this in mind, we devise a simple yet effective extension of the MLLM training objective that combines traditional language modeling with semantic guidance via minimization of semantic inconsistency. This strategy, named Semantically-Guided Training (SGT), combines the signals of both loss terms to push the vision encoder towards providing semantically consistent tokens, while preserving language alignment. Extensive evaluation shows that SGT outperforms all baselines and MLLMs with specialized vision encoders on vision-centric benchmarks while keeping strong scores on text-centric benchmarks, thus setting state of the art on average across all tasks. Our results highlight the importance of combining language alignment and semantic consistency through the lens of MLLMs rather than independently from them and pave the way towards the design of better MLLM vision encoders.
  </details>

- **[CaseScope: A Multi-Field Benchmark for Case-Level Pathology Diagnosis with Multimodal LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15325)**  `arXiv:2611.15325`  `cs.CV`  
  _Wenchuan Zhang, Chengyang Zhang, Fengling Li, Ting Yin, Chen Shu, Jie Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Pathologists rarely diagnose from a single image: they scan a section at low power for architecture, zoom into selected fields for cytology, and integrate the evidence across views and scales. Pathology benchmarks for multimodal large language models (MLLMs), however, mostly ask about one image or require whole-slide pipelines, and the few that show several fields draw nested regions from cancer slides of one public cohort. We introduce CaseScope, a benchmark of diagnosis from several curated fields of one lesion: CaseScope-Teaching, 436 cases from a residency-training image library, reconstructed, de-identified, harmonised against a WHO-based ontology and re-examined by a pathologist wherever an automated pre-review raised a doubt, and CaseScope-Atlas, 800 cases from 88 published atlases (387 of them figure series of one disease), labelled from the captions by two LLMs that must agree. They cover 843 entities across 18 organ systems and pose 4,679 items in six tasks, from open-ended and ten-way diagnosis with ontology-guided hard negatives under circular evaluation to the verification and location of morphological findings. Across twenty-eight open and four proprietary MLLMs, diagnoses are unstable -- the best open and proprietary models are correct under all ten option rotations on 17.9% and 61.7% of the teaching cases -- and wrong answers favour the classic look-alike; open models make little use of the additional fields on teaching cases, where the one-line specimen site matters more, although on atlas cases with three or more fields the fields count about as much as the site; and most open models verify findings barely above chance (median 62.0% vs. 50%, best 81.5%), gaining a median +3.4 points from images; proprietary models reach 83.2-93.2%. Rankings broadly replicate between the sources. The evaluation code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[CineMME: From Dialogue Grounding to Narrative Reasoning in Multimodal Video Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15161)**  `arXiv:2611.15161`  `cs.CV`  
  _Jin Liu, Dawei Du, Yexiang Liu, Sijie Zhu, Fan Chen, Junxian Duan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Narrative video understanding requires more than recognizing visible events: models must extract evidence from dialogue, bind it to speakers, and use it to infer narrative meaning. Existing video benchmarks evaluate aspects of this problem, including video question answering, audio-visual reasoning, and temporal grounding, but rarely test whether these abilities form a coherent dialogue-to-plot evidence chain within the same clips. We introduce \textbf{CineMME}, a human-verified benchmark designed to diagnose dialogue-grounded plot understanding, supported by approximately $1,600$ expert person-hours of annotation. CineMME contains $975$ curated short narrative video clips and two linked tracks: \textit{CineMME-Grounding}, with $13,554$ timestamped speech segments and $33,083$ speaker face boxes for temporal dialogue localization, transcription, and active-speaker grounding; and \textit{CineMME-Reasoning}, with $1,600$ multiple-choice and $316$ open-ended questions spanning perception, audio, narrative, social, and scene-presentation understanding. Our main evaluation compares $20$ MLLMs on reasoning and $9$ omni-modal models on grounding. The results reveal a pronounced who-what-when decoupling: Gemini-3 Pro localizes dialogue in time ($68.34\%$ tIoU) and transcribes it with a WER of $28.99\%$, yet achieves only $7.74\%$ vIoU for active-speaker grounding while achieving $67.66\%$ MCQ accuracy on questions with extractable answers. Modality ablations further show that vision is not uniformly beneficial: it improves performance on perception-heavy questions but can hurt dialogue-centric reasoning. CineMME provides a compact diagnostic testbed for evaluating how multimodal video models connect dialogue, speakers, visual evidence, and narrative meaning.
  </details>

- **[Keep Images in Sight: Visual Position Re-Anchoring for Long-Context MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15100)**  `arXiv:2611.15100`  `cs.CV`  
  _Juntao Xu, Qichai Zhao, Rui Sun, Zhouyang Jiang, Shiwei Wu, Zhi Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-context multimodal intelligence requires revisiting earlier images as conversations and documents accumulate, yet retaining an image does not guarantee reliable access to its details. By varying context length, redistributing a fixed text passage around the image, and changing positions without adding text, we identify the positional relation between images and questions as a direct influence on visual access. To address this gap, we propose Visual Position Re-Anchoring (VPR), a training-free method that shifts cached visual-key rotary phases toward a short-context reference before question processing. It operates directly on the existing KV cache, preserving standard attention computation without extracting attention maps or adding model forward passes. We develop static and continuous compensation schedules, using the static schedule by default. Across ten checkpoints and ten benchmarks, static VPR improves 97 of 100 model--task scores. Further analysis shows greater attention to regions containing answer evidence across six checkpoints, with minimal runtime overhead under fixed rotary frequencies. These findings demonstrate an efficient way to sustain visual access through positional adjustment within the cache.
  </details>

- **[DiFiT: Distribution-Fidelity-guided Visual Token Pruning for Multimodal Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15069)**  `arXiv:2611.15069`  `cs.CV`  
  _Jingyuan Zhang, Nihao Niu, Xinyang Lv, Beibei Han, Xiaoyun Wang, Yanning Hou, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Visual token pruning reduces the inference cost of multimodal large language models, but answer accuracy only partly reflects its effect. Pruning can change the probabilities of alternative response tokens even when greedy answers remain correct and unchanged. We compare full and pruned response distributions under shared reference prefixes. Average predictive drift grows with compression on fixed correctly answered samples. Separate analyses associate larger drift with more confidence-decision disagreements and sampling failures. We propose DiFiT (Distribution-Fidelity-guided Token pruning) to learn discrete selection from reference answers and full-token response distributions. Dual-View Distillation matches probabilities across the vocabulary, while answer supervision supports task quality. A learned scorer and differentiable Top-$K$ learn which visual evidence to retain. A multi-budget mask objective supports different retention ratios through one shared ranking over a frozen model. On Qwen2.5-VL-7B, DiFiT reduces sampling-induced negative flips by 30.3% relative to FastV at 10% retention on four-task greedy-correct controls. It preserves 98.74% of baseline performance on MME at 30% retention.
  </details>

- **[RSLiveBench-UAV: A Continually Evolving Vision-Language Benchmark for Aerial Spatial Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14953)**  `arXiv:2611.14953`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yue Zhou, Jiawei Liu, Jue Chen, Ziyang Gong, Haojia Wei, Jingxiang Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Aerial spatial intelligence (ASI) requires models to understand objects and spatial relationships across changing viewpoints and scales. Evaluating these abilities as models evolve calls for fresh physical observations, yet producing new aerial spatial questions requires repeatedly establishing entity identities, geometric references, and cross-view correspondences. We introduce \textbf{RSLiveBench-UAV}, a continually evolving vision-language benchmark driven by recurring acquisition of real UAV imagery. Its update protocol admits images captured after the preceding release and delays answer disclosure. An entity-centered production workflow uses geometry-assisted association to link repeated observations of physical vehicles, reuse human-verified attributes, and combine them with view-specific annotations and spatial references for traceable question generation. All tasks use deterministic scoring without model judges. We evaluate 30 general-purpose, remote-sensing, and embodied models on 35,491 question records from ten scenes, covering 17 tasks across 2D perception, 3D perception, cross-view perception, and reasoning. All 18 tested embodied models outperform the three selected remote-sensing comparators in Overall, although the strongest embodied model scores 43.29 versus 59.88 for GPT-6-Astra. Fine-grained vehicle recognition remains challenging, and performance varies across heights and camera pitches. Retrospective scene-group comparisons and rolling-window replay characterize score-pattern consistency under scene renewal. RSLiveBench-UAV connects fresh physical observations, entity-centered answer production, and systematic model evaluation to support continual assessment of ASI. Benchmark will release atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[From Classification to Generation: Vision-Language Models for Roman Imperial Coin Description](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14821)**  `arXiv:2611.14821`  `cs.CV`  
  _Pedro Lourenco, Ognjen Arandjelović_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Cataloguing a Roman Imperial coin involves reading its legends and identifying its imagery in order to match it to an entry in the standard reference catalogue. Previous work has automated individual parts of this process, such as identifying the issuer or recognizing reverse motifs, by formulating each part as a classification task over a predefined set of labels. We instead formulate coin description as image-to-text generation and adapt an open-weight vision-language model (VLM), Qwen3-VL-8B-Instruct, to four tasks: transcribing the obverse and reverse legends, and describing the obverse and reverse imagery. To obtain training data, we convert approximately 30,000 auction records into structured image-text pairs. We separate the two sides of each coin, extract and normalize legends and descriptions, and standardize obverse descriptions against a bust-type taxonomy. We then train a separate low-rank adapter for each task on a single 20GB GPU, and evaluate each task with metrics that we define to capture the key components of numismatic descriptions. The adapted models outperform both the unadapted base model and GPT-4.1 on all four tasks, reducing the normalized edit distance of legend transcriptions by over 40% relative to GPT-4.1. The improvement is smaller, but still present, on legends absent from the training data.
  </details>

- **[WithEveryone: Unified Planning and Identity Grounding for Group Image Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20336)**  `arXiv:2608.20336`  `cs.CV`  
  _Hengyuan Xu, Qixun Wang, Yiji Cheng, Miles Yang, Zhao Zhong, Wei Cheng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Identity-preserving image generation becomes increasingly unreliable when a scene must contain many specified people. Beyond retaining each identity, the model must bind every reference to a distinct person and location, while training-time identity losses must establish correspondence among several noisy predicted faces. We introduce WithEveryone, a unified framework for generating group images of 5-10 reference identities. WithEveryone injects each selected identity as an addressed token, predicts a structured identity-layout plan, and renders the plan as a visual condition. Its key objective, Layout-Grounded ID Loss, uses annotated face regions to supervise the intended identities directly, avoiding unstable embedding-based face matching; ID Representation Forcing additionally trains a prediction for each identity before image synthesis. WithEveryone leads in identity similarity (0.538 versus GPT-Image-2.5's 0.472), with lower Copy-Paste (0.149 versus 0.197), 94.5% identity coverage, and 9.6% identity collisions. These results show that explicit identity-layout grounding enables identity-preserving generation to scale to larger groups without relying on direct reference-face copying.
  </details>

- **[Robust Image Emotion Classification via Multi-Level Affective Captioning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.23115)**  `arXiv:2511.23115`  `cs.CV`  
  _Zibo Zhou, Zhengjun Zhai, Huimin Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Image emotion classification (IEC) has been studied for many years and has recently attracted increasing attention with the rapid development of deep learning. Although recent studies have benefited from knowledge embedded in pre-trained visual models, the performance is still limited by the "affective gap" between low-level visual features and high-level affective semantics. Psychological studies suggest that language can convey fine-grained and emotion-rich information. Inspired by this observation, we attempt to narrow the "affective gap" by introducing affective text. Specifically, we design a multi-level contrastive loss to detect emotional concepts from images and introduce an emotional-attribute-guided large language model approach to generate affective sentences. Then, a pre-trained language model is employed to combine emotional concepts with affective sentences for IEC. In addition, we introduce a contrastive loss with a semantic-similarity-based sampling strategy to address large intra-class differences and limited inter-class differences in affective datasets. Moreover, unlike many previous studies, we explicitly consider images containing embedded text. Extensive experiments on multiple benchmarks show that the proposed method helps narrow the "affective gap" and achieves competitive performance.
  </details>

- **[Robust Reasoning Segmentation for Open-World MLLMs without Catastrophic Forgetting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.16785)**  `arXiv:2510.16785`  `cs.CV`  
  _Jiazhen Liu, Long Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Ensuring trustworthiness and versatility in Open-World Visual Recognition is a core goal of Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs). While integrating segmentation is desirable, prevailing methods require invasive fine-tuning, which often leads to catastrophic forgetting of the model's general-purpose capabilities. This trade-off between task-specific performance and general reliability fundamentally undermines the goal of building unified, trustworthy systems. We introduce LENS (Leveraging kEypoiNts for MLLMs' Segmentation), a novel plug-and-play paradigm designed to equip MLLMs with pixel-level ability without compromising their foundational integrity. LENS attaches a lightweight head to a completely frozen MLLM, interpreting the intrinsic spatial cues embedded in attention maps. It extracts keypoints and translates them into prompt-compatible features for a mask decoder. Extensive experiments validate that LENS achieves segmentation performance competitive with retraining-based methods. Crucially, it largely preserves the MLLM's open-world generalization, avoiding the degradation observed in fine-tuned counterparts. By decoupling segmentation from generation, LENS offers a robust and efficient path toward truly multi-talented and reliable vision models.
  </details>

- **[PhysPatch: A Physically Realizable and Transferable Adversarial Patch Attack for Multimodal Large Language Models-based Autonomous Driving Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.05167)**  `arXiv:2508.05167`  `cs.CV`  
  _Qi Guo, Xiaojun Jia, Shanmin Pang, Simeng Qin, Lin Wang, Ju Jia, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) are becoming integral to autonomous driving (AD) systems due to their strong vision-language reasoning capabilities. However, MLLMs are vulnerable to adversarial attacks, particularly adversarial patch attacks, which can pose serious threats in real-world scenarios. Existing patch-based attack methods are primarily designed for object detection models and perform poorly when transferred to MLLM-based systems due to the latter's complex architectures and reasoning abilities. To address these limitations, we propose PhysPatch, a physically realizable and transferable adversarial patch framework tailored for MLLM-based AD systems. PhysPatch jointly optimizes patch location, shape, and content to enhance attack effectiveness and real-world applicability. It introduces a semantic-based mask initialization strategy for realistic placement, an SVD-based local alignment loss with patch-guided crop-resize to improve transferability, and a potential field-based mask refinement method. Extensive experiments across open-source, commercial, and reasoning-capable MLLMs demonstrate that PhysPatch significantly outperforms prior methods in steering MLLM-based AD systems toward target-aligned perception and planning outputs. Moreover, PhysPatch consistently places adversarial patches in physically feasible regions of AD scenes, ensuring strong real-world applicability and deployability.
  </details>

- **[ArchSpace: Recording, Reviewing, and Referencing LLM Architecture Research](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17332)**  `arXiv:2611.17332`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Jie Wang, Tong Zhu, JiaHao Kuang, Zhe Xu, Yunkai Chai, Dehong Zheng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Model architecture shapes the scaling behavior, training efficiency, and inference cost of LLMs, making it a central focus of both industry and academia. In industry, teams such as those behind DeepSeek, Qwen, and Kimi routinely introduce architectural innovations, yet public reports rarely document which ideas succeeded, which were abandoned, and why. In academia, limited compute and inconsistent baselines often restrict evaluations to small-scale, single-point comparisons, making it difficult to establish whether improvements hold at larger scales or translate into broader impact. ArchSpace addresses both gaps as a public platform for Recording, Reviewing, and Referencing Architecture Researc}: each proposal is recorded with its experiments and reasoning, reviewed by the community, and referenced by later work. To evaluate ideas before costly large-scale training, ArchSpace leverages scaling laws to predict model loss at larger scales from smaller experiments, a practice widely adopted in industry. We provide initial references that make the platform usable: Qwen3.5 and OLMo3 baselines, fitted scaling laws, and a reusable training recipe. Ideas validated through this review can in turn become new baselines, and ArchSpace thereby aims to broaden the range of ideas that shape the next generation of models.
  </details>

- **[Evaluating Pretrained Language Semantics for Structured EHR Prediction Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17053)**  `arXiv:2611.17053`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Ashvin Gupta, Brendan Delaney, Alessandra Russo_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Structured Electronic Health Records (EHR) models typically treat each clinical concept as a dataset-specific token, discarding the natural-language semantics associated with the event. As a result, relationships between concepts must largely be learned from EHR co-occurrence, without directly exploiting any prior semantic knowledge. We investigate how pretrained causal language models can be adapted for longitudinal EHR prediction by developing tEHR. Here, we represent events with their natural language description and use this text stream to apply EHR-adaptive continued pretraining, followed by task-specific discriminative fine-tuning using LoRA, utilising the LLM as a longitudinal patient-encoder. We evaluate this approach across four prediction tasks on the MIMIC-IV dataset and pancreatic cancer prediction on the CPRD dataset at 1 to 12 month horizons, with tEHR using the Mistral-7B-v0.3 backbone achieving the strongest overall performance. Ablations show that performance declines when clinical descriptions are removed or misaligned, when numerical values are omitted, and when either stage of adaptation is removed, while explicit elapsed-time descriptions provide little additional benefit. Analysis of the learned patient representations further reveals clinically coherent structure associated with distinct patterns of disease risk and clinical history.
  </details>

- **[Human or Synthetic? What a Fixed Budget Buys for Post-Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16895)**  `arXiv:2611.16895`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Shourik Banerjee, Ethan Mendes, Mohit Raghavendra, Wei Xu, Alan Ritter_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Post-training of large language models typically combines supervised fine-tuning (SFT) with preference-based fine-tuning such as Direct Preference Optimization (DPO). Human-annotated and synthetically generated preference data differ substantially in per-sample cost, but prior work typically holds sample count fixed when comparing the two. In this work, we explicitly study the interplay between cost and performance by comparing the effect of post-training with human and synthetic preference data under a fixed, dollar-matched annotation budget. We train and evaluate multiple base model families across helpfulness and summarization tasks. We find that synthetic data is far more cost-efficient at low-to-moderate budgets, achieving high win rates with as little as \$200--\$400. On helpfulness, human data closes the gap only at budgets roughly two orders of magnitude larger; on summarization, it does not close the gap at any tested budget. We also find that human-annotated data varies in reliability: one widely used human preference source substantially underperforms another, and the gap persists even after standard remedies such as domain restriction and deduplication.
  </details>

- **[ShadowBench: Exposing Lexical Anchoring and the Illusion of Forgetting in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15925)**  `arXiv:2611.15925`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Sujan Maharjan, Lu Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Models (LLMs) have emerged as primary interfaces for factual retrieval, yet current evaluation paradigms rely almost exclusively on explicit entity names. In this work, we demonstrate that LLM knowledge is fundamentally lexically anchored: models possess extensive factual information but find it significantly difficult to retrieve without explicit name tokens. To quantify this limitation, we introduce ShadowBench, a rigorously hardened, shortcut-resistant benchmark designed to evaluate Latent Entity Association via a novel Dual-Trait Association (DTA) task. Our evaluation reveals a pervasive "Shadow Gap" across all model scales$\unicode{x2013}$up to the frontier models GPT-5.4 and Claude-Sonnet-4.6$\unicode{x2013}$where removing lexical anchors causes performance drops of over 20%. Furthermore, we demonstrate that this lexical dependency exposes a critical vulnerability in AI safety. Applying ShadowBench to Machine Unlearning, we find that state-of-the-art algorithms (e.g., Gradient Difference, NPO) achieve only superficial lexical erasure. While unlearned models fail on direct queries, our novel Latent Entity Leakage Rate (LELR) metric reveals that for popular entities, models explicitly reconstruct the "forgotten" entity in their internal reasoning traces in over 74% of cases, utilizing residual shadow knowledge to solve associative tasks. Conversely, for niche entities, models retain high shadow accuracy (60-90%) via implicit attribute correlation despite 0% LELR. Ultimately, ShadowBench indicates that current unlearning paradigms create an "Illusion of Forgetting," demonstrating that existing methods act as superficial output filters and are not yet reliable for achieving true parametric erasure.
  </details>

- **[One Byte, One Token: Byteifying a 27B Hybrid-Attention LLM with Self-Generated Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13607)**  `arXiv:2611.13607`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Liwei Zhang, ZhiMing Tong, Mochen Guan, Linzhe Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Byte-level BPE fragments Chinese text (for Qwen3.8 on Chinese Wikipedia, 3.84 bytes per token on average; 64% of tokens are single characters) and hides spelling from the model. We ask whether an already-trained 27B subword model can be converted directly into a pure byte-level model (one byte, one token), without a patching structure or a local encoder, while keeping most of its ability. Starting from Qwen3.8-27B (48 Gated DeltaNet layers + 16 full-attention layers), we shrink the vocabulary from 248,320 to 384 entries (the 256 byte symbols already in the BPE vocabulary plus special ids), leave every other weight untouched, and continue training in a two-stage curriculum (encoding first, then reading) on 4.77 GB of supervised bytes: all dialogue/behavioral supervision is generated on-policy by the original model itself (character dictionary, word dictionary, general dialogue, coding sessions), and knowledge reading uses encyclopedia text (54% of supervised bytes). Training takes 178.6 h on 8xH200. On the same GPU and serving stack, the byte model's gap to the original falls within the paired bootstrap interval on MATH-500, HumanEval+, MMLU-Pro and non-thinking GSM8K, and it loses 4-6 points on C-Eval, thinking-mode GSM8K and non-thinking IFEval. Without thinking it leads on every character-level task (CUTE 75.2 -> 84.0, EXECUTE-zho 81.6 -> 93.9, our Chinese character suite 54.4 -> 64.0), but lookup knowledge about Chinese characters such as pronunciation and stroke count drops, and thinking does not recover it. The cost is throughput: per-step speed is identical, but each step yields 3-5x less output; Chinese characters per second is about 1/4 of the original, and prefill at 64K bytes is 6.5x slower. We also document four byte-model failure modes (greedy runaway, sampling breaking UTF-8, ineffective penalty terms on a small vocabulary, repetition) and their fixes.
  </details>

- **[TopicMerge: A Divide and Conquer Approach to Topic Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19323)**  `arXiv:2610.19323`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Matthew Raffel, Lizhong Chen, Sai Munikoti, Sameera Horawalavithana_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent advancements in topic modeling have split into two main paths: efficient embedding-based methods that offer scalability but are often outperformed on standard clustering metrics, and Large Language Model (LLM) approaches that achieve state-of-the-art clustering performance but at the cost of prohibitive computational expense and poor scalability. This paper introduces TopicMerge, a novel multi-stage pipeline that bridges these approaches to achieve both state-of-the-art clustering performance and computational efficiency. TopicMerge first employs an efficient clustering pipeline to deliberately over-segment a document corpus into a large set of fine-grained, semantically pure micro-clusters, which are then given initial semantic labels. It then introduces a novel, iterative merging algorithm where an LLM, guided by local neighborhood context, arbitrates on consolidating candidate pairs. These approved merges are then selectively executed and semantically relabeled by the LLM, hierarchically building broader, more coherent topics. Extensive experiments on the Bills and Wiki datasets show that TopicMerge achieves state-of-the-art clustering performance while reducing required LLM calls by over 92% compared to document-level methods, offering a practical and scalable solution.
  </details>

- **[Goal Encoding in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17297)**  `arXiv:2611.17297`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Arnab Sen Sharma, Eric Todd, Sheridan Feucht, Andy Arditi, Rohit Gandikota, Natalie Shapira, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We investigate whether and how LLMs encode goals: internal neural representations that precede and guide action, and that are flexible enough to be realized through many different execution paths. We construct a coding setting with multiple sub-tasks that allows us to evaluate goal-seeking behavior and the selection of sub-goals. In this setting, we localize an encoding of the next sub-goal in the outputs of a sparse set of attention heads. The same heads can be used to decode the LM's future actions in natural language (e.g., "I'm checking if a word is a palindrome"). We verify that this encoding is causal for next action and is plastic: transplanting it into a different environment, e.g., another programming language, triggers the same operation realized with a different sequence of tokens. The LM also sustains the encoding throughout the action's execution, which is puzzling because a transformer LM without recurrence cannot carry a state forward for more tokens than it has layers left. We propose that it does not need to. Under a non-recurrent goal-seeking (NRGS) account, the LM frequently re-reads the goal from the task specification, compares it with the progress visible in the growing context, and pursues what remains. We test the predictions of this account with targeted causal interventions.
  </details>

- **[What Roles Do System Prompts Really Assign? Validating and Steering Occupational Role Vectors in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15989)**  `arXiv:2611.15989`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Jose L. Sampedro Mazon, Ilmi Yoon, Nadim Saad_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Applications built on large language models assign the model a role through its system prompt, intending to shape how it responds for their use case. We introduce a validation framework for extracting and injecting occupational role vectors, activation-level directions that capture how someone in a given occupational role would distinctively respond to a task or question. We validate these vectors through two complementary lenses, on Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct and Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct. The interpretability lens asks whether each vector is a stable, separable, domain-consistent representation of its role: extracted vectors are highly reproducible across repeated runs and sampling temperatures, remain separable even for very similar roles, and agree more with themselves across in-domain and out-of-domain questions than with other roles' signal. The control lens asks whether injecting a role vector during generation makes the model express the role, and sustain it over a conversation, as well as a role prompt does. On Qwen, injecting the vector alone makes the model express the intended role substantially more often than unsteered, but at best matches a role prompt; on both models, adding the vector to the prompt strengthens the role in single answers but did not slow the role's decline over 30 turns. Together, the two lenses show why both are needed: out of domain the role is expressed only subtly, yet the activations still encode it; and a vector that is stable and separable can still barely steer the model's behavior.
  </details>

- **[Where Is Uncertainty Observed? A Mechanistic Understanding of Uncertainty Quantification in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15735)**  `arXiv:2611.15735`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Xiaoyu Liang, Virginia Ceccatelli, Aliai Eusebi, Tobias Braun, Nils Loose, Carlo Malagnino, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Knowing when a large language model's (LLM's) response can be trusted is critical for its safe use. Uncertainty quantification (UQ) can help LLMs recognize if their responses may be unreliable. Existing taxonomies organize UQ methods by uncertainty source or estimation mechanism. They leave unresolved the question of where uncertainty is observed in the model's computation. The answer matters because the observation point determines whether an estimator has access to enough information to recover a given target, and how well it can recover it. Drawing on mechanistic interpretability, we define four mechanistic observation lenses: the structural lens observes component updates, the representational lens observes hidden states, the distributional lens observes token probabilities, and the semantical lens observes distributions over meanings. We relate these lenses through the residual stream and order them by the information they carry, which gives a principle for selecting UQ methods for different targets. We survey existing methods through the four lenses. Furthermore, we distinguish evaluation targets, criteria, and metrics to clarify different UQ evaluation protocols. Finally, we identify open problems and research directions to connect uncertainty estimation to the internal computation and downstream use of LLMs.
  </details>

- **[AutoOntic: Automatic Ontology Induction from Text for Knowledge Graph Construction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15467)**  `arXiv:2611.15467`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Alla Chepurova, Aydar Bulatov, Mikhail Burtsev, Yuri Kuratov_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Graph-based retrieval-augmented generation depends on the quality of the knowledge graph (KG) it retrieves over, yet schema-free LLM-based extraction pipelines typically produce flat graphs: deduplicated entities and relations with no type hierarchy and no domain/range constraints. We present AutoOntic, a pipeline that automatically constructs an ontology from text by canonicalizing open-vocabulary entity types and relations, inducing a hierarchy over the canonicalized types, and mining relation-level domain and range constraints. Its hierarchy induction procedure prioritizes types using corpus-derived relation-argument profiles and places them through local, LLM-adjudicated parent/child checks, allowing newly inserted types to trigger reassignment of existing parent-child links. The resulting hierarchy constrains entity deduplication and supports the induction of relation-level domain/range constraints. For intrinsic evaluation, we introduce a reference-based protocol that classifies induced ontology types as exact, broader, narrower, or unrelated to reference domain and range classes. On the Wikidata-TekGen and DBpedia-WebNLG subsets of Text2KGBench, over 90% of AutoOntic's induced endpoint types are semantically aligned with the reference classes across two LLM backbones: about half match exactly, while most remaining differences concern granularity. On MINE, knowledge graphs organized using an ontology built by AutoOntic achieve 88% retrieval accuracy. AutoOntic is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Knowledge in the Harness, Reliability in the Weights: Teacher-Free Co-Evolution of an LLM Agent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15268)**  `arXiv:2611.15268`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Yujia Zhang, Xinqi Ren, Shixiong Zhou, Jia Meng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Small open models suit private customer-service deployments but trail frontier models in two ways: they miss what the written policy requires in a given situation, and they act inconsistently on what they know. We study whether such a model can close both gaps from its own deployment logs and their reference outcomes, without a stronger teacher model. Our starting point is a Gemma 4 12B agent that reads its domain policy from on-demand skills rather than from its system prompt. In each round, the deployed system serves a fresh batch of tasks; the base model diagnoses failed conversations and writes rules into the skills, and a rule survives only if it passes code checks, counterexample self-review, a paired retest, and a noise-calibrated validation gate. Reinforcement learning (GRPO) then trains a LoRA adapter on complete multi-turn, tool-using conversations. On the retail and airline domains of $\tau^2$-bench, without training or selecting on official tasks, the agent after two rounds improves on this starting point by 12.5 pass$^1$ points in retail and 19.5 in airline, and by 18.4 and 22.0 pass$^4$ points. It also exceeds the benchmark's reference agent, which runs the same model with the whole policy in its system prompt, by 11.8 and 8.0 pass$^1$ points and by 22.8 and 16.0 pass$^4$ points, closing most of the gap to three frontier models under the same protocol. A $2\times2$ ablation shows that the rules rescue tasks the agent never solved and carry policy knowledge that RL with the rules in context does not internalize, whereas the trained weights make solved tasks reliable.
  </details>

- **[An Experimental Study of Hybrid Retrieval, NLI and Open-source LLMs for Scientific Claim Verification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03093)**  `arXiv:2611.03093`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Lakshmi Narayanan P, Narenkarthik K, Naren Kumar C_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Claim verification is the task of deciding whether a source text supports a claim, contradicts it, or lacks sufficient evidence. Natural Language Inference (NLI) models can judge whether a single sentence supports or contradicts a claim, but they struggle when the evidence is spread across several sentences. Conversely, Large Language Models (LLMs) can reason across sentences, but may draw conclusions the text does not support. In this experimental study, we test whether a conservative fusion rule, labeling a claim Support or Contradict only when both models agree and defaulting to Not Enough Evidence otherwise, combines their strengths. We construct a benchmark of 2,035 claims from SciFact and CliniFact (858 Support, 152 Contradict, 1,025 Not Enough Evidence). We retrieve the top five most relevant sentences per claim using a hybrid of semantic and lexical (BM25) retrieval, which slightly outperforms either method alone. These retrieved sentences are then provided to every verification model. We then compare a DeBERTa-v3-base-mnli-fever-anli NLI baseline against Llama 3.1 8B, Qwen3 8B, and Mistral 7B. Qwen3 achieves the highest Macro-F1 (0.7937), followed by Llama 3.1 (0.7204) and Mistral (0.6388), while the NLI baseline reaches only 0.3157. Contrary to our hypothesis, fusion degrades performance, dropping Macro-F1 to 0.7392, 0.6730, and 0.5991, respectively. To understand this decline, we analyze agreements, disagreements, and common error patterns between the NLI and LLM verifiers. Our error analysis shows that NLI and LLM disagreements often result in correct LLM predictions being overturned.
  </details>

- **[Unintended Side Effects of Value Training in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29922)**  `arXiv:2610.29922`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Siddhesh Pawar, Chen Cecilia Liu, Anna Korhonen, Isabelle Augenstein_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As LLMs are increasingly deployed in value-sensitive contexts, aligning them with human values has emerged as a post-training objective in NLP, aimed at instilling additional values and guiding model behaviour. However, current evaluations remain narrow. Because values are interconnected, it is unclear how fine-tuning for specific values alters unrelated capabilities and behaviours. In this work, we adapt four instruction-tuned LLMs across individual Hofstede's value dimensions and evaluate them on both cross-value transfer and broader behaviours. We find that value-specific training produces unintended side effects on untrained values, risk preferences, social cognition, and safety traits. Consequently, we suggest that value alignment after post-training should incorporate broader behavioral evaluations, and we highlight the need to investigate where additional value training might best fit into the pipeline. We release all 48 value-tuned model checkpoints atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[Fast-dLLM++: Fréchet Profile Decoding for Faster Diffusion LLM Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.02955)**  `arXiv:2606.02955`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Siva Rajesh Kasa, Yasong Dai, Sumit Negi, Hongdong Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Diffusion large language models promise parallel token generation, yet inference remains bottlenecked by deciding which masked tokens can be safely committed together. Fast-dLLM addressed this with KV caching and confidence-guided parallel decoding, but its decoding theory uses a homogeneous high-confidence assumption that effectively reduces each candidate set to its weakest selected token. We argue that this leaves speed on the table because real decoding steps exhibit heterogeneous confidence profiles. We propose Fast-dLLM++, a training-free extension that introduces Fréchet profile decoding: selecting parallel commit sets from the full sorted confidence profile rather than a single worst-case confidence. The resulting rule is a heterogeneous-confidence generalization of Fast-dLLM's factor selector and it recovers the previous rule exactly in the equal-confidence case and adds a provable heterogeneity bonus when the selected tokens have uneven confidences. Fast-dLLM++ leaves the model, diffusion process, and cache implementation entirely unchanged, making it a drop-in replacement for existing Fast-dLLM decoding. Experiments on GSM8K, MATH, HumanEval, and MBPP with the LLaDA-8B model show that the theoretical improvement translates directly into empirical gains: profile-aware selection improves the accuracy-throughput frontier by exploiting safe parallelism that weakest-token rules miss, achieving up to 37% higher throughput at comparable accuracy. Our code release is atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Synthesis Smuggling: Maliciously Hiding Inferred Personal Attributes in LLM Outputs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17280)**  `arXiv:2611.17280`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Gautam Galada, Supreeth Shastri, Sagnik Ray Choudhury_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We propose Synthesis Smuggling, a novel threat model in which an adversarial LLM infers sensitive information about a user from routine assistance-seeking interactions and steganographically encodes that into innocuous-looking model-generated content. The user may then unknowingly transmit that information by sharing the generated artifact, say as an email, social-media post, or message, through ordinary communication channels. An adversary monitoring these channels can discover this information. We demonstrate this threat by training two open-weight models to infer gender and age-group labels from conversational cues and encode their predictions using invisible characters or word choices. These results show that user-shared model outputs can become a covert channel for personal inferences, even when the model itself has no direct means of contacting an adversary.
  </details>

- **[Discovering Effective Agent Memory Designs through Program Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17068)**  `arXiv:2611.17068`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Xinheng Ding, Seyyed Saeid Cheshmi, Ziyang Zhang, Tairan Chen, Wenya Xie, Yu Hou, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Program evolution provides a promising approach to discover agent memory designs and study the choices underlying their effectiveness. We introduce MAPE, a framework that evolves whole memory programs from minimal seeds through a standardized lifecycle interface and an iterative diagnosis-proposal loop. Experiments on LoCoMo and LongMemEval yield an absolute accuracy gain of up to 12% over manually designed baselines at 35% of the serving cost, with further evaluations supporting transfer across serving backbones and benchmarks. Empirical analyses and ablations identify and validate two recurring design patterns: retaining raw conversations as retrievable evidence preserves details omitted from extracted memories, while composing retrieved evidence according to query intent helps the answering model use it effectively. Transplanting the composition module into five existing memory systems improves accuracy across all five. Beyond these patterns, the search objective shapes where computation goes: optimizing serving cost alongside accuracy shifts computation from LLM-based memory construction to selective processing at query time while retaining competitive accuracy. Together, these findings offer empirical lessons for agent memory design. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[SPADE: Soft Prompts for Ai-text DEtection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16929)**  `arXiv:2611.16929`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Alex Jiang, Didier Schwab, Chuyuan Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The easy access to Large Language Models (LLMs) has made AI-generated texts on the web increasingly ubiquitous, making AI-text detection a crucial task. Prompts greatly influence the generation process, but existing detectors focus solely on the generated texts --- supervised methods learn from labeled texts only, and zero-shot methods rely on the text's probability distributions --- ignoring the underlying generation process, which limits their robustness in open-world scenarios. We introduce SPADE: Soft Prompts for Ai-text DEtection, a novel framework for detecting AI-generated text that transfers better across datasets. Specifically, we measure the prompt's influence and use it as a discriminative signal. We characterize the prompt's influence by introducing a Pointwise Mutual Information (PMI) term that captures how much a prompt explains a text. Based on this quantity, SPADE trains one soft prompt per class to amplify the separation, and predicts the class whose prompt best explains the candidate text. Extensive experiments on several benchmarks against various detectors demonstrate the robustness and effectiveness of our method, achieving state-of-the-art (SOTA) performance on out-of-distribution (OOD) datasets.
  </details>

- **[MuSeToM: Multi-Sensory Evaluation of Belief Revision with Textual Cues Across Six Senses](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16662)**  `arXiv:2611.16662`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Chunkit Chan, Kawai Chung, Sze Heng Douglas Kwok, Yuxuan Liu, Yauwai Yim, Tianshi Zheng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Theory of Mind (ToM), the ability to infer mental states in others, is pivotal for human social cognition. Existing evaluations of ToM in large language models (LLMs) mainly focus on static belief attribution and false-belief reasoning, leaving a critical aspect of social reasoning underexplored: can LLMs decide whether perceptual evidence is sufficient to justify another person's belief revision? To address this gap, we present MuSeToM, a human-validated, text-based benchmark for evaluating epistemic belief revision in ToM reasoning. MuSeToM contains 2,415 instances adapted from BigToM and OpenToM, with textual cues spanning six sensory categories, and tests whether models can determine when a protagonist should revise a belief and infer the resulting updated belief state. Using MuSeToM, we systematically evaluate state-of-the-art LLMs under direct and chain-of-thought prompting, revealing a pronounced dissonance: while models often detect that a belief may change, they struggle to identify the updated belief and frequently over-update on perceptually salient but epistemically insufficient cues.
  </details>

- **[Where in the Input Does a One-Pass Decision Encoder Look?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16477)**  `arXiv:2611.16477`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Mohammed Nayeem Khan, Allan Kevin T_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Laya is a small model that answers a multiple-choice question about a text in one pass: the question, the options (each with a name and a description) and the text form one sequence of at most 512 tokens, the window, in the English release. A longer text is silently cut at the end, and we measure what that costs. Test documents place a held-out row's text, the evidence, at a chosen token offset among distractor text; 177 items, five tasks, three checkpoints (released, short-row fine-tune, long-document fine-tune), four window sizes and 93,275 scored requests. When the evidence lies beyond the window, accuracy falls to 26.4% for the released checkpoint (27.2% for both fine-tunes), near the 27.0% of guessing and below the 35.6% of always giving the commonest label. Confidence gives no warning (AUROC 0.50 to 0.55 for telling cut from read requests; 0.5 = chance), and the runtime's truncation flag also fires on requests whose evidence was visible. The released checkpoint reads visible evidence badly once text precedes it: 38.5% pooled against 60% when the evidence comes first, so a larger window alone gains +6.2 points. Fine-tuning on long documents adds +27.6 points on visible evidence over the short-row fine-tune (95% interval +22.3 to +33.1) and nothing on cut evidence. That is an in-family result: training rows and test documents share generator and question types, and the main filler is of the training kind. With encyclopedic filler never seen in training the gain is +13.2 (+9.9 to +16.6), with rows of other tasks as filler -2.0. Among training-free rearrangements a 312-token window scan helps most (+11.9 points over the default for the released checkpoint) at six to eleven forward passes. Documents are synthetic, labels are LLM-made and one backbone is studied, so these diagnose this model, not long documents in general.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Compressing Everything: Contrastive Memory Learning for Selective Context Compression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16384)**  `arXiv:2611.16384`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Sikui Zhang, Haowei Liu, Ziyun Gan, Chunfeng Yuan, Guangze Gao, Nian Liu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As large language models (LLMs) advance toward increasingly long inputs, soft context compression offers a promising approach to efficient inference by converting long inputs into compact memory slots. Under limited memory budgets, effective context compression remains challenging because task-relevant evidence varies substantially across queries, ranging from localized evidence to multiple distant spans or information dispersed throughout the full document. Instead of compressing everything indiscriminately, we propose CoMem, a query-guided selective context compression framework that learns memory representations conditioned on both the document and the query. CoMem develops Contrastive Compressor Learning to achieve query-sensitive yet context-preserving memory representations. To inject a strong prior over query-dependent evidence distributions, we devise a same-context hard negative mining strategy by pairing a single document with multiple queries, requiring the compressor to selectively retain information according to each query's distinct evidence needs. To address the restricted information flow imposed by causal masking, we further enable bidirectional attention among memory slots. Experiments demonstrate that CoMem consistently outperforms existing context compression methods across compression ratios on question answering and RULER-NIAH benchmarks. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Label-Efficient Document-Grounded Factuality Detection via Cross-Paraphrastic Invariance Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16264)**  `arXiv:2611.16264`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Chao Chen, Shanshan Lin, Dongsheng Hong, Sibo Ju, Wenjie Yang, Sihong Xie, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) can generate fluent claims that are unsupported by a given source document, motivating accurate and efficient document-grounded factuality detection. Existing LLM-based evaluators often require repeated inference and incur high computational cost, whereas compact factuality detectors are efficient at inference time but commonly rely on large labeled or synthetically generated corpora. We propose CPIL (Cross-Paraphrastic Invariance Learning), a two-stage Siamese framework that converts a small labeled set into relational supervision without acquiring additional factuality labels. CPIL constructs positive pairs from distinct label-preserving paraphrastic views of the same document-claim instance and negative pairs from opposite-label examples associated with the same source document. It further introduces quantile-aware semi-hard negative mining, which derives a boundary from the positive-pair similarity distribution and balances informative negatives with broader coverage. In the two-stage training procedure, Stage 1 learns an encoder from these task-aware pairs, and Stage 2 jointly fine-tunes the encoder and a linear classification head for binary factuality prediction. At inference time, CPIL requires no paraphrase generation, pair construction, or Siamese comparison and uses only one encoder-classifier forward pass. On the 11-task LLM-AggreFact benchmark, CPIL-2% and CPIL-3% achieve macro-F1 scores of 81.04 and 81.60 while using only 2-3% of the FactCG-released labeled pool. Both settings outperform all compared compact detectors and two vanilla LLM references. Ablations and sensitivity analyses support the contributions of task-aware pair construction, dynamic negative mining, and multi-pivot paraphrasing.
  </details>

- **[Data-Efficient LLM Safety Alignment Guided by Interpretable Features](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16183)**  `arXiv:2611.16183`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Hongyu Li, Zhan Yu, Zhe Zhao, Kimmo Yan, Xiong Kuang, Renfen Hu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Existing large language models typically rely on massive fine-tuning datasets for safety alignment, which results in higher costs for safety alignment and often leads to the problem of over-refusal. We propose Less is Safer (LIS), a compact yet highly effective dataset for safety alignment, constructed through interpretable feature-guided selection and adversarial synthesis. Specifically, we leverage Sparse Autoencoder (SAE) activations to efficiently select prompts that cover diverse and interpretable safety features. Then, we employ a multi-agent adversarial synthesis framework to generate harmless prompts that nevertheless elicit unnecessary refusals. LIS contains only 2.4K samples, yet outperforms much larger datasets, e.g., TRIDENT-Core (26K), TRIDENT-Edge (19K) and WildJailbreak (262K), in overall performance on four safety and five over-refusal benchmarks. Through ablation experiments, we demonstrate that neither safety data nor over-refusal data alone can achieve optimal results. We also used safety features from SAE, annotated by an LLM, for data filtering, but the results were inferior to those obtained using the pipeline proposed in this paper.
  </details>

- **[Show Your Work in Swahili: Reinforcement Learning for Native-Language Mathematical Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16104)**  `arXiv:2611.16104`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Jessica Lundin, Katherine Rosenfeld, David Adelani, Geoffrey Mosoti Nyakiongora, Halfan Ngowo, Sloan Coffin, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present Fikiria, a two-stage SFT+GRPO recipe for Swahili mathematical reasoning. Its reward multiplies a format gate by a weighted sum of answer accuracy and the ChrF\texttt{++} similarity of the Swahili reasoning to an in-language reference chain of thought, so that training targets the surface fidelity of the reasoning trace and not only the final answer. We train on a 2.3k-problem Swahili dataset created by translating GSM8K with GPT-4.1 and filtering with SSA-COMET. On AfriMGSM Swahili, Fikiria-AfriqueQwen-14B reaches 79.2\%. Under the same fixed prompt and strict answer-format scoring, GPT-4o reaches 68.0\%, partly because it often fails the required answer format; IrokoBench reports 78.8\% for GPT-4o using a best-per-model prompt, a figure numerically close to ours but not directly comparable. In a small human evaluation with co-author annotators, GPT-4o's outputs were preferred overall. ChrF\texttt{++} measures surface fidelity, not the validity of individual reasoning steps.
  </details>

- **[Faithful in Steps: Improving Generalization and Citation in RAG via Query Decomposition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15538)**  `arXiv:2611.15538`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Yue Liu, Zhongying Ru, Shimin Di, Jipeng Zhang, Ruiyuan Zhang, Xiaofang Zhou_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Retrieval-augment generation is a prevalent strategy to mitigate hallucinations of LLMs. The attributable RAG (RAGQ) generates quotes for its answers. The quotes indicate which input contexts support the RAG to derive the answers, enhancing the answer's verifiability and trustworthiness. However, existing RAGQs exhibit significant degradation when dealing with questions that require multi-hop reasoning and multi-modal understanding, suffering from over-citation, implicit entity identification failure, and poor generalization. In this paper, we propose a novel RAGQ framework, namely QDRAG. QDRAG breaks down the input question into atomic subquestions to identify the implicit entities. Then, the reranker prunes context distractors to eliminate the downstream over-citation. To facilitate query decomposition, we propose two zero-shot approaches: QD-C and QD-R, which guide the QD MLLM to decompose the question based on context knowledge and retrieval rewards, respectively. One interesting finding is that finetuning on the QD task shows better generalizability compared to directly finetuning on the downstream RAGQ task. Experiments on four multi-modal QA benchmarks demonstrate QDRAG's efficacy in grounding answers and generating faithful citations. The framework significantly outperforms all the baselines on both in-domain and out-of-domain tests, even surpassing Gemini-Pro.
  </details>

- **[Breaking Bad: "Going Badly" Activations Drive Reward Hacking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14996)**  `arXiv:2611.14996`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Natalia Fischl-Lanzoni, Punya Syon Pandey, Merlin Stein, David Demitri Africa_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Humans often cheat when a task is going badly and a goal feels out of reach. We explore and validate an analogous idea in language models: they exhibit a similar internal pressure state that precedes reward hacking. Using everyday stories (crucially, without any hacking examples), we extract a task badness direction that rises as failures accumulate across three models we study (Qwen3.6-27B, GPT-OSS-20B, and Gemma-4-31B). On impossible or difficult coding tasks, task badness rises over the turns leading up to a hack. Further, subtracting the direction a moderate amount reduces cheating in Qwen, more than steering against desperation or task impossibility. Subtracting task badness by an even larger amount blinds the agent to its own failures, causing it to interpret failing tests as passing. However, the relationship to hacking differs across models: in GPT-OSS, the state separates hacking runs from honest solves but not honest failures. Our results suggest monitoring and mitigation strategies based on tracking this state of task badness. Further, as agents are set on more difficult and long-horizon tasks, states like this may push them toward illegible and obscured chain-of-thought, so oversight will be needed on internal representations such as this.
  </details>

- **[MITRA-Swarm: Injecting Explicit Linguistic Structure into Foundation Models for Classical Machine Translation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14675)**  `arXiv:2611.14675`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Kayshav Bhardwaj, Pragun Seth, Kush Bhardwaj, Vijay Nagasamy, Kurt Keutzer_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Classical Sanskrit poses three challenges unresolved by single-call monolithic models: morphophonemic fusion Sandhi, dense nominal compounding Samāsa, and discourse-level zero-anaphora forced by poetic meter. We present MITRA-Swarm, a multi-agent architecture injecting explicit linguistic structure into an otherwise monolithic pipeline: a Sandhi-splitting agent, a parallel array of generalist gemma-4-26B-A4B-it and domain-specialized gemma-2-mitra-it translators, and a discourse-aware Ellipsis Restorer that consults corpus-derived register tags to avoid inventing active-voice subjects in impersonal legal and philosophical registers. Two separated judges evaluate quality: a routing judge ($J_{\text{route}}$, same model family as the generators) driving an internal critique loop, and an independent reporting judge ($J_{\text{report}}=$gpt-4o-mini, no shared vendor lineage) scoring every output exactly once. On a stratified benchmark of 1,000 verses across five registers (23,990 scored verdicts, 12-condition pre-registered ablation), the full swarm reaches $85.90$ MQM against a monolithic baseline of $85.27$ ($\Delta=+0.63$, $p_{\text{adj}}=0.0018$, 95% CI $[+0.30,+0.97]$), and isolated discourse restoration alone accounts for $+0.44$ MQM ($p_{\text{adj}}=0.0288$), outperforming a call-count-matched self-refinement baseline by $+0.51$ MQM ($p_{\text{adj}}=0.0288$). Severe per-verse failures (MQM$<80$) fall from $9.0\%$ to $7.6\%$, at roughly $9\times$ the per-verse inference cost of the baseline. Additionally, $J_{\text{route}}$ scores its own family's outputs $2$-$7$ MQM higher than $J_{\text{report}}$ on every one of the 12 conditions, empirically motivating the two-judge split; and a preflight screen of a 192,151-entry retrieval index against all 12 evaluation works verifies 0 reference leaks across 269 runtime checks. These are small gains on a metric saturating near 85-86.
  </details>

- **[An AI Assistant for Automating Anti-Corruption Expertise of Draft Legislation Based on the TRACE Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14244)**  `arXiv:2611.14244`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Serhii Zabolotnii, Alina Tarasova_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present an AI assistant for automating anti-corruption expertise of draft legislation based on the TRACE architecture, designed to screen bills for corruption risks: pinpoint provisions an expert would criticise and name the regulatory defect in each. The pipeline combines a deterministic parsing core, model-guided single-provision qualification, rule-based factor assignment, and external human oversight. To evaluate the assistant, we constructed an open benchmark from the published conclusions of Ukraine's National Agency on Corruption Prevention (NAZK), comprising 132 draft laws with 474 model-extracted findings. A system flag agrees with a finding when it targets a compatible provision and an LLM judge rates the risk as the same (strict) or partly the same (lenient). On 100 draft laws, the pipeline matches 0.28 of the findings within its top ten flags using the local Gemma 4 31B model, 0.30 with Sonnet 5.5, and 0.43-0.46 across all flags (non-blind; the frozen blind baseline achieves 0.21, 95% CI 0.16-0.26). Across all runs, most misses occur on provisions that the system located but qualified as a different risk, and none of the 32 legal-collision findings was matched. On 21 acts held out from tuning, the pipeline outperforms a monolithic prompt containing the full methodology under the strict measure, though not under the lenient one, and Opus 5.5 adds nothing over Sonnet. The reported scores reflect agreement with model-extracted findings rated by an LLM judge whose two access routes agree on 73 of 74 labels, without human expert validation.
  </details>

- **[Program-Aided Patchable Skill Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14019)**  `arXiv:2611.14019`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Jiahao Wang, Yuqi Xiong, Qing Yang, Zhipeng Xu, Zhenghao Liu, Zulong Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agent skills have emerged as an effective means of enabling LLM agents to solve complex, interactive tasks by encoding reusable knowledge without modifying model parameters. Recent approaches further improve skill adaptation by distilling reusable experience from agent execution trajectories and evolving skills based on downstream task feedback. However, existing methods primarily represent skills as natural language documents, intertwining procedural logic with descriptive knowledge and making skills difficult to maintain and evolve. To address this limitation, we propose PASE (Program-Aided Patchable Skill Evolution), a program-aided framework that iteratively evolves skills by associating them with executable programs. Unlike prior approaches that encode trajectory-derived experience primarily in natural language, PASE separates reusable procedural logic into editable programs, enabling targeted updates to executable components while reducing the need to repeatedly revise natural language skills. Extensive experiments across diverse models and benchmarks demonstrate that PASE consistently outperforms all baselines, by achieving over 2.8% improvements. Further analyses show that PASE exhibits greater evolutionary stability and generalisation capability, highlighting the critical role of programmatic knowledge in skill evolution. We will release all data and code via GitHub.
  </details>

- **[Evaluating Jev on Arabic: Comparing Decision Models and LLMs Across Arabic Tasks and Dialects](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10802)**  `arXiv:2611.10802`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Hossam Elsafty, Tobias Deußer, Lorenz Sparrenberg, Rajkumar Ramamurthy, Rafet Sifa_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  How does Jev compare with other systems on Arabic tasks, and how does its performance change between Modern Standard Arabic (MSA) and dialects? We evaluate 67 systems, including 47 decision models and 20 large language models (LLMs), on 43 tasks across 9 task families using a shared zero-shot protocol. The Arabic-focused frozen suite includes English controls in three parallel resources, which we also remove in a separate descriptive comparison. Jev 1.14.0 achieves a mean task-primary metric of 69.24 on a scale multiplied by 100, compared with 68.77 for Jev 1.13.0 and 71.81 for Gemma-4-31B, the highest-scoring system on this aggregate. Removing the recorded English controls, repeated cross-task items or both leaves the full system order unchanged. Relative performance and gains between Jev versions vary across task families. In content-matched comparisons within Jev 1.14.0, pooled dialect-minus-MSA accuracy gaps range from -7.94 to -0.35 percentage points across four datasets; dialect-specific results show larger variation, and SIB-200's source-clustered interval includes zero. Paired contrasts with Jev 1.13.0, Gemma-4-31B and Jais-2-8B show a larger Palestinian banking penalty for Jev 1.14.0 than for either LLM under simultaneous uncertainty; the other gap contrasts remain inconclusive. These descriptive findings show why aggregate scores should be read alongside task-family results, coverage and matched language comparisons. They do not establish a general model ranking for dialect robustness or explain the causes of the observed gaps.
  </details>

- **[Adaptive Retrieval, Selective Answers: Evaluating Conformal Factuality in Agentic RAG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34872)**  `arXiv:2610.34872`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Muhammad Aimal Rehman, Chi-Kuang Yeh_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) improves the grounding of language-model responses through external evidence, yet retrieved information alone does not ensure factual support for generated claims. In agentic RAG, evidence acquisition is further complicated by adaptive retrieval decisions, where an agent iteratively determines whether to retrieve additional information or terminate the search. We investigate how conformal factuality can be applied in this setting to improve the reliability of generated responses. We formalize the agent-driven retrieval process and characterize a query-aware relevance-scoring formulation that accounts for evidence collected across successive retrieval decisions. Building on this formulation, we evaluate post-generation conformal claim filtering using GPT-6 Luna and Llama 3.1 8B across HotpotQA, Natural Questions, and TriviaQA. Our experiments demonstrate that increasingly stringent conformal targets are consistently associated with higher observed response-level factual support, accompanied by reductions in claim retention and non-empty response coverage. The extent of these trade-offs varies across models and datasets, highlighting the importance of jointly evaluating factual reliability and answer availability. These findings establish an empirical foundation for studying conformal factuality in agent-driven retrieval systems and motivate further investigation into improving the reliability of their generated responses.
  </details>

- **[Low-Cost Assays for Measuring Model Behavior Across Vendors and Releases](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30012)**  `arXiv:2609.30012`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Tapan Parikh_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Language models advise people, keep them company, and write software while they sleep. Measuring what they do is hard: behavior has to be sampled repeatedly across models, prompts and releases, most of it lives in unstructured text that has to be coded before it can be counted, and the result has to be legible and rigorous enough to meaningfully compare models and vendors. To address these constraints, we present a simple, cheap, scalable, and replicable model for studying model behavior. Each study is a frozen, public stimulus run identically on a cross-vendor panel, at a few dollars per model or less. Each reads its transcripts one of three ways, chosen by how much interpretation the behavior needs: exact match on a clamped reply, a codebook applied by LLM judges whose agreement with a human coder is reported per code, and an instrumented environment that records what an agent did independently of what it said. Run across four years of model releases from both frontier and open-source labs, these instruments find four things. Convergence: asked to pick a word, 27 of 44 models answer serendipity at least once in four tries. Resistance: a trailing "right?" moves endorsement by up to 32 points, and the sign flips from sycophantic to resistant as generations advance, keyed to the tag's surface form. House: whether a model holds a position under pressure tracks its generation, and how it holds tracks the lab that built it. Account: told to do something the documentation in their repository contradicts, some coding agents never went along silently and others always did, and the same model can change with the harness it runs in. Re-run on every release, batteries like these track how behavior is changing across vendors and over time.
  </details>

- **[When Failures Propagate: Causal Failure Attribution in Agentic Retrieval-Augmented Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20627)**  `arXiv:2608.20627`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Lauren Pothuru, Spurthi Setty, Natan Vidra_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agentic retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) interleaves retrieval, reasoning, and answer generation across multiple hops. A retrieval error at hop 1 can surface only as a wrong answer at hop 3, while later retrieval can also repair the trajectory. This paper introduces AgenticRAG-FP, an interventional benchmark for causal failure attribution in agentic RAG. The benchmark injects a certified fault at a specified hop, re-executes the downstream trajectory, and evaluates diagnosers against the known intervention site. Its central question is whether a post-hoc trace still identifies the injected hop after the suffix changes. In the dense Claude Haiku 4.5 structural sweep on three-hop MuSiQue questions, coverage-based diagnosis is 0.91 at hop 1 and 0.00 at hops 2 and 3 (n=43,36,21 failed trajectories), but it predicts hop 1 in 96% of failures, an earliest-hop rule; at hops 2 and 3, an LLM judge and frozen-hop repair localize only 0.25 to 0.48 of failures. In larger content-corruption runs, depth-2 exact-hop accuracy is 0.00 for coverage, 0.49 for an LLM judge, 0.59 for frozen-hop repair, and 0.14 for suffix regeneration (n=51); the judge and frozen-hop repair do not significantly differ (exact McNemar p=0.33).
  </details>

- **[SLIDERS: Evidence Synthesis with Autonomous Database Reconciliation for Systematic Reviews](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.22294)**  `arXiv:2604.22294`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Harshit Joshi, Priyank Shethia, Jadelynn Dao, Monica S. Lam_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Systematic reviews requires comprehensive evidence synthesis from large document corpora to answer targeted research questions and are foundational in finance, social sciences, and other technical fields. Manual construction of evidence tables is labor-intensive, and recent LLM-based assistants relying on embedding or keyword search often fail to meet the coverage standards of systematic reviews. We introduce SLIDERS, a novel LLM-based methodology for automatically assembling evidence tables tailored to research questions for systematic reviews. SLIDERS extracts full-text excerpts that serve as direct evidence or as provenance for structured data. Core to SLIDERS is an autonomous evidence reconciliation coding agent that iteratively writes SQL to analyze and reconcile extracted evidence, bringing together information fragmented across documents, resolving inconsistencies and removing duplicates across records. We evaluate SLIDERS on BrowseComp+, achieving 90% accuracy, and show that it outperforms all baselines on additional benchmarks within the LLM's context window. To assess scalability, we introduce two new evidence-synthesis benchmarks, WikiCeleb100 (3.9M tokens) and FinQ100 (36M tokens), which include follow-up questions beyond the original research query. Although SLIDERS synthesizes evidence knowing only the original question, it answers 78.9% and 55.2% of all questions correctly on WikiCeleb100 and FinQ100, respectively
  </details>

- **[LongReasonArena: A Long Reasoning Benchmark for Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.19363)**  `arXiv:2508.19363`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Jiayu Ding, Shuming Ma, Lei Cui, Nanning Zheng, Furu Wei_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Existing long-context benchmarks for Large Language Models (LLMs) focus on evaluating comprehension of long inputs, while overlooking the evaluation of long reasoning abilities. To address this gap, we introduce LongReasonArena, a benchmark specifically designed to assess the long reasoning capabilities of LLMs. Our tasks require models to solve problems by executing multi-step algorithms that reflect key aspects of long reasoning, such as retrieval and backtracking. By controlling the inputs, the required reasoning length can be arbitrarily scaled, reaching up to 1 million tokens of reasoning for the most challenging tasks. Extensive evaluation results demonstrate that LongReasonArena presents a significant challenge for both open-source and proprietary LLMs. For instance, even GPT-5 achieves only 27.8% accuracy on Level 3. Further analysis also reveals that the accuracy exhibits a linear decline with respect to the logarithm of the expected number of reasoning steps. Our code and data are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[When Language Overrules: Revealing Text Dominance in Multimodal Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.10552)**  `arXiv:2508.10552`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Huyu Wu, Meng Tang, Xinhan Zheng, Haiyun Jiang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have demonstrated remarkable capabilities across a diverse range of multimodal tasks. However, these models suffer from a core problem known as text dominance: they depend heavily on text for their inference, while underutilizing other modalities. While prior work has acknowledged this phenomenon in vision-language tasks, often attributing it to data biases or model architectures. In this paper, we conduct the first systematic investigation of text dominance across diverse data modalities, including images, videos, audio, time-series, and graphs.
