To measure this imbalance, we propose two evaluation metrics: the Modality Dominance Index (MDI) and the Attention Efficiency Index (AEI). Our comprehensive analysis reveals that text dominance is both significant and pervasive across all tested modalities. Our in-depth analysis identifies three underlying causes: attention dilution from severe token redundancy in non-textual modalities, the influence of fusion architecture design, and task formulations that implicitly favor textual inputs. Furthermore, we propose a simple token compression method that effectively rebalances model attention. Applying this method to LLaVA-7B, for instance, drastically reduces its MDI from 10.23 to a well-balanced value of 0.86. Our analysis and methodological framework offer a foundation for the development of more equitable and comprehensive multimodal language models.
  </details>

- **[MoralBench: Moral Evaluation of LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2406.04428)**  `arXiv:2406.04428`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Jianchao Ji, Yutong Chen, Mingyu Jin, Wujiang Xu, Wenyue Hua, Yongfeng Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In the rapidly evolving field of artificial intelligence, large language models (LLMs) have emerged as powerful tools for a myriad of applications, from natural language processing to decision-making support systems. However, as these models become increasingly integrated into societal frameworks, the imperative to ensure they operate within ethical and moral boundaries has never been more critical. This paper introduces a novel benchmark designed to measure and compare the moral reasoning capabilities of LLMs. We present the first comprehensive dataset specifically curated to probe the moral dimensions of LLM outputs, addressing a wide range of ethical dilemmas and scenarios reflective of real-world complexities.The main contribution of this work lies in the development of benchmark datasets and metrics for assessing the moral identity of LLMs, which accounts for nuance, contextual sensitivity, and alignment with human ethical standards. Our methodology involves a multi-faceted approach, combining quantitative analysis with qualitative insights from ethics scholars to ensure a thorough evaluation of model performance. By applying our benchmark across several leading LLMs, we uncover significant variations in moral reasoning capabilities of different models. These findings highlight the importance of considering moral reasoning in the development and evaluation of LLMs, as well as the need for ongoing research to address the biases and limitations uncovered in our study. We publicly release the benchmark atthis https URLand also open-source the code of the project atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Shortcuts or Memorisation? Revisiting Data Corruption in Natural Language Understanding with Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16843)**  `arXiv:2611.16843`  `cs.CL`  
  _Aarne Talman, Stergios Chatzikyriakidis_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Pre-trained encoders fine-tuned on natural language inference (NLI) and other language understanding tasks keep much of their accuracy after the input is perturbed, e.g. by removing all nouns or shuffling the words. Does the same hold for large language models (LLMs), and if so, is it because they have seen the benchmarks during pre-training? In this paper we revisit these perturbation experiments with eight LLMs alongside fine-tuned encoders on GLUE and the three rounds of ANLI. Regardless of model size, every system that answers directly retains 36-72% of its above-chance performance without nouns on MNLI compared to only 14-29% on ANLI. Only a model that reasons before answering departs from this pattern. We also check contamination indicators (including exact $n$-gram counts in the public pre-training data of two of the models) and find no link between exposure and robustness. Our strongest evidence comes from 368 new, human-validated items that none of the models can have seen. All eight LLMs keep more of their performance without nouns on items written in MNLI's style than on items written without its cues. When the perturbation instead removes the function words carrying such cues, this advantage reverses. In light of these results, we argue that robustness to perturbation on familiar NLI benchmarks reflects dataset shortcuts rather than memorisation, and tells us more about the benchmark than about the model.
  </details>

- **[Outcome-Mass Alignment: Controlling Length-Dependent Trajectory Influence in Multi-Turn LLM Agent Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16816)**  `arXiv:2611.16816`  `cs.CL`  
  _Dawei Zhang, Yubin Huangfu, Jessica Fan, Boyu Wei, Likang Wu, Hongke Zhao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  To keep long interaction contexts tractable, stepwise group-relative training for multi-turn LLM agents materializes each source trajectory into multiple response rows and valid tokens. This expansion creates an outer-accounting problem distinct from within-trajectory credit assignment: row-based outcome statistics can count one episode outcome multiple times, while token-level reduction can assign unequal aggregate objective mass across trajectories. We term these effects outcome-statistics multiplicity and objective-mass imbalance. We introduce Outcome-Mass Alignment (OMA), a two-component method that makes both accounting choices explicit. Outcome Alignment computes outcome statistics over unique source trajectories, while Mass Alignment controls aggregate objective mass after within-trajectory aggregation of optimizer-native token objectives. Neither redesigns the base optimizer's credit assignment, importance ratios, or clipping. Across matched ALFWorld GRPO comparisons at two model scales, where failed trajectories typically expand much more than successful ones, OMA improves matched-horizon endpoint success by 7.0-9.4 percentage points. Ordinary WebShop exhibits weak or reversed expansion-outcome coupling and correspondingly mixed effects, whereas in a success-oriented WebShop setting, WebShop-SOL, OMA improves success nAUC from 0.399 to 0.511. Component ablations and GiGPO and GSPO comparisons further support component separability and cross-optimizer compatibility. These results show that trajectory expansion always defines an implicit weighting rule, but its practical effect depends on how expansion couples to outcomes and optimizer-native updates.
  </details>

- **[Towards Green Text Generation: Memory-Based Speculative Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16714)**  `arXiv:2611.16714`  `cs.CL`  
  _Antal van den Bosch_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Speculative decoding accelerates text generation with large language models by letting a cheap draft model propose tokens that a large verifier checks in a single forward pass. We introduce Olifant, a draft model built on a memory-based classifier. It predicts the next token from a four-token window in under 0.3 ms on CPU, requiring no GPU, no backpropagation, and no accelerator memory. We measure throughput and energy under two verifier regimes on consumer hardware. Without a KV cache, drafting is nearly free: a domain-matched Olifant model reaches 38.1% acceptance on EU legal text, delivering 2.43x throughput and 2.51x lower energy per token than the neural verifier model alone. With a KV-cached verifier, itself a ~10x energy saver, drafting costs matter: a TinyLlama draft model halves throughput and triples energy despite high acceptance, whereas Olifant still yields 1.25x throughput and the lowest energy in the study (93 J per 100 tokens, 14x below the uncached baseline). We further show that KV caching decouples time from energy: verification of longer drafts raises power draw, shifting the energy-optimal draft length below the speed-optimal one. Speculative decoding is not dead in the age of fast verifiers, provided drafting is nearly free.
  </details>

- **[Forecasting Sales Delay through Time-aware Analysis of Sales Events in Textual Reports](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16704)**  `arXiv:2611.16704`  `cs.CL`  
  _Masaya Tsunokake, Yuta Koreeda, Yuhei Niwa, Terufumi Morishita, Teppei Inoue, Hiroaki Ozaki, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Business-to-business (B2B) companies develop and deliver a one-of-a-kind product in response to a client's orders. To prepare resources for development beforehand, companies estimate dates when they will receive orders from clients in their sales process, called scheduled order dates. However, dates when the companies actually receive orders can differ from the scheduled order dates. This difference increases costs in the development. If the difference could be forecasted before receiving orders, companies could optimize their resource preparation. Towards reducing the costs, we introduce sales delay forecasting, a novel task of forecasting delay days from the scheduled order dates during sales process where sales representatives (sales reps) interact with the clients to receive orders. Sales delay forecasting is challenging because the sales process progress inherently involves uncertainty and depends on the client's willingness to order. To address this task, we propose confidence features which represent sales reps' confidence as a proxy measure of the client's willingness to order. To represent it, our method extracts dates of future sales events anticipated by sales reps from textual reports. To consider whether the confidence objectively looks reasonable, we also introduce status features which describe sales process status based on current date and past events. In experiments using real sales data, our method achieved a 16% lower mean absolute error compared with a feature-based baseline similar to a prior work. Moreover, our method outperformed non-feature-based baselines using large language models, indicating that it is crucial to explicitly create our features.
  </details>

- **[Unibuc - NLP at "Voight-Kampff" Generative AI Detection PAN 2025](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16612)**  `arXiv:2611.16612`  `cs.CL`  
  _Teodor Marchitan, Claudiu Creanga, Liviu P. Dinu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  This paper describes the approach of the Unibuc - NLP team in tackling the "Voight-Kampff" Generative AI Detection task from PAN at CLEF 2025. In the Subtask 1: Voight-Kampff AI Detection Sensitivity we have explored different approaches of leveraging embeddings extracted from LLMs, using either classical ML models or further fine-tuning LLMs with a classification head for the downstream binary classification task. Our experiments showed that using a majority vote ensemble of classical ML models (Light GBM, XGB, Logistic Regression and SVM) is comparable with fine-tuning for downstream task achieving around 99.4% F1 score. For the Subtask 2: Human-AI Collaborative Text Classification we explored a way of combining features at different layers extracted using transformer backbone with layer-wise projection and attentive pooling which proved modest results.
  </details>

- **[EvenVLM: A Text and Multimodal Dataset for the Even Language with Vision-Language Fine-Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16500)**  `arXiv:2611.16500`  `cs.CL`  
  _Daniil Makovetskii, Ivan Bondarenko_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present EvenVLM, the first text and multimodal (VQA) dataset for Even (Lamut), an endangered Northern Tungusic language spoken by approximately 6,800 people in Siberia. Our dataset comprises 50,521 structured Even-Russian entries extracted from dictionary and phrasebook sources via GPT-4o Mini-based parsing, from which we synthetically generate 107,543 training examples spanning translation, grammar, reading comprehension, and semantic classification. To address tokenization inefficiency on Even's agglutinative morphology, we propose a three-step tokenizer expansion algorithm that adds Even-specific graphemes (eng, oe, o-umlaut), whitelisted vocabulary, and BPE merges, reducing average tokens per word by 27% (4.99 to 3.64). Using LoRA fine-tuning of Qwen3-VL-4B, we adapt the model for text tasks (0.53% trainable parameters, rank 32) and separately for visual question answering (rank 64, frozen vision encoder). Our best configuration achieves 11.4% exact match and a 13.6 weighted score on text benchmarks, 0.1283 chrF for sentence translation, and 65% accuracy on VQA (up from 0% for the base model and text-only fine-tuning). We release our datasets, evaluation benchmarks, and training pipeline to support further research on Even and other low-resource languages.
  </details>

- **[daVinci-Prover: Agent-Native Mid-Training for Formal Theorem Proving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16433)**  `arXiv:2611.16433`  `cs.CL`  
  _Wenhong Zhu, Xuefeng Li, Jiashuo Zhang, Yu Chen, Tiantian Mi, Qipeng Guo, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Advances in large language models (LLMs) are rapidly transforming formal theorem proving. Yet further progress remains constrained by the lack of a transparent and effective data recipe for systematically improving theorem-proving models. In this work, we argue that effective agentic provers require strong mathematical skill priors to ground theorem proving and agentic trajectories to enable long-horizon, interactive proof construction. To this end, we synthesize an agent-native 33.7B-token mid-training corpus covering decomposition, search, self-verification, and revision, together with verifier-grounded agentic trajectories. We further introduce the daVinci-Prover harness, which enables models to construct proofs and interact with a Lean verifier in an isolated workspace, and use it to collect approximately 36K high-quality agent trajectories for supervised fine-tuning. Our mid-training recipe yields an average improvement of 13.7 points over the matched Qwen3-8B SFT-only baseline across MiniF2F, FATE-M, and ProofNet. When applied to the Qwen3.5 series, our approach also yields strong performance across theorem-proving benchmarks and achieves near-saturating MiniF2F performance with memory compaction. The results highlight agent-native mid-training as an effective foundation for developing stronger interactive theorem-proving agents.
  </details>

- **[From Large Language Models to Efficient System One Models via Adaptive Computation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16321)**  `arXiv:2611.16321`  `cs.CL`  
  _Minh-Anh Nguyen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Transferring LLMs into System One decision models typically replaces the language modeling head with a decision head while retaining the full backbone, making every decision pay for all layers. Yet it remains unclear how much depth such decisions require. We find that decision information is concentrated in intermediate layers, with early layers building essential features and the final six layers removable without loss, while independently trained smaller models largely mimic the full model rather than developing specialized decision capabilities. These findings suggest that efficiency requires not simply smaller backbones, but shared computation across different depths. Building on this, we propose \method{}, which jointly trains a full model with prefix sub-models that share its final layers and decision head through self-distillation. A lightweight gate reads the hidden state at each exit to determine whether additional computation is needed, allowing one model to operate at multiple layers. Across seven decision benchmarks, our approach matches or outperforms independently trained models at every depth, while achieving better accuracy-efficiency trade-offs through adaptive computation.
  </details>

- **[ChJGBench: A Comprehensive Benchmark for Chinese Legal Judgment Generation with Diagnostic Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15639)**  `arXiv:2611.15639`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yinghao Hu, Linggang Kong, Qian Xiao, Yuting Huang, Yunlong Zhang, Yujie Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  With the rapid development of LLMs, increasing attention has been paid to whether they can reliably generate complete and legally valid judicial documents. This calls for a benchmark capable of evaluating whether models can accurately apply statutes and resolve outcome-critical issues, including claims, charges, penalties, and monetary obligations. However, existing benchmarks typically focus on isolated legal tasks or coarse-grained evaluation. We introduce ChJGBench, a benchmark for generating the reasoning and dispositive sections of Chinese judgments across civil, criminal, and administrative cases. Built from 3,462 real-world judgments, ChJGBench provides a case-type-aware diagnostic evaluation framework comprising 13 metric types and adopts a hybrid paradigm. Experiments reveal no uniform advantage for legal-domain models over general-purpose models, with model strengths varying across diagnostic dimensions. Performance is generally stronger on criminal cases, while civil and administrative cases continue to present substantial challenges.
  </details>

- **[Can We Make an LLM Dumber, or Is It Just Playing Dumb? An Empirical Study of Believable Performance Throttling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15568)**  `arXiv:2611.15568`  `cs.CL`  
  _Chengguang Gan, Yunhao Liang, Hanjun Wei, QingHao Zhang, Shiwen Ni_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A benchmark score is commonly read as a proxy for model capability. We study the inverse problem: can a model be driven to a target score, and do its failures resemble those of a naturally weaker model? We operationalize believable performance reduction along three axes: target calibration, item-difficulty structure, and error-distribution similarity to a real weak reference. We introduce CapLadder, an IRT-calibrated suite of 1,985 items spanning mathematics, code, multiple-choice reasoning, and tool use. A simple weak-teacher LoRA dial reaches unseen target levels on reasoning with a 1.9-point held-out calibration error, whereas prompting, temperature, and weight noise cannot combine comparable range and precision. Yet on multiple choice, the dial converges to an answer-token attractor: up to 97% of its newly introduced errors land on one letter, with the attractor changing from D in Qwen to A in Llama. Logit interventions identify a dominant near-constant shift: subtracting the estimated shift on held-out items recovers 52 to 59% of lost accuracy and removes the dominant attractor, while option permutation confirms that the bias tracks token position rather than option content. With high inter-annotator agreement ($\kappa=0.94$), human-coded error-category distributions nonetheless match the weak reference on reasoning (JS = 0.00), showing that local plausibility and aggregate detectability differ. Structured tool calls exhibit more natural degradation in the Qwen family (JS = 0.09), while a globally calibrated dial transfers poorly across benchmarks (14.2-point error on two usable benchmarks). Precise score control, therefore, does not imply a portable or believable reduction in skill.
  </details>

- **[Towards Better Legal Reasoning LLMs: Signal Balancing and Reward Scheduling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15346)**  `arXiv:2611.15346`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yinghao Hu, Qian Xiao, Yuting Huang, Kaisong Song, Yiquan Wu, Ang Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In the legal domain, LLMs can improve reliability and interpretability by generating explicit reasoning traces. However, training LLMs to produce high-quality reasoning traces remains challenging. Existing supervised fine-tuning methods struggle when reasoning traces are significantly longer than final answers, as the learning signal for the answer becomes diluted. Meanwhile, reinforcement learning methods such as Group Relative Policy Optimization also have several limitations, including costly reward design, performance plateaus, and reward hacking. We therefore propose a two-stage training framework. In Stage I, JurisCoT-SFT employs a length-normalized dual objective to balance learning signals between reasoning traces and final answers. In Stage II, Lifecycle-Aware Backtrackable Policy Optimization dynamically evaluates and retires auxiliary reward signals based on their impact on final evaluation objectives, enabling efficient reward utilization. Our framework substantially improves legal reasoning performance on relevant tasks selected from multiple legal benchmarks, including ChJGBench, LawBench, and LegalBench. Experimental results demonstrate consistent improvements in both reasoning trace quality and final answer accuracy, while our models remain competitive with larger models.
  </details>

- **[FIBER: Bayesian Fusion of Fast-DetectGPT and IRT-Informed Detector Evidence for LLM-Generated Text Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15058)**  `arXiv:2611.15058`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yan Li, Guangsheng Bao, Zhipeng Chen, Guandong Zhang, Yanbin Zhao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Detecting large language model (LLM)-generated text remains challenging under domain and generator shifts. Supervised detectors may generalize poorly, while zero-shot methods often rely on individual statistical signals without leveraging historical detector behavior. We propose FIBER (Fast-DetectGPT with IRT-informed Bayesian Evidence Recalibration), which integrates continuous Fast-DetectGPT scores with auxiliary detector outputs through Bayesian evidence fusion. Item response theory (IRT) models historical detector-correctness patterns, providing class-conditional detector-output likelihoods through marginalization over latent text representations. These likelihoods are combined with Fast-DetectGPT score densities and a historical class prior for prediction. When historical Fast-DetectGPT scores are unavailable, a variant called FIBER-Stream estimates class-specific score distributions from an unlabeled batch. On a 32,000-text multi-domain benchmark, FIBER achieves the highest accuracy (0.620) and improves mean AUC over Fast-DetectGPT (0.664 versus 0.600) under five-fold cross-validation. FIBER also consistently improves AUC across four individual domains. Under out-of-distribution transfer, FIBER-Stream achieved an AUC of 0.911, compared with 0.889 for FIBER and 0.922 for Fast-DetectGPT alone. These results demonstrate the potential of integrating complementary detector evidence and historical information to improve detection performance across heterogeneous settings.
  </details>

- **[CoLFA: Collaboration-Guided Lightweight Failure Attribution for LLM-Based Multi-Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14980)**  `arXiv:2611.14980`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yufei Luo, Yuchen Ling, Chunrong Fang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Failure attribution in multi-agent systems requires tracing error formation to identify the decisive error step and the responsible agent. However, agents' division of work and information exchange lead to Interleaved Steps and Cluttered Content, requiring models to link executions and check details in lengthy records. This makes it difficult to balance localization accuracy and token cost. We find that multi-agent collaboration also leaves records of how executions connect and content passes between them as results are formed, providing evidence for tracing error formation. Based on this observation, we propose CoLFA (Collaboration-Guided Lightweight Failure Attribution), which uses collaboration cues to organize the attribution input according to how results are formed, determining which steps are examined and how their content is presented. Through Handoff-Guided Step Expansion and Cross-Referenced Content Organization, CoLFA helps the model focus on examining error formation and the role of each decision while reducing the processing of irrelevant and repeated records. Across five settings spanning Who&When and LongRCA and three backbone models, CoLFA achieves the highest exact step-localization accuracy with the lowest token consumption. Compared with individual baselines in each setting, CoLFA improves accuracy by up to 37.50% and reduces token consumption by up to 97.6%.
  </details>

- **[ToolTurns: Benchmarking LLM Agents in Multi-Turn Dialogue](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14925)**  `arXiv:2611.14925`  `cs.CL`  
  _Milad Gholami, Brendan King, Jeffrey Flanigan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Tool-using language models are increasingly deployed in conversational settings, yet most large-scale tool-use benchmarks focus on isolated requests. We introduce ToolTurns, a benchmark of 2,620 problems spanning 1,827 tools and 11,004 API endpoints across 47 categories. Each dialogue begins with an intentionally underspecified request and continues through two context-dependent follow-ups, testing clarification, tool use, and retention of conversational context. We also introduce an open-source user simulator that reveals withheld context through dialogue and generates follow-up requests, with a checker--fixer pipeline for improving its turns. Across nine language models, the best agent resolves 69.6% of individual requests but completes only 39.8% of full dialogues. Under this evaluation protocol, every evaluated model receives lower success scores on follow-up requests than on the initial request, and dialogue-quality rubric scores and task success can rank models very differently. Paired turn-level evaluation shows that the checker--fixer reduces detected dialogue defects, and blind human evaluation favors the revised turns. ToolTurns provides a testbed for evaluating whether agents sustain task success across multi-turn dialogue with tools.
  </details>

- **[Is Jev a Good Multilingual Judge?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14861)**  `arXiv:2611.14861`  `cs.CL`  
  _Wei Liu, Yi Fan, Xiyan Fu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Generative large language models are widely used for multilingual text evaluation, yet whether decision models that directly output judgment probabilities can serve as multilingual judges has received limited attention. We evaluate Jev against generative LLM judges on four multilingual benchmarks with human annotations, covering up to 47 language varieties and three evaluation formats: binary decisions, pairwise comparisons, and graded ratings. At approximately $1/31$ to $1/61$ of GPT-6.1's evaluation cost, Jev is competitive on most multilingual judging tasks, but falls behind GPT-6.1 on knowledge-intensive answer verification. Further analysis shows that Jev is more likely to accept incorrect answers in low-resource languages than in English and that its probability outputs help identify errors across tasks. Based on these findings, we offer recommendations on evaluation instructions, confidence-based selection, and model routing, providing empirical guidance for using Jev as a multilingual judge.
  </details>

- **[LLMs and the Homogenization of Teaching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14828)**  `arXiv:2611.14828`  `cs.CL`  
  _Mei Tan, Hyunji Nam, Hari Subramonyam, Dorottya Demszky_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) are known to lack diversity in open-ended text generation, a limitation that is especially consequential in education, where homogeneity can narrow the kinds of thinking and disciplinary work students are invited to do. We study diversity in two educational generation tasks: math items and essay feedback. We compare frontier and open-weight models with human-authored reference distributions using lexical, semantic, and domain-grounded measures. For math, we examine the mathematical activities students are asked to perform; for feedback, we examine the revision work requested and the rhetorical strategies used to communicate it. We evaluate direct prompting, two diversity prompting methods, and post-training. Across both tasks, directly prompted model outputs are markedly less diverse than human-authored content--78% higher on lexical (Self-BLEU) and 51% higher on semantic (embedding) similarity--and represent a narrower range of instructional forms. Diversity prompting and post-training increase variation largely through surface features without broadening the instructional repertoire. Models inherit and amplify the most normative routines of human instruction. Overcoming mode collapse in education will require richer instructional data and investment in teachers' creative capacity and professional judgment.
  </details>

- **[LLM Validation Makes Minimal-Pair Benchmarks Easier for Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13788)**  `arXiv:2611.13788`  `cs.CL`  
  _Guofang Tang, Zheng Qu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Minimal-pair benchmarks are only as good as their labels, and LLMs are an obvious substitute for costly human validation. We test it on BLiMP (English) and QFrBLiMP (Quebec French), the two public minimal-pair datasets releasing every forced-choice human judgment. Three LLMs validate by forced choice, by rating, and as a panel, scored against human panels of one, three and five judges in two pre-registered phases. On BLiMP, forced-choice validators agree with held-out human majorities more often than five-judge panels, yet catch far fewer pairs whose label humans reject than one judge does. By point estimate, no validator is worth more than one judge. The errors are not random. On both datasets, across 10 open LMs, human-supported pairs that the pre-registered LLM filter flags are 18.9 points harder than those it accepts, in all 70 LM-validator combinations, and harder than pairs a single human judge flags. Filtering thus removes items on which LMs fail but humans do not, raising accuracy more than random removal. A protocol frozen before any output replicates the gap and the lift on Dutch BLiMP-NL. LLM validation makes minimal-pair benchmarks easier for the models they are meant to test; we release code, validator outputs and item-level scores.
  </details>

- **[OpticalRAG: Pixel-Space Compression for Token-Efficient Retrieval-Augmented Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13675)**  `arXiv:2611.13675`  `cs.CL`  
  _Senhao Liu, Yuheng Zhang, Chunyu Wei, Yueguo Chen, Xinran Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) is bottlenecked by the token budget of large language models: feeding long documents into the context window is expensive, while aggressive textual compression discards fine-grained information that is irreversibly lost in a one-dimensional token sequence. We argue that this bottleneck is not fundamental to information density but to the choice of compression \emph{modality}. Modern visual encoders can pack the contents of a rendered text page into roughly a hundred visual tokens with little semantic loss, opening a new compression-fidelity tradeoff that purely textual methods cannot reach. We present \textbf{OpticalRAG}, a framework that renders documents as page images, encodes them with a frozen vision encoder, and distills the retrieved visual tokens into as few as $16$ injected tokens before passing them to the LLM. Two ingredients make this work without expensive cross-modal training: (i) \emph{Encoder-Bridged Retrieval}, which projects text-query states into the visual feature space of the frozen encoder, through a projection shared with the compressor, to retrieve visual tokens from a text query, with no contrastive training; and (ii) \emph{Query-Driven Distillation}, a lightweight cross-attention compressor that condenses the retrieved visual tokens into a small set of query-conditioned prefix tokens. On LongBench-v2, BAMBOO, and LooGLE-v2, OpticalRAG achieves the best compression-aware accuracy (CAP) on all three benchmarks while injecting only $16$ tokens, and improves the closed-book Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct backbone by $5.17$ points on LongBench-v2. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[khammoon: From a Linguistic Guideline to a Thai Morpheme Segmenter](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13012)**  `arXiv:2611.13012`  `cs.CL`  
  _Warit Sirichotedumrong, Adisai Na-Thalang, Chanakan Wittayasakpan, Kritsadha Phatcharoen, Kunat Pipatanakul_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Thai is written without spaces between words, making segmentation a foundational step in most Thai NLP pipelines. Existing tools primarily reproduce the word-level conventions of corpora such as BEST; however, because the notion of a "word" in Thai lacks a unified linguistic consensus, these conventions struggle with productive compounding, leading to arbitrary boundaries and ambiguity in downstream representations. Instead, we propose operating at the morphological level, focusing on khammoon, the smallest atomic unit that carries meaning. Segmenting at the morphemic level provides an explicit, consistent morphological representation that eliminates compounding ambiguity. Crucially, this representation benefits downstream learning: models trained on morphemes perform on par with or exceed word-level baselines across part-of-speech tagging, named-entity recognition, and sentiment classification, demonstrating that linguistic consistency improves representation quality without downstream performance penalty. To operationalize this representation, we translate linguistic principles into a formal decision guideline with near-perfect human agreement ($\kappa \approx 0.99$), scale annotation with an LLM, and correct systematic errors with human labels. Finally, we train khammoon, a lightweight 1M-parameter convolutional BiLSTM segmenter designed for efficient CPU inference, achieving a boundary F1 of 0.992 on out-of-domain text. The guideline, datasets, model, and library are all publicly released.
  </details>

- **[YRC-Bench: Benchmarking Culturally Aware Machine Translation for Historical Heritage via Tri-Level Semantic Bridging](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10555)**  `arXiv:2611.10555`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yan Huang, Aobo Li, Jiayi Wang, Huidong Zhu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Translating historical cultural heritage poses profound challenges for machine translation systems due to the acute semantic asymmetry across eras and cultures, where terse classical syntax frequently obscures domain-specific cultural knowledge. While Large Language Models have demonstrated remarkable prowess in generic translation benchmarks, they struggle to negotiate Culturally-Specific Items and diachronic shifts in domain-rich historical texts, such as Yellow River cultural records. To bridge this critical gap, we introduce YRC-Bench, the first human-curated, multi-parallel benchmark dedicated to historical heritage translation across Classical Chinese, Modern Chinese, and nine target foreign languages. YRC-Bench establishes a tri-level parallel corpus incorporating modern explanatory glosses as explicit semantic bridges and operationalizes translation strategies through paired conserved versus substituted CSI variants categorized by cultural translatability. Systematic evaluations of nine leading LLMs across bidirectional translation directions reveal that leveraging modern Chinese glosses - especially human-curated ones - dramatically elevates both lexical fidelity and semantic adequacy. Furthermore, multidimensional human preference audits by native experts unveil distinct cross-cultural preferences between Sinographic-tradition and non-Sinographic language groups.
  </details>

- **[What Hallucinations Have We Measured? Semantic Illusion, Confabulation, and Expressed Uncertainty](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38520)**  `arXiv:2610.38520`  `cs.CL`  
  _Hazel H. Kim, Jakub Binkowski, Ekaterina Fadeeva, Juan David Guerra, Yijie Hao, Zheng Yi Ho, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Hallucination remains one of the most critical challenges in deploying large lan- guage models (LLMs) reliably and safely. Yet the term is used inconsistently across the research community. We argue for a strict definition of hallucinations in LLMs. First, hallucinations should be confident incorrectness, kept separate from expressed uncertainty (UNK). UNK reveals that the model may be wrong, whereas a hallucination does not reveal it and is therefore more dangerous in prac- tice. Conflating the two obscures their distinct behaviors and makes them hard to study. Second, hallucinations should be split by a simple re-elicitation test: does any semantically equivalent prompt recover the correct response? This test probes whether the relevant knowledge is accessible. If none does, the knowledge is likely absent or inaccessible, and the error type is a confabulation (CF) that likely requires external knowledge or retraining; if some does, the knowledge is accessible from the model but applied inconsistently, and the error type is a semantic illusion (SI) that simple prompt or decoding changes can mitigate3. Re-evaluating 14 recent hallucination methods under this taxonomy yields three findings. (1) Mitigation methods are far more effective for SI than for CF. (2) Detection methods flag UNK more often than hallucinated responses (i.e., SI and CF). (3) Latent signals are more informative than output-level signals for CF. An audit of recent hallucination papers shows that most conflate these categories. We recommend reporting CF, SI, and UNK separately, and introduce a practical labeling protocol that needs only a modest number of semantically equivalent prompts.
  </details>

- **[SAG2: Embedding-Free Multi-Hop Retrieval with BM25 and Decision Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22588)**  `arXiv:2610.22588`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yuchao Wu, XingCheng Liang, Guanxian Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-hop retrieval must find passages that become useful only after intermediate evidence is retrieved. We present SAG2, an embedding-free retriever whose only persistent structure is a SQLite lexical index, built without an LLM. Where SAG and HippoRAG 2 rely on a generative LLM for graph extraction and selection, SAG2 uses a decision model. Given the question and a shared context of selected retrieved passages, the decision model scores whether each candidate supplies an answer-chain fact. Terms activated from the top-scoring passages retrieve unvisited passages through the lexical index, which holds a passage-term hypergraph implicitly. SAG2 reaches Recall@10 of 86.62%, 97.43%, and 99.00% on 1,000-question subsets of MuSiQue, 2WikiMultiHopQA, and HotpotQA, the highest among the compared systems. With a decision model priced at about a fifth of SAG's LLM, indexing and retrieval on MuSiQue cost USD 4.33 against USD 14.85 for SAG. On EnterpriseRAG-Bench, SAG2 reaches 81.12% document Recall@10. As the benchmark's nested corpus grows 447.5-fold, SAG2's Recall@5 lead over BM25 alone widens from 7.28 to 14.76 points, at a nearly constant model cost per question. Because indexing makes no model call, SAG2 suits corpora that change often or must be searchable as soon as they are indexed.
  </details>

- **[Memory Consolidation Flattens the Temporal Shape of User Facts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.36457)**  `arXiv:2609.36457`  `cs.CL`  
  _Sugam Panthi, Muhaiminul Yeamin, Siyan Luo, Rabab Abdelfattah_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM agents with long-term memory store conversations as short notes that later models read. A note can turn progressive "I am working at Corvida" into simple-present "The user works at Corvida," dropping the cue that the job may be temporary and that the stored fact may go stale. We call this aspectual flattening and measure it with LAPSE, a benchmark of matched user statements that differ only in temporal form. Three writer models flattened the progressive statement but kept its simple-present match in 210 of 381 pairs, never the reverse. This one-way loss held in all 11 models tested and in the agent memory systems mem0, Graphiti, and Letta. In exploratory tests, reader models judged a fact less likely to still hold when only its stored verb was progressive. In a planned test, the reader GLM-5.2 acted without asking the user on 64% of "works" notes but 40% of "is working" notes. Gemini 3 Flash showed a smaller gap, one reader added later showed the reverse, and others showed none. In a further exploratory test, free-text notes erased this difference and notes that kept the user's words restored it. Agent memory systems should keep the user's wording as evidence for later readers.
  </details>

- **[Verifiable by Construction: How Well Do LLMs Substantiate Their Clinical Claims with Verbatim Quotes?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.15964)**  `arXiv:2609.15964`  `cs.CL`  
  _Jiashuo Zhang, Yuling Chen, Yvonne Commodore-Mensah, Michael Oberst_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) have been widely adopted for clinical question answering (QA). Current systems can attach citations to their answers, but these often point to broad texts, leaving time-pressed clinicians unable to verify them efficiently. An alternative is to ensure that responses are verifiable by construction: providing fine-grained verbatim quotes from reference material that substantiate claims, so users can verify an answer without opening other documents. In this paper, we evaluate the ability of current models to perform this task end-to-end: from providing citations for every factual claim, to producing verbatim quotes, to ensuring that those quotes fully substantiate the claims. To do so, we build a standardized harness over four clinical practice guidelines and evaluate fourteen LLMs on 222 synthetic clinical questions, measuring each of these stages separately. We find that most models can attach verbatim quotes to over 90% of their claims from prompting alone, apart from some lightweight models such as claude-haiku-4.5. Yet these quotes often fail to substantiate every detail of the claims they accompany. For instance, claude-opus-5 produces verbatim quotes for 98.0% of its claims, but fully substantiates only 37.1%. Our work provides insights into the current capability gap of LLMs in building verifiable clinical QA systems, along with artifacts for future research.
  </details>

- **[Improving General Role-Playing Agents via Psychology-Grounded Reasoning and Role-Aware Policy Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.27025)**  `arXiv:2606.27025`  `cs.CL`  
  _Zhenhua Xu, Dongsheng Chen, Jian Li, Yitong Lin, Zhebo Wang, Jiafu Wu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Building general-purpose role-playing agents that faithfully portray any character from a natural-language profile remains challenging. Explicit reasoning can help models deliberate before responding, but generic reasoning does not necessarily preserve character fidelity. We therefore propose Psy-CoT, a psychology-grounded chain-of-thought framework that decomposes pre-response reasoning into three role-specific steps: Interaction Perception, Psychological Empathy, and Logical Construction, so that the model thinks dynamically from the profile rather than merely mimicking surface patterns. While structured reasoning provides a foundation, it alone is insufficient; reinforcement learning is essential to further align the model with character fidelity. However, we observe that under LLM-based reward models, both generic phrases that hack the reward model and genuinely role-specific phrases receive identical gradient signals. This hacking accumulates over training, misleading the model into treating both as equally optimal choices. To address this, we propose Role-Aware Policy Optimization (RAPO), which uses profile-induced entropy differences to weight gradients asymmetrically, amplifying role-specific tokens under positive advantage while attenuating them under negative advantage. Experiments on CoSER, CharacterBench, and CharacterEval demonstrate that Psy-CoT outperforms existing role-playing CoT methods, and RAPO consistently surpasses GRPO across multiple model scales.
  </details>

- **[Found in Conversation: LLMs Teach Themselves to Close the Multi-Turn Gap](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24432)**  `arXiv:2605.24432`  `cs.CL`  
  _Tianlang Chen, Shirley Wu, Jure Leskovec_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Model (LLM) interactions are typically underspecified, with users clarifying all necessary details across multiple conversational turns. Yet recent work shows that LLMs perform far worse in this multi-turn setting than in a single turn with the same information available at once, a phenomenon termed "Lost-in-Conversation." However, bridging this gap effectively remains an open problem. Here we introduce Found in Conversation (FiC), a training framework where a model teaches itself to find and recover its single-turn competence given underspecified multi-turn prompts. We develop View-Asymmetric Self-Distillation, which distills across two views of the same task information (a single-turn view for the teacher and a multi-turn view for the student), transferring strong single-turn behavior into weak multi-turn behavior. This requires no stronger external teacher, which is unavailable as even frontier LLMs exhibit this gap. Across model families (Llama, Qwen, Phi, and OLMo) and sizes (3B-14B), FiC attains 92-100% of single-turn performance, yielding more efficient and helpful multi-turn conversations with single-turn capabilities largely intact.
  </details>

- **[Not All Gap Correction Helps: A Geometric View of External Information in LLM Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.08294)**  `arXiv:2602.08294`  `cs.CL`  
  _Dingzirui Wang, Xuanliang Zhang, Keyan Xu, Qingfu Zhu, Wanxiang Che, Yang Deng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Providing external information beyond the query is a common way to improve large language model (LLM) inference, as seen in in-context learning (ICL), retrieval-augmented generation (RAG), and memory-based methods (Mem). Existing work increasingly suggests that useful external information should compensate for the gap between LLMs and user queries, implicitly assuming that stronger gap-correction can lead to better performance. In this paper, we challenge this assumption and show that more gap-corrective external information can instead harm performance. We analyze this phenomenon in Transformers through the lens of reasoning error, defined as the difference between the predicted answer vector and the ground-truth answer vector. Our analysis reveals that external information can be not only under-corrective but also over-corrective, where excessively strong correction increases reasoning error. We further show that the induced correction vector is jointly determined by attention and the complementarity between the external information and the query, and derive conditions under which information is properly corrective in both direction and magnitude. Experiments on four mainstream LLMs and seven reasoning benchmarks across ICL, RAG, and Mem validate our theory and support a theory-guided information selection method.
  </details>

- **[The Art of Asking: Multilingual Prompt Optimization for Synthetic Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.19806)**  `arXiv:2510.19806`  `cs.CL`  
  _David Mora, Viraat Aryabumi, Wei-Yin Ko, Sara Hooker, Julia Kreutzer, Marzieh Fadaee_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Synthetic data has become a cornerstone for scaling LLMs, yet its benefits for multilingual modeling remain bottlenecked by translation. Translation for data augmentation inherits English-centric framing and style and neglects cultural dimensions, ultimately constraining model generalization. We argue that the overlooked prompt space offers a more powerful lever for improving multilingual performance. We introduce a lightweight framework for prompt-space optimization, where translated prompts are systematically transformed for Naturalness, Cultural Adaptation, and Difficulty Enhancement. Using an off-the-shelf multilingual LLM, we apply these transformations to prompts from 12 languages spanning 7 families. Under identical data conditions, our approach achieves substantial and consistent downstream improvements over the translation-only baseline: +4.7% accuracy on Global-MMLU, +2.4% XCometXL on Flores and +35.3% wins in preferences on mArenaHard. These gains are further corroborated by human preferences on open-ended creative writing. We establish prompt-space optimization as a simple yet powerful paradigm for building multilingual LLMs that are more robust, culturally grounded, and globally capable.
  </details>

- **[Divide and Conquer: A Hybrid Strategy Defeats Multimodal Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2412.16555)**  `arXiv:2412.16555`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yanxu Mao, Peipei Liu, Tiehan Cui, Zhaoteng Yan, Congying Liu, Datao You_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) are widely applied in various fields of society due to their powerful reasoning, understanding, and generation capabilities. However, the security issues associated with these models are becoming increasingly severe. Jailbreaking attacks, as an important method for detecting vulnerabilities in LLMs, have been explored by researchers who attempt to induce these models to generate harmful content through various attack methods. Nevertheless, existing jailbreaking methods face numerous limitations, such as excessive query counts, limited coverage of jailbreak modalities, low attack success rates, and simplistic evaluation methods. To overcome these constraints, this paper proposes a multimodal jailbreaking method: JMLLM. This method integrates multiple strategies to perform comprehensive jailbreak attacks across text, visual, and auditory modalities. Additionally, we contribute a new and comprehensive dataset for multimodal jailbreaking research: TriJail, which includes jailbreak prompts for all three modalities. Experiments on the TriJail dataset and the benchmark dataset AdvBench, conducted on 13 popular LLMs, demonstrate advanced attack success rates and significant reduction in time overhead.
  </details>

- **[Food Critic: An Agentic Visualization System for Auditable Restaurant-Safety Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15843)**  `arXiv:2611.15843`  `cs.AI` `cs.MA`  
  _Arleth Salinas, Ishrat Jahan Eliza, Valerio Pascucci_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Generative AI agents are increasingly being embedded into visualization systems that can search data, invoke tools, summarize evidence, and produce recommendations. However, when agents rely solely on their own knowledge, the visualization problem is not only whether the agent provides a useful answer, but also whether users can inspect how that answer is grounded. We present Food Critic, an agentic visualization system for diners' restaurant decision support that integrates public health inspection records with crowd review data. The system grounds agents under three conditions: a database-only ReAct agent, a retrieval- augmented agent with access to food-safety regulations, and an ontology-guided agent that produces structured hypotheses and typed evidence. Food Critic externalizes the agent's reasoning within these groundings as inspectable visual evidence. We evaluate LLM models with several use cases under each grounding of our agentic system. Our work positions restaurant choice as a concrete case for studying human-agent collaboration, visual provenance of agent reasoning, and the design of visual interfaces for grounded GenAI decision support.
  </details>

- **[Incentive-Responsive Agents: A Principal-Agent Framework, Metric Suite, and Runtime Architecture for Long-Lived Personal AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15515)**  `arXiv:2611.15515`  `cs.AI` `cs.MA`  
  _Jipu Su_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Personal AI agents now persist: they hold accounts, mailboxes, long-term memory, and open-ended task streams, yet the dominant way to improve them remains capability-side: prompt engineering, larger models, longer thinking, and retrieval. We argue that long-lived agents are missing an incentive layer. We formalize an incentive not as text in a prompt but as a runtime contract over the agent's operating point (model tier, test-time compute, memory budget, tool authority, task priority, verification, and compensation) that the agent's policy must causally consume. We develop a principal-agent model with hidden effort, a contractible operating point, and costly verification. We derive (i) a non-consumption invariance principle separating genuine incentives from prompt-level persuasion, (ii) a persistence-as-discount theorem making identity continuity a precondition for sustained effort, (iii) an incentive-capability exchange-rate frontier yielding a per-task routing rule, and (iv) a Goodhart bound for multi-channel verification, plus a three-channel decomposition of mechanism gains (allocation, verification, continuation). A 240-run real-LLM experiment with two models and 60 verifiable tasks shows that prompt-level incentive framing has no detectable effect (p = 0.62), whereas runtime allocation raises success on the hardest task family from 0.60 to 1.00 (p = 0.008) at 4.8x price-weighted cost; the exchange-rate theorem predicts the break-even baseline at 0.21. A 20-seed, 3,000-task simulation shows resource ROI rising from 7.09 to 16.67 and gaming falling from 0.333 to 0.115. Code, simulator, and all results are released; live deployment remains future work.
  </details>

- **[Where Autonomy Belongs: A Separation of Powers for Agentic Program Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15309)**  `arXiv:2611.15309`  `cs.AI` `cs.MA`  
  _Zhengyang Ai, Tong Liu, Jingxian Tang, Xiaobing Du, Tao Xiao, Fengshi Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM-driven program evolution rests on three decisions: govern directs the search from accumulated evidence, propose constructs the next candidate, and judge settles validity and fitness. All three appear in every system, and existing work splits into two lines over who makes each. Controller-centric systems strengthen govern and neglect the autonomy of propose, which caps what evolution can reach. Agent-centric systems let a coding agent take over both govern and propose, at a cognitive and computational cost. Neither line protects the independent authority of judge, so benchmark reliability suffers. These failures share one root: autonomy is misallocated across the three decisions. We address this problem with EvoTrias, a separation-of-powers architecture in which each decision has one owner: Structured Governance for what spans evolution rounds, Open Proposal for construction inside a task, and Protected Judgment behind a private evaluator. These owners exchange typed contracts only, and runtime hooks reinforce the boundaries. This separation also lets EvoTrias run as a plugin that turns an existing coding agent into an evolution system. EvoTrias sets a new state of the art on mathematical, systems, and algorithm-design benchmarks. Three further results show what each owner buys: separating Governance from Proposal cuts token cost by 64%, Open Proposal extends evolution to a 680K-line industrial repository, and Protected Judgment keeps benchmark scores faithful to transfer.
  </details>

- **[DrugCoding: A Scientific Programming Agent for Drug Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13651)**  `arXiv:2611.13651`  `cs.AI` `cs.MA`  
  _Junwei Hu, Zhuohao Lin, Kun Li, Hengnan Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Drug discovery involves multiple interconnected research stages, including molecular design, property prediction, and virtual screening. These processes are characterized by complex workflows, interdependent computational tasks, and diverse specialized tools, placing substantial demands on the coordination of scientific models and computational resources. However, existing large language model (LLM)-based scientific agents still face challenges in autonomous planning of complex tasks, adaptive selection of specialized models, and reliable execution of multi-stage scientific computations, limiting their effectiveness in automating complex drug discovery workflows. To address these challenges, we propose DrugCoding, a scientific programming agent system that integrates scientific task planning, adaptive model routing, multi-agent collaboration, and programmatic tool execution. DrugCoding enables autonomous task planning and dynamic model adaptation for complex research objectives. It can flexibly invoke specialized scientific models, autonomously generate and execute code according to task requirements, and leverage multi-agent collaboration to support efficient execution and reliable validation of complex scientific tasks. To evaluate DrugCoding's performance in scientific tool invocation and multi-stage task execution, we conduct multi-turn tool-calling experiments across diverse language models and scientific tool interfaces. Experimental results demonstrate that DrugCoding achieves an average session-level Pass@1 of 97.35% on the full benchmark, outperforming the strongest baseline, Codex, by 9.24%. On the challenging subset, DrugCoding achieves 97.92%, exceeding the strongest baseline.
  </details>

- **[Four conditions for composing plugin-based agents without a host agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28553)**  `arXiv:2608.28553`  `cs.AI` `cs.MA`  
  _Hanzhang Jia, Liheng Zeng, Hao Cheng, Yi Gao, Bo Ma, Guolong Xu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model agents now run long-horizon, multi-tool, autonomous sessions, and the infrastructure must keep the current assembly of capabilities, the progress of execution, and the basis for recovery after a fault. A single-process host holds this information in its own running state, so its death takes the session record and the assembly with it. This paper gives four conditions under which a plugin-based agent needs no host process, one each on state placement, record carriers, recovery coordination, and provider resolution. The store, writer, and delivery disciplines keep the conditions in force, and each carries formal support from the spatiotemporal-composability calculus and from the proofs in the supplementary materials. An agent that meets them can be constructed with its assembly on a bus of peer processes and its record in an append-only carrier owned by no process, and removing any one part makes the assembly need a host again. The conditions themselves are the contribution, and every implementation that meets them inherits the same advantages. Such an agent keeps its session through the death of any process, wastes no calls after a fault, loses no records, recovers locally, changes its capability set while it runs, and carries its undo semantics across processes. This paper builds one SDK of the conditions, Logos, and measures whether it fulfills their promise against the single-process reference and a configuration over MCP under one fault in each of 200 tasks, realized in 197. The reference lost all 197 faulted sessions, the MCP configuration kept its sessions but issued 1,212 wasted calls, and the SDK kept every session, wasted no call, lost no record, and fulfilled the promise of the conditions in every faulted task, with task performance preserved. The conditions give builders a tested route from single-process hosts to peer processes.
  </details>

- **[CADET: Construction of PCB Routing Instances with Executable Demonstrations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16913)**  `arXiv:2611.16913`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Hyungseok Song, Won-Seok Choi, Seohui Bae, Han-Seul Jeong, Junseok Park, Youngjoon Park, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Printed circuit board (PCB) routing is the stage of physical layout that finds paths connecting the placed pads of each net. Public datasets in this domain are few, and most contain only the final states of routed boards, not demonstrations of the electronic design automation (EDA) engine actions that produce them. To close this gap, we propose CADET (Constructing executAble Demonstrations for Engine-grounded Training), which jointly generates synthetic boards and demonstrations built from EDA engine actions. The resulting RouteDemos provides 100,000 synthetic boards routed with zero design rule violations (DRVs), together with their demonstrations. Their distributions over 17 statistics resemble those of 142 real reference boards. CADET trains a routing policy on these demonstrations with behavior cloning and fine-tunes it with reinforcement learning (RL). On real test boards, the policy raised the rate of complete DRV-free routing from 38.0% to 67.2% over an RL-only baseline. This rate was comparable to that of the open-source autorouter FreeRouting and exceeded those of the other baselines, including GPT-6 Astra. We release the dataset and its generation code as open source atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Benchmarking AI risk behaviors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16318)**  `arXiv:2611.16318`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Luhan Mikaelson, Robert Adragna, Emmett Brosowsky, Hayley Clatterbuck_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We evaluate LLM risk propensities across a range of contexts and task demands. We evaluate models for how risk averse or risk tolerant they are in various conditions, as well as how stable and consistent their behavior is across conditions. We present a toolkit for developing model risk profiles that can be used to predict model behavior and which can be adapted to include new evaluations of risk propensities. We also examine how these properties have changed across model generations and families. We observe that 2026 state-of-the-art models tend to be more stable, more consistent, and more risk neutral than older models. These trends appear to be related; newer models are more stable and consistent because they are more likely to use expected value maximization as a decision procedure.
  </details>

- **[SAGE-QA: Semantic-Aware, Grounded Evaluation for Closed-Context Question Answering on SQuAD 2.0](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16300)**  `arXiv:2611.16300`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Chandra N Sekharan, Vishakan Umapathy, Abhishek Phadke_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Closed-context question answering (QA) requires a system to generate an answer strictly supported by a supplied passage, or to abstain when the passage lacks adequate evidence. SQuAD 2.0 is a widely used benchmark for this setting, scored by Exact Match (EM) i.e., character-level agreement with human reference spans after lexical normalization. For generative large language models (LLMs), however, an EM failure may reflect a valid paraphrase or an alternative passage-supported answer rather than a hallucination. The best prior leaderboard EM is approximately 91%, but evaluating generative models requires separating lexical divergence from factual error. We introduce SAGE-QA (Semantic-Aware, Grounded Evaluation for Question Answering), a multi-tiered framework combining reference-based Semantic Equivalence Scoring (SES) and passage-based Re-Validation Scoring (RVS). On a fixed set of 10,000 SQuAD 2.0 validation examples, we compute EM and decision-matrix F1 from TP, TN, FP, and FN counts for zero-shot closed-weight frontier models and for open-weight models, untuned and fine-tuned. Zero-shot, Claude Opus 4.6 and Gemini 3.1 Pro reach 85.21 and 86.66 EM, outperforming untuned Llama 3 8B (56.88), gpt-oss-20b (66.28), and Llama 3.3 70B (71.39). Parameter-efficient fine-tuning adds 21.19 to 34.06 EM points, raising these models to 90.94, 87.47, and 94.24 EM, respectively. Auditing the frontier models with SAGE-QA and an external LLM judge yields dataset-wide SES scores of 92.81 for Claude Opus 4.6 and 91.67 for Gemini 3.1 Pro, and hybrid RVS scores, which combine benchmark credit with passage-based judge assessments, of 97.96 and 98.02. Fine-tuned open-weight models perform best, with fine-tuned Llama 3.3 70B surpassing the previous best known EM.
  </details>

- **[AutoAssumption: Searching over Assumptions Discovers ML-Systems Algorithms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14844)**  `arXiv:2611.14844`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Igor Sadalski, Minghao Guo, Benjamin Jones, Wojciech Matusik_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Many advances in ML systems came from breaking an assumption that earlier methods took for granted: speculative decoding dropped the assumption that a model needs one forward pass per token. Yet algorithms rarely state their assumptions, so experts and discovery harnesses search over programs, where one edit changes several design choices and gains are difficult to attribute. Searching over the assumptions themselves remains largely unexplored. Writing algorithms as proofs makes their assumptions explicit and turns algorithm design into a search over a small, structured space that an LLM can explore and a proof checker can audit. We formalize 89 algorithms from KV-cache eviction, weight pruning and speculative decoding as proof graphs with explicit assumptions, checked in Lean. Our harness, AutoAssumption, relaxes one assumption of a published method, re-derives and re-proves it, and generates its code. In KV-cache eviction and weight pruning, this search improved on every published algorithm we ran and produced state-of-the-art algorithms. The eviction algorithm has the highest RULER-4K score of the published methods we ran, and the pruning algorithm the lowest perplexity of thirteen on four LLaMA models. At equal budget, the harness scores within a point of OpenEvolve, an open-source implementation of AlphaEvolve, with a lower standard deviation across repeats. Future work will formalize more fields and combine them through the principal assumptions they share.
  </details>

- **[SkillDuet: Co-Evolving Models and Skills through Response Feedback](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12109)**  `arXiv:2611.12109`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Xingguang Ji, Ziqiang Dong, Yubo Zhang, Xi Leng, Ninghua Yan, Yang Yang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  During reinforcement learning, experience can be both internalized in LLM parameters and retained externally as reusable textual skills. Parameter updates alter the model's behavior and the interaction experience from which new skills are extracted. These skills, in turn, guide the model's subsequent interactions and parameter updates. As the model's capabilities evolve, previously beneficial skills may become redundant or detrimental. We present SkillDuet, a framework that coordinates parameter updates and skill evolution through model response feedback. Skills extracted from on-policy trajectories guide parameter updates through OPD at every step. A response-based score, Strength, ranks candidate skills by the magnitude of the shift they induce in the current model's responses, and the highest-scoring skills are periodically consolidated into a persistent global skill set (Global). The updated model and Global jointly guide subsequent interactions, generating experience for further parameter and skill updates. With Qwen3-4B, SkillDuet achieves success rates of 98.57% on ALFWorld and 92.97% on WebShop, exceeding the matched SEED-API baseline by 2.86 and 5.47 percentage points, respectively. Ablations show that response-based skill selection and Global consolidation improve task performance. Further experiments show that the benefits of retained skills depend on both their content and their compatibility with the model. SkillDuet provides a concrete mechanism for model-skill co-evolution and skill-level recursive self-improvement.
  </details>

- **[Multi-Task Self-Evolving Harnesses with the Agent as Its Own Optimizer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38372)**  `arXiv:2609.38372`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Qiankai Xu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A harness is the code around a language-model agent that organizes prompts, calls tools, manages context, and controls execution. As models grow stronger, recent work has begun to let agents improve their own harnesses, a line of work known as self-evolving harnesses. In most existing methods, a separate proposer running on a human-designed harness modifies the solver's harness, and a separate harness is evolved for each benchmark. Real-world tasks come from many domains, so both the evolution and the evaluation of a harness should cover a diverse range of tasks. We propose a framework close to recursive self-improvement: the same frozen model, on the same version of the harness, first solves tasks as the solver and then, as the proposer, reads the complete run records and directly edits the harness that runs it. Each evolution batch draws tasks from five benchmarks in different domains. To measure generalization, training and held-out tasks are strictly separated, and we additionally evaluate on five out-of-distribution benchmarks never used during evolution. We frame the evolution process as deep-learning training with two stages, multi-task pretraining and continual training. Starting from a 49-line seed harness, the harnesses evolved by GLM-5.3 and GPT-5.6 Sol gain about 5 points on the in-distribution benchmarks, matching or exceeding Claude Code and Codex, and the GPT-5.6 Sol harness is on par with Codex on five out-of-distribution benchmarks. Continued evolution on Claw-Eval in the second stage then lifts GPT-5.6 Sol and GLM-5.3-Flash above Codex and Claude Code. We also provide an in-depth analysis of the mechanisms that emerged during evolution, including output truncation, history compaction, completion checks, independent review, and visual inspection. Code and the evolved harnesses are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[SkillEarth: Teaching General-Purpose Agents to Act on Earth via Skill Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15481)**  `arXiv:2611.15481`  `cs.AI` `cs.CV`  
  _Haojun Tang, Wenda Zhao, Xiang Zhou, Qiwei Lin, Lida Huang, Yan Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recently developed remote sensing (RS) agents execute real-world tasks with complex workflows through multi-step tool invocation. These agents, however, typically rely on predefined expertise or parameter updates. They thus require updating expert knowledge or retraining models whenever new tools, tasks, or execution patterns emerge, limiting their continual adaptability in dynamic Earth environments. In this work, we seek to equip general-purpose agents with RS tool-call knowledge that can be automatically extracted from demonstrated trajectories without expert intervention or parameter updates. Specifically, we propose SkillEarth, a self-evolving skill extraction method that externalizes RS tool-call knowledge as reusable, retrievable, and evolvable skill files and refines them through observation-grounded evolution. Unlike existing automatic skill extraction methods that rely on outcome-based reflection to refine skills holistically, SkillEarth grounds skill evolution in real tool-chain execution and combines dual-judge feedback with a grounded observation routing graph for precise component-level refinement. The resulting skills are generalizable, reliable, and transferable: they improve overall RS tool-call performance across three frontier LLMs and four open-source LLMs, are refined through real tool execution feedback, and support cross-model reuse without retraining. Experiments on OpenEarth-Bench and Earth-Bench demonstrate that SkillEarth achieves better tool selection, argument correctness, execution-order consistency, and task completion than existing RS agents and skill extraction methods.
  </details>

- **[LOLPlay: Benchmarking Real-Time Visual Agents in League of Legends](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03685)**  `arXiv:2611.03685`  `cs.AI` `cs.CV`  
  _Zhaojian Yu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Visual agents need to coordinate reasoning and action while their environment evolves during inference. We introduce LOLPlay, a benchmark in native League of Legends with 36 champion-specific tasks spanning resource management, combat, cooperation, and objectives. Agents interact through screenshots and mouse-keyboard actions; automated initialization and 82 executable subgoal checks distinguish partial progress from completion. Across 324 task evaluations of three models at three reasoning-effort settings, GPT-6 Astra achieves the highest task success rate of 25.0% at low and medium effort, and the highest subgoal success rate of 40.3% at medium effort. However, high effort improves neither metric over low effort for any model. For Astra, longer requests leave fewer interaction rounds, suggesting a cost to additional reasoning in real-time control. Trajectories reveal incomplete subgoal composition and actions executing after the observed scene changes, motivating agents that combine visual reasoning with timely action.
  </details>

- **[A Contradiction Judge Counted Reported Teammate Errors as Hallucinations: An Assertion-Aware Re-Score of One Multi-Agent LLM Harness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.21666)**  `arXiv:2606.21666`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.MA`  
  _Carson Rodrigues_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM judges that score hallucination flag any statement that contradicts a reference fact. Agents in multi-agent LLM systems quote and dispute each other's claims, so such a judge can count a correct report of a teammate's error as a hallucination. An earlier version of this study, a preprint (arXiv:2606.21666v1), reported that broadcasting raised a hallucination rate from 0.492 to 0.658 (+34%) and called it contamination; this version withdraws that claim, and the preprint's runs cannot be re-scored. An unpublished revision of that harness reported 0.289 to 0.554 (+0.265, 95% CI [0.225, 0.305]), and its saved outputs are re-scored here. Under an analysis plan fixed before any judge call, three judges from three vendors labeled each of six facts in 1,832 outputs as asserted wrong, attributed to another source, asserted correct or absent. On values that outputs assert as their own, the broadcast effect was -0.0208 (95% CI [-0.0458, +0.0041]) on 10 Full-Broadcast trials: not distinguishable from zero, and equivalent to zero only within a post hoc margin of +/-0.0663 and under the plan's prompt. On these LLM-judge labels, reports of a teammate's wrong value accounted for nearly all of the Haiku travel effect. Scored for mentions, the judges reproduced the effect (+0.1996). With Llama agents, 2 of 40 teammate outputs asserted the wrong destination. One of twelve Claude judge-prompt combinations gave an asserted increase (Sonnet 4.6, one paraphrase: +0.0246, 95% CI [+0.0055, +0.0436]). In 40 new trials per arm, judged by Claude models like the agents, the asserted difference was -0.0505 when both judges agreed and +0.0161 (95% CI [+0.0024, +0.0299]) when either did. Main limit: the labels have no human validation; human labels for 120 outputs are pending. The outputs come from one harness and 13 hand-written scenarios, so the shares do not transfer.
  </details>

- **[Let's Think in Two Steps: Mitigating Agreement Bias in MLLMs with Self-Grounded Verification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.11662)**  `arXiv:2507.11662`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.LG` `cs.MA` `cs.RO`  
  _Moises Andrade, Joonhyuk Cha, Brandon Ho, Vriksha Srihari, Karmesh Yadav, Zsolt Kira_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Verifiers--functions assigning rewards to agent behavior--have been key to AI progress in math, code, and games. However, extending gains to domains without clear-cut success criteria (e.g., computer use) remains a challenge: while humans can recognize desired outcomes, translating this intuition into scalable rules is nontrivial. Multimodal LLMs (MLLMs) offer a promising solution, given their world knowledge, human-preference alignment, and reasoning skills. We evaluate MLLM verifiers across web navigation, computer use, and robotics, spanning 13 models, 28 judge designs, diverse agent trajectories, and distinct downstream applications. We identify a critical limitation: a strong tendency for MLLMs to over-validate agent behavior, a phenomenon we term agreement bias. This bias is pervasive across models, is resilient to test-time scaling, and harms pipelines relying on MLLM judgments (e.g., self-improvement). We discuss principles for design and evaluation of verifiers, and introduce SGV, a method that improves verification by harnessing MLLMs' own capabilities via modulation of (un)conditional generation. First, an MLLM generates broad priors about desired behavior, independent of the data under evaluation. Then, conditioned on generated priors, it reasons over and judges a candidate trajectory. Our methods yield more human-aligned verifiers, with gains of up to 25pp in failure detection, 14pp in accuracy, and benefits extending to downstream applications. In self-improvement and online supervision, our verifiers boost task completion of a GUI specialist in OSWorld, a diffusion policy in robomimic, and a ReAct agent in VisualWebArena--surpassing the previous SOTA by 20pp. Finally, we release an updated version of VisualWebArena, featuring improved evaluators, high-fidelity environment parallelism with >10x speedups, and VWA-Lite, a 1/3-scale subset with comparable evaluation fidelity.
  </details>

- **[When Is Proof-Step Verification Worth Its Cost? Operating Requirements of Certify-or-Decline Verification for Open-Weight Mathematical Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14794)**  `arXiv:2611.14794`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Abhinav Gupta, Ben Slivinski, Yashica Patodia, Michael Saldivar_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Proof-step verification rewrites a solver's answer into typed steps and uses LLM judges to certify or decline. We evaluate this pipeline on open-weight models across GPQA, AIME-2025, and a MATH-500 subset. Certification raises answer-key-match precision by 31 percentage points on the strong GPQA configuration and 46 on AIME, while answering only 35% and 21% of attempts. On near-ceiling MATH-500, gains are at most 5 points at a loss of 13-27 points of coverage. An output-token-matched self-consistency selector obtains 82.9% precision at 35% coverage, compared with the verifier's pooled 87.6% (paired difference: +4.8 points, 95% interval [-8.2, 18.5]). A diagnostic sample reveals a distinct failure mode: 52 of 88 completed problems end in invalid proof construction without ever reaching a judge. Finally, external-LLM acceptance of rendered certified chains is 61.0-67.6%, compared with 87.6% answer-key match. These audits lack human or formal-kernel ground truth. Open-weight auditor pairs show near-zero chance-corrected agreement, which measures consistency rather than accuracy against proof truth. These results motivate separate reporting of execution failures, selected-answer precision, and independently assessed proof validity. We provide per-configuration results and evaluation details in the supplement.
  </details>

- **[Automata from Agent Traces: Failure and Next-Step Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23670)**  `arXiv:2608.23670`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Seonglae Cho, Franklin Cardenoso Fernandez, Umar Mohammed, Zekun Wu, Kleyton Da Costa, Ilham Wicaksono, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM-based agents execute multi-step tasks, but their behavioral structure remains opaque: long unstructured traces resist the safety auditing and runtime monitoring that deployment requires. Existing approaches operate per-trace or success-only, so they miss the cross-run topology that links next-step and failure prediction.
