To recover that shared structure, we collapse an entire trace corpus into a single, compact finite-state machine (FSM) that serves as a structural substrate for the otherwise unpredictable behavior of LLM agents. Across twelve public datasets, the FSMs are compact (7-43 states), replay held-out data at >=0.997 fitness with near-identical topology across splits, and build in milliseconds. This substrate addresses both prediction goals. For next-step prediction, FSM-state context outperforms Agent Workflow Memory on every ground-truth-matched dataset. For failure prediction, per-state behavioral features reach held-out AUROC up to 0.94, and an online monitor ranks failing runs above passing ones from a partial trace, triggering early stopping well before completion. Behavioral topology thus appears shaped more by the deployment harness than by the LLM, providing a model-agnostic structural primitive for safety auditing and runtime monitoring.
  </details>

- **[RewardFlow: Can Action-Level Reward Be Estimated from State-Graph Topology in Agentic RL?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.18859)**  `arXiv:2603.18859`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Xiao Feng, Bo Han, Zhanke Zhou, Jiaqi Fan, Jiangchao Yao, Ka Ho Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In agentic tasks such as household assistance and web shopping, large language model (LLM) agents take multiple actions before receiving a final outcome indicating success or failure. For reinforcement learning (RL), this outcome obscures individual actions' contributions: a shopping trajectory may find the right product but fail to complete the purchase. To address this credit-assignment problem, we introduce RewardFlow, which connects multiple trajectories for the same task through shared states (states grouped by task-relevant equivalence or semantic similarity). The resulting directed state graph links observed trajectory segments. Therefore, an action in a failed trajectory may reach a state from which another trajectory succeeds. It propagates reward signals backward from states where the task succeeds, assigning state potentials that decrease with shortest-path distance to success in the graph. Potential differences then reward actions that reduce this distance, bringing the agent closer to success in the graph. These estimated rewards yield action-level advantages that complement trajectory-level advantages from outcome rewards during policy optimization, without a learned process reward model or manual action-level annotations. Experiments on ALFWorld, WebShop, Sokoban, and search tasks show gains over RL baselines across model scales. With 3B policies, graph construction and reward estimation take $1.47$-$2.39$ seconds per training step on ALFWorld, WebShop, and Sokoban (Fig. 1). Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Actor, Judge, or Teacher? Understanding Decision Models in Long-Horizon Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17167)**  `arXiv:2611.17167`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Haochen Liu, Qianhao Zhu, Zixuan Wang, Dingming Li, Tongbo Chen, Peng Qiu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Decision models answer a question with a typed output, such as a choice among options with a probability for each, so one model can choose an agent's actions, evaluate actions already taken, or score every action to teach a policy with reinforcement learning. Prior work has studied them mainly as actors, judging their usefulness by how well they act. We ask whether a decision model that acts poorly can still teach well. We compare three decision models and three general LLMs as actors and judges on ALFWorld, and use JEV as a teacher on ALFWorld and WebShop. As actors, all six models are pulled away from a subgoal that must come first by an easier one. As judges that see the whole trajectory, the same models credit the steps toward that subgoal, and asking whether each action helped, rather than whether the task will succeed, separates useful from useless actions. As a teacher, JEV raises a Qwen2.5-1.5B policy over GRPO from 72.8% to 82.9% success on ALFWorld and from 56.8% to 71.4% on WebShop. These results suggest that a poor actor can still teach well, because judging an action after the episode ends reveals the consequences that acting must anticipate. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[When Expert Disagreement Hurts: Prestige and Selectivity in LLM Decision Revision](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16694)**  `arXiv:2611.16694`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Yupeng Tang, Mingfeng Lin_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  When language models revise a decision after another agent disagrees, neither reversal counts nor final accuracy shows whether they are fixing mistakes or abandoning correct answers. We introduce a controlled two-pass audit that holds the question, the evidence, and the model's initial answer fixed and varies only the revision context: neutral reflection, anonymous disagreement, or disagreement attributed to a domain expert. For binary decisions the accounting is exact: revision raises accuracy only if it is selective enough, reversing wrong answers more than $a/(1-a)$ times as often as correct ones, where $a$ is initial accuracy (nine times at 90%). Across five models and 1,989 evidence-grounded decisions in medicine, science, and law, no model clears this bar under expert disagreement, and accuracy falls by up to 16.1 points. The expert label, a prestige cue, adds reversals for four of the five models, yet its effect on accuracy ranges from a 5.2-point loss to a 2.4-point gain, so reversal counts are a poor proxy for harm. With identical arguments from both sources, the label still raises reversal for three proprietary models and in a pooled analysis of four newer models. Models also respond to stated source reliability. Revision pipelines should therefore be evaluated with paired, conditional metrics, per model and protocol.
  </details>

- **[Lost in Delegation: Studying the Effectiveness of Task Orchestration in Generative Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16526)**  `arXiv:2611.16526`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Tamara Stojanovska, Elisabeth Kirsten, Leon Swazinna, Muhammad Bilal Zafar_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLMs have quickly evolved from models used in isolation to agentic systems that delegate tasks to external tools and other LLMs to solve complex assignments. This agentic augmentation has led to impressive capability gains. However, when and how delegation improves performance remains poorly understood. For example, it is unclear whether delegation helps when tasks involve post-cutoff information that the orchestrating LLM cannot reliably resolve from its own knowledge. In this work, we use purpose-built datasets with pre- and post-cutoff splits to compare various delegation modes. We find that delegation provides no consistent accuracy improvement over non-delegated setups, with effects varying across models and tasks. When tasks involve knowledge unavailable to the orchestrator, delegation can substantially degrade accuracy, while direct tool use with the same LLM performs much better. Our analysis identifies distorted delegated queries, inefficient tool use, and failures to preserve and use information between agents as recurring failure modes. These findings suggest that effective orchestration requires both well-calibrated delegation decisions and effective exchange of information between agents.
  </details>

- **[MuEdit: An efficient multi-task editing method towards inter-domain knowledge conflicts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14910)**  `arXiv:2611.14910`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Songshi Liang, Ting-En Lin, Zixuan Huang, Yuchuan Wu, Yongbin Li, Zihe Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) require frequent knowledge updates across heterogeneous domains, but existing editing methods fail when applied sequentially to multi-task settings. We trace this collapse to inter-domain null-space misalignment: heterogeneous tasks induce distinct Jacobian geometries, causing their common null space to shrink dramatically and leaving insufficient admissible directions for conflict-free updates. We propose the Conflict Index to quantify this geometric interference, and introduce Mu-Edit, which mitigates multi-task conflicts via (1) conflict-aware ordering to minimize cumulative interference, and (2) dynamic low-rank approximation to expand the null space when ordering alone is insufficient. Experiments across five functional domains and three backbones show that Mu-Edit significantly outperforms existing baselines in multi-task editing performance while preserving general capabilities, and remains effective under incremental task arrival.
  </details>

- **[Rational Use of Time for Social Coordination in Humans and AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14766)**  `arXiv:2611.14766`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Xiulin Yang, Sirisha Gudavalli, Jennifer Hu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  All social interactions are situated in time. Previous studies have shown that humans are able to use implicit temporal cues, such as the duration of a pause, to infer others' mental states. But how do humans use temporal cues for dynamic, fine-grained social coordination? And can AI systems do the same, without ``experiencing'' continuous real-time as humans do? We study these questions using a cooperative card game task called The Mind, which requires players to achieve a shared goal across repeated interactions but forbids explicit communication: the only available signal is the passage of time. We evaluate 150 humans, and two classes of AI models which reflect different ways of operationalizing time in artificial systems: computer-use agents (CUAs), and standard text-prompted LLMs. We find that human behavior is best captured by a Bayesian strategy that infers a partner's belief from their timing---and similarly for frontier models. However, unlike humans, none of the tested models seem to efficiently adapt their behaviors to their partners' over the course of the game. From a cognitive perspective, our findings suggest that humans perform implicit probabilistic reasoning about continuous-time signals in dynamic social settings, and that such reasoning mechanisms may emerge without real-time experience. Practically, our study highlights new challenges for AI systems to coordinate efficiently with other agents by learning to interpret and adapt to their implicit signals in dynamic settings.
  </details>

- **[AGORA: Reliability-Aware Aggregation of LLM Judges on Small Evaluation Sets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05556)**  `arXiv:2611.05556`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Junhyung Ahn_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM-as-a-judge is a common substitute for costly human evaluation, and aggregating several judges is the standard remedy for any single judge's bias. Judge pools mix judges of very different reliability, while the human-labeled sets that validate them hold a few hundred items or fewer. In this regime the per-item mean weights weak judges as heavily as strong ones, and confusion-table models such as Dawid-Skene must estimate a per-judge error table from too few items. We propose AGORA (Aggregation Of Reliability-weighted Assessments), an item-response-theory aggregator that places every item on one latent quality scale and describes each judge by a sensitivity parameter and ordered rating thresholds, so judge reliability is learned from all items jointly and without human labels. Across three benchmarks, AGORA matches or exceeds Dawid-Skene on large evaluation sets, and its advantage widens as the set shrinks toward a few hundred items. It remains accurate as pools grow more uneven, a label-free dispersion statistic indicates in advance when trust weighting helps, and its learned trust weights track per-judge human validity. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Understanding LLM Performance Degradation in Multi-Instance Processing: The Roles of Instance Count and Context Length](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.22608)**  `arXiv:2603.22608`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Jingxuan Chen, Mohammad Taher Pilehvar, Jose Camacho-Collados_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Users often rely on Large Language Models (LLMs) for processing multiple documents or performing analysis over a number of instances. For example, analysing the overall sentiment of a number of movie reviews requires an LLM to process the sentiment of each review individually in order to provide a final aggregated answer. While LLM performance on such individual tasks is generally high, there has been little research on how LLMs perform when dealing with multi-instance inputs. In this paper, we perform a comprehensive evaluation of the multi-instance processing (MIP) ability of LLMs for tasks in which they excel individually. The results show that all LLMs follow a pattern of slight performance degradation for small numbers of instances (approximately 20-100), followed by a performance collapse on larger instance counts. Crucially, our analysis shows that while context length is associated with this degradation, the number of instances has a stronger effect on the final results. This finding suggests that when optimising LLM performance for MIP, attention should be paid to both context length and, in particular, instance count.
  </details>

- **[LiveGeo: Benchmarking Geographic Agents in Continuously Evolving Worlds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17267)**  `arXiv:2611.17267`  `cs.AI`  
  _Pengcheng Zhou, Xuanyu Liu, Yanchen Yin, Tianjie Ju, Bobo Li, Shengqiong Wu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We introduce GeoWorld, a controllable and continuously evolving geographic environment platform for LLM agents, and LiveGeo, a benchmark for evaluating reliable decision-making in real-world geographic environments using GeoWorld. Unlike existing evaluations that largely reason and plan over fixed geographic states, GeoWorld is grounded in real-world geographic structure and allows external world states to evolve along their own temporal processes while preserving verifiable state histories. LiveGeo places geographic tasks within this evolving environment, requiring agents to actively acquire task-relevant information, verify whether the evidence supporting prior decisions remains valid, and revise their plans when necessary. Our results reveal that even frontier LLMs struggle with static tasks, with no model consistently excelling across tasks, while strong static performance does not ensure dynamic reliability. Further analysis identifies a key bottleneck: agents struggle to acquire and translate relevant evidence into valid decisions and maintain their correctness as the world evolves. By releasing LiveGeo together with its GeoWorld platform, we aim to provide the community with a flexible and extensible infrastructure for studying the next generation of agents capable of reliable action in evolving geographic worlds.
  </details>

- **[Ballad: Bandit-Based LLM Routing for Automated Heuristic Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17241)**  `arXiv:2611.17241`  `cs.AI`  
  _Samidha Verma, Ankit Anand, Sayan Ranu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Modern automated heuristic discovery (AHD) systems increasingly rival hand-crafted heuristics by leveraging Large Language Models (LLMs) to iteratively mutate and refine solutions. A natural assumption is that more powerful and more expensive models consistently yield better heuristics. We challenge this empirically: across multiple NP-hard combinatorial optimization problems, we find no consistent advantage for larger, costlier models. This raises a deeper question: given a pool of LLMs spanning frontier and freely hosted or open-weight models, how should one adaptively select which model to invoke at each step of the search? The challenge is non-trivial. The optimal LLM may shift over the course of a run, and because one LLM's output seeds the context for subsequent calls, each model's reward signal is non-stationary and entangled across the heuristic population, thus violating the independence assumptions of classical online decision-making settings. We address this through Ballad (Bandit-based LLM Routing for Automated Heuristic Discovery), an online bandit algorithm that learns to orchestrate a pool of LLMs into an ensemble that matches or exceeds what any soloist LLM achieves alone. This orchestration is powered by an LLM selection policy that favors rare, high-scoring heuristics over stable averages, a DAG-based credit propagation mechanism through the genealogy of the heuristic population, and discounting of stale observations to remain attuned to each model's current utility. Across five NP-hard combinatorial optimization problems and a pool of four models of varying capability and cost, Ballad not only matches the best individual LLM in hindsight but, in several cases, surpasses every individual model while reducing API cost by up to 65.6% relative to always using the strongest model.
  </details>

- **[Skill Adapter: Personalized Modification and Online Routing of General-Purpose Skills](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17007)**  `arXiv:2611.17007`  `cs.AI`  
  _Qijia Zhuang, Yanyi Gou, Wenbin Ma, Yuxiang Ren_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM) agents use external skills to reuse task procedures and experience with tools. General-purpose skills describe shared execution procedures, while specific tool choices, operating practices, and output requirements vary across users. In long-horizon tasks, subgoals, available inputs, and environmental states change as execution progresses. Agents must select currently applicable skills from an expanding skill pool and prepare candidates for subsequent steps.We propose Skill Adapter, which enables personalized modification and online routing of general-purpose skills. The system distills local adjustments from user requirements and execution feedback and stores them as Adapters linked to general-purpose skills. The routing agent selects general-purpose skills and applicable Adapters based on the current subtask, execution progress, and environmental feedback, incrementally maintaining a task-level online skill library that the main LLM reads on demand. The system preloads candidates for subsequent nodes and rechecks their applicability once actual inputs are determined. Reinforcement learning optimizes the two types of decisions using modification gains and routing outcomes, respectively.We evaluate Skill Adapter on ALFWorld, WebShop, and SkillsBench. Compared with SkillRL, it improves task success rates by an average of 11.43 percentage points and reduces mean execution time on all three benchmarks. Skill Adapter retains its performance advantage when the skill pool grows to 1,000 skills.
  </details>

- **[Primer: An AI Framework for Instructor-Centered Educational Simulation Creation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16986)**  `arXiv:2611.16986`  `cs.AI`  
  _Ethan Mollick, Lilach Mollick, Ben Przystanski, LJ Ciccarelli, Natalie Bach, Laura Zarrow_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Educational simulations are a well-established pedagogical technique that gives learners opportunities to apply knowledge, respond to challenges, and reflect on their performance. High-quality simulations, however, are difficult to build, requiring instructor input, scenario design, character development, scaffolding, technical implementation, and repeated revision. This paper presents Primer, an open-source, LLM-agnostic AI-supported system that helps educators create adaptive educational simulations. Educators contribute their knowledge of the subject, lesson, and students, while generative AI produces responsive variations of practice for students. Primer uses a multi-agent system centered around executing educator's instructional intent through the entire learning experience. We hope that by releasing Primer as an open-source system, including its prompts and GitHub repository, we provide a foundation for development and research on educator-in-the-loop AI authoring, multi-agent educational workflows, and simulation-based pedagogy. Further, by allowing the use of various AI models, including local and open source models, we provide the opportunity for implementation under varied governance regimes. Primer points toward a model of educational AI in which instructors can create locally grounded practice opportunities based on their own materials, goals, examples, and knowledge of students.
  </details>

- **[S1-Evo: Experience-Guided Evidence Competition Attacks on Typed System-1 Decision Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16937)**  `arXiv:2611.16937`  `cs.AI`  
  _Kaixiang Wang, Junjie Li, Zhaojiacheng Zhou, Jiong Lou, Jie Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Typed System-1 (S1) decision models are increasingly deployed in LLM agents for routing, moderation, and authorization to cut inference cost, yet untrusted context can still manipulate typed outputs and alter execution branches. Unlike autoregressive System-2 (S2) models, S1 interfaces score dynamic rules and options without token-by-token decoding, mismatching attacks targeting text continuation or explicit override commands. We propose S1-Evo, a self-evolving framework tailored to S1's contract-based option competition and margin feedback. S1-Evo perturbs untrusted fields via contract-aware evidence channels to induce \emph{evidence competition} with original task evidence while preserving fact invariance. Offline on seed tasks, it optimizes candidates via feedback search guided by directional margins and re-verification, distilling verified flips into experience cards. At test time, S1-Evo retrieves relevant cards to construct perturbations for unseen tasks. Across four benchmarks and three S1 models, S1-Evo reaches 71.99\% mean ASR and 57.47\% high-confidence ASR on \texttt{jev-1.14}, and causes up to 24.00\% E2E agent violations.
  </details>

- **[Mind the Accuracy-Approval Gap: How Users Evaluate Sycophantic AI in Everyday Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16722)**  `arXiv:2611.16722`  `cs.AI`  
  _Wajiha Naveed, Brandon Fain_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) often exhibit sycophancy, a tendency to agree with users, flatter or affirm them, and preserve their face, even when independent judgment or critical feedback would be more useful. Prior work has largely evaluated sycophancy through benchmark evaluations, but less is known about how users perceive and respond to sycophantic AI in everyday tasks. We combine benchmark evaluations with two large-scale user studies. We study four personas defined through system prompts: objective (instructed to prioritize accuracy over agreement), soft-sycophantic, hard-sycophantic, and baseline (no persona-specific system prompt). Benchmarks show that even baseline exhibits substantial sycophancy, agreeing with users' stated views 71.7% of the time on NLP Survey, compared with 54.8% for objective and 89.5% for hard-sycophantic. Under conversational pressure on TruthfulQA, accuracy ranged from 81.9% for objective to 59.6% for baseline and 8.7% for hard-sycophantic. Participants were assigned to one of the four personas, and either discussed contested policy topics or collaborated with AI on email writing. Despite its higher accuracy under pressure, objective AI was rated significantly less respectful and satisfying than the other personas, and less trustworthy than baseline. Baseline and sycophantic personas did not differ significantly. Persona did not significantly affect how often participants changed their opinions, although participants who discussed with objective AI had greater odds of reduced confidence. Objective AI prompted greater writing expansion than soft-sycophantic AI, but final writing quality did not differ significantly across personas. These findings highlight a tension between accuracy and user approval: personas with substantially lower accuracy under conversational pressure received more favorable ratings for respect and satisfaction than objective AI.
  </details>

- **[AI has its own style in mathematical proving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16527)**  `arXiv:2611.16527`  `cs.AI`  
  _Xiaokai Li, Ziyu Zhou, Qibin Cheng, Yangcheng Wu, An Zeng, Ying Fan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As large language models increasingly generate mathematical proofs, whether they develop a proving style distinct from that of human mathematicians remains an open question. This question is particularly pressing as mathematicians increasingly voice concerns that proofs generated by large language models are difficult to interpret. Here we ask three large language models to independently reprove all 465 propositions of Euclid's \textit{Elements} and compare their citation choices against the human-proof baseline through Jaccard similarity and complex network analysis. We find that AI-generated proofs differ from human proofs, and this difference is stable and reproducible: mean pairwise Jaccard similarity among AI runs (~0.68) is nearly twice that between AI and human proofs (~0.37), and cross-run classification consistency is also high. By examining citation structure in detail, we identify the concrete signature of this difference: approximately 40% of AI citations point to foundational elements (definitions, common notions, and postulates), compared with only 13\% in human proofs; even among theorem-to-theorem citations, AI tends to cite more distant results. Roughly half of AI's extra citations trace to the upstream dependencies of the human proof. Together, these findings suggest that the AI proving style is a tendency to construct arguments on its own from foundational elements rather than directly citing later, more established results, which we term "building from the ground up". Our findings offer a new perspective on how AI systems organise and invoke mathematical knowledge, with implications for understanding the interpretability of AI-generated proofs.
  </details>

- **[SignRot: LLM Quantization with Massive Outlier-Aware Sign-Adjusted Rotation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16502)**  `arXiv:2611.16502`  `cs.AI`  
  _Jaehun Gim, Jaewoo Kim, Gyuwan Kim, Seo Yeon Park_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  While quantization compresses LLMs and accelerates inference, outlier activations impede efficient low-bit representation. In particular, massive outliers cause substantial performance degradation. Although rotation-based methods that rely on randomized Hadamard transforms for online inference aim to mitigate this, they can produce fragmented distributions with empty bins when a massive outlier dominates a token's activation. This fragmentation severely undermines uniform quantization performance. Moreover, massive outliers concentrate in the down-projection input, where the preceding nonlinearity requires the rotation to be applied at inference time. This constraint prevents training-based approaches such as SpinQuant from learning the online rotation at this position, as a learned dense matrix cannot use the Fast Walsh-Hadamard Transform and would require a full matrix multiplication during inference. To address this, we propose SignRot (Sign-adjusted Rotation), which identifies massive outlier channels and constructs a sign adjustment vector that unifies the signs of the corresponding Hadamard row, collapsing the bimodal distribution into a unimodal profile amenable to quantization. Since the aligned outlier shifts the activation mean away from zero, we pair SignRot with asymmetric activation quantization, whose zero-point absorbs this shift. As a lightweight, drop-in improvement compatible with any Hadamard-based quantization method, SignRot requires only a single calibration forward pass and adds only element-wise sign flips at inference, without an additional matrix multiplication. Applying SignRot to QuaRot achieves a WikiText2 perplexity of 5.88 and an average zero-shot reasoning accuracy of 72.73, surpassing SpinQuant on the LLaMA3-70B model. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[How many LLM-labelled rows does a decision encoder need, and how wrong can the labels be?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16498)**  `arXiv:2611.16498`  `cs.AI`  
  _Mohammed Nayeem Khan, Aliyas Ahmad Misri_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A decision encoder such as Laya reads a question with named options (for example, which department should handle a ticket) and a text, and picks an option. It reads both in one forward pass and returns a probability for every option, without writing any text. We ask how many LLM-written training rows such an encoder needs and how wrong those labels may be. We ran 19 fine-tunes of Laya (ModernBERT-large) on nested subsets of a 2,818-row pool and scored them on 1,065 held-out items from the same generator, so every test item is in-distribution and no out-of-family test was run. Accuracy rises from 69.4% before fine-tuning to 74.3% with 5% of the rows, 84.8% with 50% and 86.9% with all rows. The curve flattens, and a saturating fit with bootstrap intervals cannot pin down its plateau. Seed spread at 20% of the rows (s.d. 1.6 points) exceeds some of the steps between sizes. Giving small subsets as many updates as the full pool gains 1.9 points at 5% and 1.9 points at 20% of the rows (one seed; p=0.14 and 0.13), and at 20% that is below the 2.3-point difference between two ordinary seeds. A single run with 10% symmetric label noise shows no detectable change (-0.2 points, 95% interval -1.7 to +1.4); 20% costs 3.4 points and 40% costs 8.7 points. Confusable-pair noise at 20% costs 7.4 points, and under noise the model moves from over-confident to under-confident. An independent labeller disagrees with the gold labels on 3.5% of held-out items, and about half of its choice disagreements (16 of 33, leaving each item out of the partner map) name the partner option that the confusable-pair noise uses. Laya is +0.9 points ahead of a TF-IDF classifier trained on the same rows at full size (the interval includes zero) and +21.1 points ahead at 5% of the rows. Everything is synthetic, LLM-labelled and from one backbone, one trainer and mostly one seed.
  </details>

- **[Fine-Tuned Small Language Model for Synthetic Clinical Data Generation and Predictive Healthcare Analytics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16460)**  `arXiv:2611.16460`  `cs.AI`  
  _Mihnea Sebastian Burlacu, Ionut Anghel, Tudor Cioara_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The reduced availability of medical datasets represents an important challenge in the development of predictive models for remote healthcare solutions, especially when the available data are imbalanced or difficult to extend through traditional methods. Small Language Models (SLMs) have become a promising alternative to Large Language Models (LLMs) for addressing these limitations due to their lower computational requirements and adaptability to specialized tasks. However, their performance remains highly dependent on the quality and representativeness of the available training data. In this paper, we propose a method for clinical data augmentation based on a fine-tuned Microsoft Phi-2 model that is used to generate synthetic instances, with the purpose of improving deep learning-based model for predicting remotely monitored vital signs. The Phi-2 model was adapted to the medical domain through supervised fine-tuning on the MedQuad dataset, through a Quantized Low-Rank Adaptation (QLoRA)-based method and by applying few-shot prompting techniques. The resulting model was used afterwards in the augmentation of a small-sized dataset, composed of vital sign values and labels associated with pathological and physiological states. The experimental results demonstrate that fine-tuned SLMs improved the performance on medical domain, achieving higher scores for both lexical (ROUGE) and semantic (BERTScore) evaluation metrics compared to the pretrained model. Moreover, the fine-tuned SLM is effective for generating structured synthetic clinical data. When used for data augmentation, the generated dataset improved the performance of a LSTM predictive model, increasing the classification accuracy from 89.0% to 93.7% and showing consistent improvements in precision, recall, and F1-score.
  </details>

- **[FPSRG: Heterogeneous User Simulation First-Person Post-Training and Multidimensional Semantic Response Grids](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16445)**  `arXiv:2611.16445`  `cs.AI`  
  _Ruimin Pei, Haibo Liu, Fang Zhang, Xunhua Guo_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Simulating heterogeneous individuals with large language models is an active research direction, and heterogeneous user simulation requires models with different characteristics. We study profile construction: given dimensions, items, and scoring rules, build model configurations that can be retrieved by multidimensional target scores. We introduce First-Person Semantic Response Grids (FPSRG). Shared post-training first teaches the model to judge and answer as the participant; self-report and behavioral targets then refine dimension-specific control vectors. With the model frozen, we measure joint scores for vector-coefficient combinations, filter inadmissible configurations, and store the results in a searchable catalog. Per-token configuration routing, continuous batching, and isolated caches support parallel measurement of different profiles. Non-thinking five- and ten-dimensional Qwen instances and a thinking-mode six-dimensional instance following full-parameter post-training of Huihui demonstrate the complete workflow. With matched data, layers, and norms, refinement increases mean five-axis composite control span from 30.75 to 40.45. Within the five-dimensional 25-75 target region, the qualified five-level vector catalog covers 92.2% of fixed targets under per-axis error at most 20 and total error at most 60; combining it with the prompt catalog increases coverage to 98.7%. At a fixed coefficient range, three-, five-, and nine-level catalogs achieve 71.2%, 92.2%, and 94.6% coverage, demonstrating the gains from denser measurement. These results describe measured reachable regions under a fixed assessment protocol. Through shared adaptation, a small set of learned directions, and reusable joint measurements, FPSRG constructs heterogeneous individual configurations without retraining a model for every profile.
  </details>

- **[Small Language Models for Inference-Capability Routing: Effects of Model Capacity and Prompt Optimization on Downstream Quality and Efficiency](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16414)**  `arXiv:2611.16414`  `cs.AI`  
  _Sailendra Kolluru, Maheshkumar Duvvarapu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Optional reasoning, retrieval, web search, and tool use can improve large language model responses but incur unnecessary computation when activated indiscriminately. This study investigates whether prompt optimization can compensate for limited router capacity and whether classification gains translate into downstream efficiency. The proposed framework uses prompt-driven small language models to make four independent capability decisions, allowing multiple capabilities to be selected per query. Qwen2.5 routers ranging from 1.5B to 14B parameters are compared across five prompting strategies, followed by an evaluation of capability-specific optimized configurations using a fixed GPT-OSS-120B downstream model on 127 questions. Under minimal prompting, increasing router capacity does not consistently improve classification performance. Prompt optimization narrows the capacity gap for reasoning and calculator routing, whereas RAG and web search retain larger benefits from higher capacity. Similar classification F1 scores do not necessarily imply equivalent downstream behavior: false-positive activations increase unnecessary computation, while missed capabilities can reduce answer quality. The 14B router achieves a mean BERTScore F1 of 0.8408, compared with 0.8418 when all capabilities are enabled, while reducing average downstream token consumption by 68.7\% (8,837 to 2,764 tokens) and median downstream latency by 80.5\% (9.79 to 1.91 seconds). These savings exclude router overhead, which is measured separately in a different inference environment and increases with router capacity. The findings demonstrate that the relationship between router capacity, prompt design, and downstream efficiency is capability-dependent. Consequently, router selection must consider error profiles and routing overhead alongside classification performance.
  </details>

- **[Turning Multi-Agent Communication into Token-Level Credit with On Policy Self Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16206)**  `arXiv:2611.16206`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zixu Wang, Yu Zhao, Xing Zheng, Bingbing Xu, Tian Xueyun, JiaTang Luo, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning improves problem solving in large language model multi-agent systems. Existing approaches overlook communication knowledge or compress it into scalar rewards, leaving coarse credit with little guidance on which decisions each agent should reinforce or revise. OPSD4MAS converts future communication into fine-grained token-level credit through On Policy Self Distillation (OPSD). An external model extracts facts and assesses their trajectory relations for coarse-grained filtering, followed by fine-grained leave-one-out filtering of target-token influence against agent-wise outcome advantages. The fact-conditioned policy supplies token-level credit, combined with GRPO through a linearly annealed joint objective. Our method improves mathematical pass@4 over GRPO by up to 20.00 percentage points. Ablations support complete filtering, while frozen-history injection diagnostics support fact utility. Together, these results support combining agent-level outcome guidance with communication-derived token-level credit. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[DISCERN: Preserve at Write, Resolve at Read for Persistent LLM-Agent Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15729)**  `arXiv:2611.15729`  `cs.AI`  
  _Chirag Ahuja, Vladislav Tanov, Ramcharan Kakarla, Jonathan Taws_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Enterprise agents accumulate memory across dozens of sessions in which facts contradict, get superseded, and blur as similarly named entities collide in embedding space. The field treats this as a retrieval problem; we show it is not. On ENT-310, a synthetic enterprise sales-agent memory benchmark of 310 queries (210 answerable, 100 unanswerable), a vanilla Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) reader over a memory store already matches strong memory systems on the recall pillars. Retention and currency are near-saturated, roughly 0.93 to 0.98 for every competent system, so recall is essentially solved. What remains unsolved is resolution: which entity a query refers to, which time-state is current, and whether to commit an answer at all or decline when evidence is insufficient. We propose DISCERN (DISambiguation and Cross-session Episodic memoRy Navigation), an agent-memory framework built on a single rule: preserve at write, resolve at read. At write time DISCERN builds a strictly additive bi-temporal provenance graph that never merges confusable entities and never drops superseded values. At read time a zero-LLM tool surface drives an agentic loop that navigates this graph and commits exactly one of four gated terminal actions. A four-judge LLM-as-a-Judge council scores two axes: answer correctness on answerable questions, and the answer-versus-abstain decision as a classification problem. On ENT-310, DISCERN is the best-calibrated system on abstention (F1 0.924 versus at most 0.802 across eight baselines) and leads macro answer correctness under equal pillar weighting (0.861 versus 0.806 for the strongest baseline), a lead driven by entity resolution (0.800, where no baseline exceeds 0.40). Applied with zero tuning to the full LoCoMo corpus, the same pipeline again leads macro answer correctness (0.663 versus 0.638) and stays among the best-calibrated on false-premise abstention.
  </details>

- **[MemCredit: From Relevance to Utility for Decision-Time Memory in Long-Horizon Web Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15600)**  `arXiv:2611.15600`  `cs.AI`  
  _Quanjiang Guo, Sijie Wang, Qianyang Zhu, Ben Zhang, Jinchuan Zhang, Ke Yan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM) agents increasingly operate over long Web interaction histories, making memory management critical for reliable and efficient decision making. Existing methods primarily optimize storage, retrieval, or compression, but the memories most semantically relevant to the current state are not necessarily those that most improve the agent's action. We introduce MemCredit, a decision-centric memory framework that shifts memory selection from semantic relevance to decision utility. MemCredit estimates the state-, reference-context-, and policy-conditional marginal contribution of individual memories through controlled counterfactual interventions, and distills these intervention-derived credits into a lightweight utility estimator. At inference time, semantic relevance is used only for high-recall candidate generation, while predicted utility arbitrates which memories are retained under a fixed token budget, without modifying the underlying agent policy or requiring additional environment interaction. We evaluate MemCredit on ALFWorld and WebShop against representative memory baselines. On ALFWorld, MemCredit achieves 86.57 SR@50 under a 500-token historical-memory budget, compared with 65.67% for BM25 retrieval. Counterfactual analysis further shows that memory utility is sparse, strongly state dependent, and poorly captured by semantic relevance. These results suggest that decision utility provides a more effective principle than relevance alone for long-horizon agent memory management.
  </details>

- **[Can MLLMs Reason over Surround-View Driving Videos? A Multi-View Autonomous Driving QA Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15225)**  `arXiv:2611.15225`  `cs.AI`  
  _Shunming Liu, Jianyi Peng, Cheng Li, Lu Hou, Pei Huang, Yajing Sun, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Surround-view video understanding in autonomous driving is a crucial capability for Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) to achieve reliable driving reasoning. Real-world driving often requires information beyond the front-view camera. Existing MLLMs primarily focus on front-view information, while their spatio-temporal understanding and reasoning abilities remain unknown. To address this gap, we propose MVDriveBench, a high-information-density video QA benchmark for autonomous driving, characterized by synchronized surround-view inputs, spatio-temporal understanding, and reasoning-chain-oriented evaluation. We design 12 categories of QA tasks that cover the complete reasoning pipeline from low-level visual perception to high-level driving decision-making, enabling a systematic evaluation of MLLMs surround-view spatio-temporal understanding and reasoning capabilities in complex driving environments. We conduct a comprehensive evaluation of 23 open-source, closed-source, and expert MLLMs on MVDriveBench. The results show that current MLLMs still exhibit insufficient surround-view spatio-temporal reasoning ability. Meanwhile, closed-loop evaluations of specialist models in AlpaSim reveal a correlation between surround-view spatio-temporal reasoning capabilities and closed-loop driving performance, highlighting the importance of comprehensive multi-view understanding for reliable vehicle control.
  </details>

- **[guardscope: Measuring Which Actions Runtime Controls Check in LLM Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15223)**  `arXiv:2611.15223`  `cs.AI`  
  _Kentaroh Toyoda, Alex Leung, Bhuvaneswari U_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large-language model (LLM) agents execute actions on external systems through tool calls, and enterprises deploy runtime controls, such as agent gateways, to check this behavior in real time. However, a control can only evaluate the actions that cross the point where it is placed, and an agent can act through more paths than the tool-call channel; no existing work measures this coverage against kernel-level ground truth. In this paper, we present guardscope, a framework that makes this coverage measurable. guardscope works in two stages: a static stage that predicts which of an agent's 15 action paths a control can check from its source, and an empirical stage that runs a fixed workload on a deployment that realizes one of eight topology designs and classifies every executed action as checked, bypassed, or prevented by reconciling the control's decision records against kernel-level ground truth. guardscope makes a control's coverage claim verifiable: a runtime control developer can publish which paths its product inspects under a kernel-verified protocol. We conducted experiments with two agent harnesses and real runtime controls, including Microsoft's agent governance toolkit and agentgateway. guardscope is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[HyperSkill: Training-Free Omnimodal GRPO via Hypergraph-Indexed Skill-Library Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15178)**  `arXiv:2611.15178`  `cs.AI`  
  _Haoran Luo, Shangkai Lin, Jinyang Wu, Xinliang Zhou, Luu Anh Tuan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning sharpens LLM reasoning, but parameter updates are expensive, forgetful, and opaque; training-free libraries swap gradients for a textual store but still use trajectory-level, correlation-based admission and flat structure. To address both, we propose HyperSkill, a Training-Free Omnimodal GRPO framework that keeps the backbone frozen and replaces gradient updates with auditable edits to a Hypergraph-Indexed Skill-Library, where skills are advantage-bearing nodes and compositions are hyperedges. It has three components: training-time distillation admits counterfactually checked skills and learns a protocol skill from failures; gated hypergraph retrieval injects advantage-ranked skills where a per-dataset gate admits them; and an execution protocol makes the frozen backbone commit to an answer. On 11 text, vision-language, and omnimodal benchmarks, HyperSkill is best or tied on every dataset and beats the best baseline by 13.0 average F1 points while keeping the backbone frozen. Our software and data are publicly available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Same Task, Different Statement: When and How Reformulation Helps LLM Problem Solving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14997)**  `arXiv:2611.14997`  `cs.AI`  
  _Wentao Zhang, Xiaoyu Zhang, Chengwei Liu, Yutao Hu, Tao Zhang, Junsheng Wu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  When people face a hard problem, they often restate it in a clearer way before solving it. We study whether the same habit helps large language models by rewriting the statement of a problem. A legal rewrite must keep the meaning, the constraints, and the correct answer unchanged. We test three kinds of rewrites. Decomposition makes hidden subgoals explicit. Typed Reformulation casts the task into a more suitable solving frame. Concept Reduction removes surface detail and exposes the task structure. We evaluate them on eight math, logic, and code benchmarks with eight models, all under the same chain-of-thought solving prompt. The effect is large but depends on the problem type. An oracle that picks the best rewrite after the fact raises accuracy by +27.6% on average. Yet no single rewrite helps every problem, and a mismatched rewrite can hurt. Still, the effect follows clear patterns. Each kind of rewrite fits certain problem types. A simple fixed rule learned from one set of problems transfers part of the gain to new problems. It gains +11.0% on the problems it chooses to rewrite, while blind rewriting loses 5.6% on a similar share of problems. Rewriting is therefore not a universal way to solve problems. The question is when and how to rewrite.
  </details>

- **[Early Signals, Strong Decisions: Prefix-Guided Sampling for Parallel Test-Time Scaling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14965)**  `arXiv:2611.14965`  `cs.AI`  
  _Jie Ren, Jonathan Rosenfeld, Neil Thompson_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Parallel test-time scaling (TTS) has been shown to enhance reasoning in large language models by generating and aggregating multiple independent reasoning paths. Internal confidence scores can further improve this process by identifying paths of varying quality. However, existing methods remain computationally inefficient, as low-quality paths are fully expanded before being evaluated. In this work, we aim to improve the use of internal confidence signals for better model performance and computational efficiency. We begin by offering a new perspective on the role of confidence in aggregation, showing that it is most impactful for questions near the decision boundary. Notably, when amplified by multi-sample aggregation, even small confidence signals derived from short prefixes can meaningfully influence outcomes in these boundary cases. Building on this insight, we introduce Prefix-Guided Sampling (PreG), a method that reallocates computation by first generating short prefixes, ranking them using internal confidence scores, and then completing only the most promising candidates. This strategy reduces token usage while maintaining gains in path quality. We provide theoretical analysis demonstrating that, under a fixed compute budget, PreG strengthens the effective signal by widening the decision boundary margin. Empirically, our method consistently outperforms standard parallel TTS approaches across benchmarks, while significantly improving token efficiency and reducing overall computation.
  </details>

- **[RSI-PDEbench: A Benchmark for Self-Improving Agents in PDE Solving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14949)**  `arXiv:2611.14949`  `cs.AI`  
  _Binghang Lu, Lang Cao, Changhong Mou, Yige Sun, Cindy Xiangrui Kong, Zheyuan Deng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Identifying and solving partial differential equations (PDEs) from sampled solution data often requires substantial expert effort in equation discovery, coefficient estimation, and numerical solver configuration, especially under noise and distribution shifts. Can large language model (LLM) agents automate this workflow accurately and efficiently? To investigate this question, we introduce \bench{}, a benchmark of 1,001 cases spanning 42 equation templates, including both classical and fractional PDEs. It provides three evaluation settings: Full evaluates performance on noise-free solution data; Noisy tests robustness to Gaussian noise with an RMS equal to 50\% of the clean-signal RMS; and Hard-OOD tests challenging out-of-distribution generalization to unseen equation templates and shifted coefficient ranges. We further introduce RSI-PDE, a recursively self-improving agent harness that shortlists candidate equations by residual loss, uses relative $L^2$ feedback to refine coefficients and numerical solvers, and applies a validation gate to policy and memory updates. We compare RSI-PDE with single-run baselines and native agent harnesses (Claude Code or Codex) across four frontier models. Across the three settings, RSI-PDE improves the pass rate averaged across the four models by 106.5--181.9\% relative to single-run baselines and by 3.0--19.6\% relative to native harnesses, while reducing total recorded model calls by 46.9\% across the 12 model--setting pairs. These results highlight the potential of validated recursive self-improvement in LLM agents to advance scientific computing.
  </details>

- **[Edge-Native Multimodal RAG: Hardware-Aware Quantization and Architectural Optimization for Consumer GPUs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14924)**  `arXiv:2611.14924`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zahraa Selim, Menna Hamed, Wesam Ahmed, Sohyla Said, Sara Basheer, Rami Zewail_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Deployment of retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) for Edge-AI is constrained by memory and bandwidth limitations. A standard 7B parameter model consumes 14 GB Video Random Access Memory (VRAM) in half-precision (FP16), precluding co-location of vector indices and Key-Value (KV) caches. This paper presents an engineering integration and empirical evaluation of a multimodal RAG framework optimized for consumer-grade Turing hardware (e.g., NVIDIA Tesla T4). Our system-level analysis identifies a critical trade-off: while generative pre-trained transformer quantization (GPTQ) offers superior throughput (51.6 tok/s), it exhibits significant context instability at 2048+ tokens on Turing architectures. Conversely, NormalFloat 4-bit (NF4) maintains robust long-context performance with viable latency (81 ms/tok). By coupling NF4 with a decoupled Florence-2 vision integration, we achieve visual reasoning with only 430 MB VRAM overhead. Furthermore, empirical observations of prompt-induced search space pruning show that structured prompting reduces latency by 47% without altering computational intensity (1.88 GFLOPs/token). The integrated architecture maintains an 8.37 GB footprint, establishing a viable RAG pipeline for 12-16 GB GPUs. Finally, structured few-shot prompting effectively compensates for quantization-induced reasoning loss, restoring mathematical accuracy from 35.0% to 92.0% across 200 trials.
  </details>

- **[When Should Semantic Priors Enter Partially-Identified Causal Discovery?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14736)**  `arXiv:2611.14736`  `cs.AI`  
  _Muhammad Ahmed Mohsin, Saad Bin Ather, Valentin Six, Lancelot Da Costa, Tommaso Salvatori, Bernhard Scholkopf_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Observational causal discovery often leaves causal di- rections unresolved or unstable. Large language mod- els (LLMs) can provide semantic information to resolve these directions, but incorrect proposals can exclude the true causal graph and invalidate downstream inference. We study three separate decisions: where to allocate semantic queries, which proposals to admit, and which causal graphs to retain. Our framework distinguishes population non-identifiability from finite-sample insta- bility, evaluates semantic orientation against statistical and random controls, and examines calibrated admis- sion and error-tolerant DAG retention. Experiments across benchmark networks show that semantic infor- mation improves recovery over coin-flip orientations, but targeting unstable edges has no established ad- vantage over matched-random allocation. The tested admission certificate cannot support nontrivial admis- sion with the available calibration tasks. Moreover, downstream retention depends on reference adequacy: learned references contain the true DAG in only three of 80 planned tasks. These findings separate the value of semantic information from the reliability of its alloca- tion, admission, and retention, and identify calibration size and reference adequacy as key limitations.
  </details>

- **[Evaluating AI Agents for Industrial-Level Chip Floorplanning: A Framework and Empirical Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12048)**  `arXiv:2611.12048`  `cs.AI`  
  _Nova-Silicon Team_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Floorplanning is an early step in turning a logical circuit into a physical chip: it positions large circuit modules and constrains the space available for subsequent logic placement and wiring. These decisions affect congestion and timing, but their physical consequences emerge through downstream implementation. For AI agents, the task requires translating engineering constraints into spatial decisions whose quality cannot be judged by successful tool execution alone. We present an evaluation framework that connects submitted layouts to a prescribed downstream implementation flow, checks geometric validity, measures physical quality, and reports resource use. On one industrial design whose 99 macros occupy approximately 71\% of a fixed L-shaped layout region, we compare our specialized system Nova-SI with general-purpose engineering agents and agents restricted to file access, obtaining complete physical measurements for 39 layout outputs. Under our 0--100 composite quality score, Nova-SI achieves a mean of 57.97 without online feedback from the prescribed power-grid and placement evaluation, compared with 31.17 for the strongest measured general-purpose configuration, GPT-6 Astra. The difference is supported by raw congestion and timing measurements and persists across scoring-sensitivity checks. Layout-level analysis further shows that similar global utilization can conceal substantially different local congestion. Together, the protocol and case study assess agents through the physical consequences of their delivered layouts and provide evidence about system performance on this industrial instance.
  </details>

- **[AutoQS: Automated Quantization Strategy Design with Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11975)**  `arXiv:2611.11975`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zhen Liu, Zhiqin Yang, Yonggang Zhang, Jianyi Liu, Jinjun Wang, Jingwen Fu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Quantization reduces LLM deployment costs, but effective strategies must balance model quality and storage. LLM-driven search can automate strategy design through repeated proposal and evaluation. This process must identify useful strategies within a limited evaluation budget. The key challenge is that \textit{actual quantization is expensive, while inexpensive proxies may not reflect quantized-model performance.} To address this challenge, we present AutoQS, an \textbf{Auto}mated \textbf{Q}uantization \textbf{S}trategy Design with LLMs. AutoQS keeps weight precision fixed and searches group-size allocations across layers and operators under deployment constraints. Evaluation history guides coordinated LLM proposals, while deterministic validation ensures compatible allocations. An inexpensive quality proxy evaluates candidates, and an candidate repository retains alternative allocations to inform subsequent proposals. Periodic actual quantization complements these proxy evaluations with measured quality and checkpoint storage, providing feedback for further search within the evaluation budget. Experiments on Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct show that AutoQS improves seven-task average accuracy over uniform-group GPTQ and AWQ by 0.70 and 1.12 percentage points, respectively, at four-bit weight precision.
  </details>

- **[Don't Blame The Preferences: Examining The Emergence of Sycophancy in LLM Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10045)**  `arXiv:2611.10045`  `cs.AI`  
  _Chao Peter Yang, Catherine Zehr, Pranav Bijith, Brandon Fain_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  AI sycophancy, the tendency of large language models (LLMs) to flatter users and affirm their beliefs, can undermine accuracy and contribute to misinformation, dependence, and overconfidence. Although prior work has benchmarked sycophancy in released models and proposed mitigations, its origins during training remain poorly understood. Preference alignment methods such as RLHF and DPO are often blamed, but their effects are rarely compared with earlier training stages. We investigate sycophancy across the training pipelines of three model families and three benchmarks. We find that sycophancy is largely established by the end of supervised fine-tuning (SFT), rather than introduced by preference alignment. Across all nine model benchmark combinations, SFT checkpoints attain at least 92% of preference aligned models' sycophancy scores and at least 85% of final models' scores; on average, they are about as sycophantic as final models. Preference alignment increases sycophancy in only four of nine cases, by an average of 4.2 percentage points when it does. To probe the underlying causes, we isolate model representations associated with sycophancy using mechanistic interpretability and measure their activation on training datasets from each stage. SFT datasets show roughly the same average activation as other training data but contribute substantially more examples, and the highest-scoring individual datasets all come from SFT. Our findings suggest that addressing sycophancy requires interventions throughout the training pipeline, particularly in data selection and augmentation for pre-training and SFT.
  </details>

- **[SafeOrchestrate: Runtime Checked Intent-Driven Orchestration for Cyber-Physical Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09364)**  `arXiv:2611.09364`  `cs.AI`  
  _Muhammad Ashfaq, Ahmed R. Sadik, Ewan Warburton, Pyry Kotilainen, Niko Mäkitalo, Tommi Mikkonen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Modern cyber physical systems (CPS) integrate heterogeneous sensors, actuators, controllers, and services, composed at runtime rather than at design time. Orchestrating such systems through natural language (NL) is attractive, but unconstrained large language model (LLM) tool use is risky because actions must respect device capabilities, runtime state, and safety constraints. We present Safe Orchestrate, a reference architecture for runtime checked, intent driven CPS orchestration. Given an NL intent, it derives atomic goals, generates a runtime checkable specification for each, grounds the specifications to device affordances, and verifies every candidate action against runtime state and safety constraints before execution. In a Web of Things (WoT) instantiation, the architecture produces Linear Temporal Logic (LTL) style specifications from the NL intent, grounds them to Thing Description (TD) affordances exposed through the Model Context Protocol (MCP), and checks candidate actions against satisfiability modulo theories (SMT) backed guardrails. We evaluate this instantiation on simulated smart home, greenhouse, and industrial automation tasks against a direct LLM to MCP baseline. Safe Orchestrate maintains 100% required action accuracy on every task and 100% MCP Execution Similarity (MCP ES) on all but one, where MCP ES is 92%. By contrast, the baseline degrades on multi device, event triggered, and ordered tasks, with MCP ES falling to 13%. These results suggest that combining LLM based specification generation with role aware grounding and runtime constraint checking improves reliability for open CPS orchestration without enriched TD annotations or planning domain expertise.
  </details>

- **[Patient-specific rubric-based evaluation of large language models across longitudinal cancer care](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35847)**  `arXiv:2610.35847`  `cs.AI`  
  _Wei-Wei Zhang, Pengkai Wang, Yan Li, Zhijie Sang, Guan-Qun Zhou, Jun-Yan Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) show growing promise in specialty medicine, yet existing evaluations largely focus on isolated clinical encounters rather than assessing the distinct clinical responsibilities along longitudinal care pathways. We introduce NPC-RubricBench, a phase-aware benchmark for evaluating multistage clinical reasoning across the cancer care continuum, using nasopharyngeal carcinoma as a defined disease context. Curated from real-world records of 405 patients at two cancer centers, it comprises 941 evaluation units across six sequential care stages, from work-up through follow-up, and 17,688 patient-specific criteria across five dimensions. Each assessment is restricted to clinical evidence available at the corresponding decision point. Across 30 general-purpose and medical LLMs, we find strong diagnostic scores did not ensure reliable downstream care. Decision Quality ranged from 56.8 to 94.7 in diagnosis on a 0-100 scale, but dropped to 8.9-56.0 in staging and spanned 32.4-94.2 in treatment. Crucially, failure modes differed across phases: explicit decision errors predominated in anatomical staging (48.2%), whereas decision omissions were more frequent during initial work-up (33.4%) and post-treatment follow-up (30.4%). Varying query granularity revealed a trade-off between decision quality and other clinical requirements. Broad open-ended requests reduced decision quality across all 30 models, whereas more detailed, checklist-like prompts improved decision quality but were accompanied by lower evidence faithfulness and safety adherence. These findings highlight the need to assess clinical reliability against the distinct clinical requirements of each phase along the longitudinal care pathway. Finally, NPC-RubricBench informs the design of stage-aware clinical guardrails and offers a reusable blueprint for evaluating LLMs across other cancers and chronic diseases.
  </details>

- **[CauProphet: Teaching Agents to Develop Causal Cognition like Humans](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32477)**  `arXiv:2610.32477`  `cs.AI`  
  _Kaiwen Wu, Chaochao Lu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM) agents increasingly support long-horizon tasks by leveraging external memory and past experience. However, existing agents mainly retrieve records or summaries, lacking the ability to form and refine causal understanding of the world. Inspired by human causal cognition, we introduce CauProphet, an agent that develops and updates causal knowledge within its Causal Cognition. For each task, CauProphet combines relevant knowledge in Causal Cognition with current evidence to construct a Causal Belief, a structured belief capturing causal factors and the effects of possible actions. It uses this belief for decision-making and continuously updates both the belief and Causal Cognition as new observations emerge, enabling causal knowledge that persists beyond individual experiences. Evaluations demonstrate that CauProphet progressively acquires causal knowledge and transfers it to future decision-making. In financial research, CauProphet leverages prior discoveries to improve downstream analyses and outperforms all baselines in mean rank correlation with next-day returns, achieving a 25.4% improvement over a baseline agent. It also improves prediction accuracy from 21.3% to 28.6% in dynamic real-world settings. Further experiments validate its ability to perform reliable causal analysis and action selection across scientific and everyday tasks, demonstrating the potential of causal experience learning for next-generation LLM agents. The project repository is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[MoveBench: A Rubric-Based Benchmark for Evaluating Large Language Models in Sports and Health Coaching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26185)**  `arXiv:2610.26185`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yitong Li, Jiang Bian, Jingling Wang, Pan Ding, Chengying Gu, Xing Huang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Despite rapid advances in large language models (LLMs), their deployment in the sports and health domain remains high-risk due to the absence of domain-specific evaluation benchmarks. Existing general-purpose benchmarks (e.g., MMLU and HELM) primarily assess broad knowledge and reasoning capabilities, but fail to capture critical domain requirements such as exercise safety, physiological appropriateness, personalization, and actionable coaching quality. This gap is particularly concerning in fitness and health applications, where incorrect or unsafe recommendations can lead to physical harm. To address this limitation, we introduce MoveBench, the first comprehensive benchmarking framework specifically designed for evaluating LLMs in sports and health coaching. The proposed benchmark integrates heterogeneous task formats, including multiple-choice questions (MCQs) and open-ended question answering (OpenQA), grounded in real-world user scenarios and paired with structured rubrics emphasizing safety compliance, scientific validity, and training logic. Through large-scale evaluation of 15 diverse LLMs on 809 questions with 5,012 rubric-level criteria, our results reveal substantial performance variability, particularly in safety awareness, structured program design, and personalization capability, highlighting a critical gap between knowing and doing. Our work establishes a foundational evaluation standard for sports and health LLM applications and provides a reproducible framework for advancing safe, reliable, and context-aware AI-driven coaching systems. Open resources are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Representational Simplicity and Circuit Size Dissociate in a Threshold-Dependent Way: A Controlled Test via Adversarial Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35890)**  `arXiv:2609.35890`  `cs.AI`  
  _Adam Elimadi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Sparse-autoencoder decomposability and concentrated feature attribution are increasingly treated as evidence that a model's computation is easier to reverse-engineer. Whether this representational and attributional cleanliness actually predicts a smaller or more tractable causal circuit remains an open question. We test this directly using adversarial training as a controlled instrument: it reliably reshapes internal representations, but this alone does not constitute a test of circuit size. We investigate this question through reverse-engineering complexity: the causal structure required to recover a model's behavior at a fixed level of faithfulness. To our knowledge, this is the first controlled empirical test of whether representational or attributional simplicity translates into causal simplicity at the circuit level. Starting from the same pretrained GPT-2 Small checkpoint, we apply matched standard and adversarial continual training, requiring both conditions to retain competence on indirect object identification and pass independent robustness verification before comparing mechanisms. We then compare the models along three complementary axes: sparse-autoencoder decomposability, SAE feature engagement in task attribution, and the size of faithful circuits recovered from the raw computational graph. The robust model is more SAE-decomposable and engages fewer SAE features in task attribution. Circuit size is regime-dependent: on competence-matched IOI, standard leads or ties below 85% faithfulness, but robust needs substantially fewer edges at high faithfulness (90%, 95%). The high-faithfulness advantage holds for every qualified robust model in a seven-point adversarial-strength sweep and for a second corpus, as do the representational trends.
  </details>

- **[Persona Following Is Not Selective Control: The Neutrality Gap in LLM User Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35036)**  `arXiv:2609.35036`  `cs.AI`  
  _Jiashen Ren, Wenlin Zhang, Bohan Zhang, Xiaopeng Li, Zichuan Fu, Wanyu Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Persona prompting is widely used in LLM user simulation, yet it relies on an untested assumption: specifying a target user attribute should shift target behavior while leaving other fixed attributes unchanged. Across eight black-box and five open-weight models, however, changing a target attribute alone also shifts unspecified non-target choices, such as risk preference shifting color choice. We test whether explicitly specifying non-target attributes restores selective control across 477 held-out synthetic worlds and 200 independent items under unspecified, directional, and neutral states. Assigning a clear non-target direction nearly eliminates cross-attribute shifts. Explicitly declaring no preference, however, still leaves 37.7%-76.3% of held-out worlds and 51%-81% of independent non-target items shifting with the target attribute while preserving target efficacy. We call this disparity the neutrality gap. Because directional tests can be passed simply by following a stated preference, the neutral state reveals selective-control failures they miss: following a stated persona does not imply keeping unrelated choices invariant. In simulated investment decisions, incidental brochure color labels shift estimated target risk treatment effects by 25.5 percentage points, even when models accurately report having no color preference. Counterfactual attention interventions show that unwanted influence enters primarily at query time, when option evaluation reads the target persona directly. Blocking query-time attention cuts non-target drift by 72.1%-87.8%, whereas directional declarations suppress drift primarily by overwriting option values rather than rerouting attention. Because target and non-target choices share query-time access, blocking it weakens target efficacy while value overwrite imposes an artificial direction, leaving task-dependent selective access as a central challenge.
  </details>

- **[GraphSkillAA: Attribution-Guided Skill-Graph Updating with Targeted Validation and Rollback](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20455)**  `arXiv:2609.20455`  `cs.AI`  
  _Ziqiao Shang, Ling-Yue Ge, Lan-Zhe Guo_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  External skills provide domain knowledge and procedures without updating model parameters, but flat text makes their applicability, dependencies, and composition difficult to express. Existing skill graphs organize rules into nodes and relations mainly for retrieval and composition. They give less attention to what should change after a failure, which examples should be retested, and which edits should be reverted. We introduce GraphSkillAA, short for Graph-Skill Abductive Attribution, which connects rule use, error localization, and update validation through the same skill graph. Nodes record applicability, procedures, and exclusion conditions, while edges describe prerequisite and enhancement relations. The student records the nodes and edges used during execution. The teacher contrasts related successful and failed trajectories to identify the fields or relations that need repair, keeping changes as small as possible. Execution lapses or insufficient evidence leave the graph unchanged. A Local Gate retests affected examples, while a Big Gate checks the merged graph on the complete update pool. Rejected edits are rolled back, and model parameters remain frozen throughout. With GPT-5.6-sol, GraphSkillAA reaches 81.5%, 66.7%, and 91.2% on SearchQA, LiveMath, and DocVQA, respectively, and achieves the highest mean performance in every main setting. These results show that graph structure supports skill selection and composition while also providing explicit targets for repair, retesting, and rollback.
  </details>

- **[Anchoring Clinical Events in Time: UID-Preserving Multimodal Reconstruction and Source-Grounded Adjudication](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13062)**  `arXiv:2609.13062`  `cs.AI`  
  _Sayantan Kumar, Nicolas Grimaldi, Jack Cummins, Jeremy C. Weiss_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Interpreting a patient's hospital course depends on when symptoms developed, tests were performed, and treatments began. Discharge summaries often leave this chronology unclear, while structured electronic health record (EHR) tables timestamp only some of these events. We reconstruct clinical timelines by retrieving structured evidence for narrative events and jointly revising their estimated times. Each extracted occurrence carries a persistent unique identifier (UID) linked to its source span, keeping repeated mentions distinct throughout reconstruction. We also introduce GAVEL, a large language model (LLM) judge that checks disagreements between two timelines against the note and structured record and cites evidence for its verdicts. We evaluated six open-weight models on 40 clinician-annotated discharge summaries. Our pipeline recovered more reference events than LLM-Timeline and TKW2 when each comparison used the same backbone. For GLM-5.2, adding structured evidence improved timestamp agreement with essentially unchanged event recovery; ablations showed that links to timestamped source rows contributed to this improvement. Results varied across the other models, with some timing gains accompanied by fewer recovered events. Both GAVEL judges, GLM-5.2 and MiMo-V2.6-Pro-RL, favored multimodal over text-only GLM-5.2 timelines, while neither clearly distinguished the DeepSeek V3.2 variants. Manual review upheld most sampled GAVEL findings. The source links allow reconstructed times to be checked, and GAVEL identifies disagreements for human review.
  </details>

- **[UMER: Unifying Embedding and Ranking via Pair-Aware Discriminative Reasoning for Universal Multimodal Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18504)**  `arXiv:2608.18504`  `cs.AI`  
  _Libiao Chen, Xiyang Liu, Yanheng Wei, Zhenyu Tang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Modern Web information access increasingly involves heterogeneous multimodal content, including images, videos and visually rich documents. Users may seek not only globally similar content but also candidates that satisfy fine-grained attributes, relations or answer evidence under complex instructions. Supporting such access requires both efficient corpus-scale retrieval and fine-grained relevance reasoning. Multimodal large language model (MLLM)-based embedding methods derive representations from MLLM hidden states, and recent work further uses Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning to improve embedding capability by injecting intermediate semantic evidence into the representation. However, existing CoT methods typically use item-wise reasoning over queries and candidates in isolation, providing no explicit evidence to distinguish a positive from a semantically confusable hard negative. Moreover, contrastive embeddings capture global similarity but struggle with meta-tasks requiring answer verification, category judgment or fine-grained reasoning. In this paper, we propose UMER, a Unified Multimodal Embedding and Ranking framework for universal multimodal retrieval. UMER replaces item-wise reflection with PairAware Discriminative Reasoning, which compares query-candidate pairs to identify instruction-relevant matching and discrepancy evidence. UMER jointly learns contrastive embeddings for efficient global matching and discriminative ranking for explicit pairwise relevance judgment within a single MLLM. A complementary mutual distillation strategy further transfers reliable pairwise preferences between the embedding and ranking functions. On MMEB-V2, UMER-R scores 65.5/70.6 with Qwen2-VL-2B/7B, outperforming the strongest included matched-backbone baselines by 1.4/2.0 points. UMER also supports budget-adjustable inference from efficient embedding retrieval to optional pair-aware reranking.
  </details>

- **[Isotropy Cliffs: The Geometric Signature of Decision-Making in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.00828)**  `arXiv:2608.00828`  `cs.AI`  
  _Okan S. Coskun, Florian Rottach, Carsten Eickhoff, William Rudman_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We investigate the geometry of decision-making in Multiple Choice Question Answering (MCQA) through the lens of isotropy. Analyzing five open-weight models across diverse datasets, we identify decision-critical transition layers characterized by a shift in isotropy, coinciding with a major representational change and the emergence of task-relevant clusters. We demonstrate that this synchronized geometric behavior is strongly correlated with downstream accuracy ($r\approx0.84$), displaying its relevance for successful decision-making.
